Q1NumericalFrom your PDF
A share is currently selling at Rs 50. It is expected that a dividend of Rs 2 per share would be paid during the year and the share could be sold at Rs 54 at the end of the year. Calculate the expected return from the share.
Formula in words
Holding period return
R = [D₁ + (P₁ − P₀)] / P₀

In words: Return equals the dividend you receive plus the change in the share's price, all divided by the price you paid.

  • D₁dividend received during the year
  • P₁ − P₀price gain (selling price minus buying price)
  • ÷ P₀divide by what you paid, to turn rupees into a %
Solution
R = [D₁ + (P₁ − P₀)] / P₀
  = [2 + (54 − 50)] / 50
  = 6 / 50 = 0.12

Expected return = 12%

Q2NumericalFrom your PDF
Calculate the expected return and the standard deviation of returns for a stock having the following probability distribution of returns.
Possible return (%)Probability
−250.05
−100.10
00.10
150.15
200.25
300.20
350.15
Formula in words
Expected return
E(R) = Σ pᵢRᵢ

In words: Expected return equals each possible return multiplied by its probability, all added together.

  • Rᵢa possible return
  • pᵢthe chance of that return (all p add to 1)
  • Σadd the products for every outcome
Variance and standard deviation
σ² = Σ pᵢ[Rᵢ − E(R)]², σ = √σ²

In words: Risk is how far each return is from the expected return: square each gap, weight it by its probability, add them up (variance), then take the square root (standard deviation).

  • Rᵢ − E(R)gap between an outcome and the expected return
  • ( )²square it so minus and plus gaps don't cancel
  • × pᵢ, Σweight by probability and add → variance σ²
  • √square root brings risk back to % → σ
Solution
R (%)ppRR − E(R)(R − E)²p(R − E)²
−25.000.05−1.250−41.2501,701.56285.078
−10.000.10−1.000−26.250689.06268.906
0.000.100.000−16.250264.06226.406
15.000.152.250−1.2501.5620.234
20.000.255.0003.75014.0623.516
30.000.206.00013.750189.06237.812
35.000.155.25018.750351.56252.734
Total1.0016.250274.688
E(R) = Σ pR = 16.25%
σ² = Σ p(R − E)² = 274.688
σ = √274.688 = 16.57%

Expected return = 16.25%; standard deviation = 16.57%

Q3NumericalFrom your PDF
A stock costing Rs 120 pays no dividends. The possible prices that the stock might sell for at the end of the year with the respective probabilities are as follows. Calculate (a) the expected return and (b) the standard deviation of returns.
Price (₹)Probability
1150.1
1200.1
1250.2
1300.3
1350.2
1400.1
Formula in words
Return from a possible price
Rᵢ = (P₁ᵢ − P₀) / P₀

In words: For each possible year-end price, the return is the price change divided by today's cost.

  • P₁ᵢone possible year-end price
  • P₀today's cost of the share
  • Rᵢthe return if that price happens
Expected return
E(R) = Σ pᵢRᵢ

In words: Expected return equals each possible return multiplied by its probability, all added together.

  • Rᵢa possible return
  • pᵢthe chance of that return (all p add to 1)
  • Σadd the products for every outcome
Variance and standard deviation
σ² = Σ pᵢ[Rᵢ − E(R)]², σ = √σ²

In words: Risk is how far each return is from the expected return: square each gap, weight it by its probability, add them up (variance), then take the square root (standard deviation).

  • Rᵢ − E(R)gap between an outcome and the expected return
  • ( )²square it so minus and plus gaps don't cancel
  • × pᵢ, Σweight by probability and add → variance σ²
  • √square root brings risk back to % → σ
Solution
Step 1: convert each price into a return, R = (P₁ − P₀)/P₀
  ₹115: (115 − 120) / 120 = −4.17%
  ₹120: (120 − 120) / 120 = 0.00%
  ₹125: (125 − 120) / 120 = 4.17%
  ₹130: (130 − 120) / 120 = 8.33%
  ₹135: (135 − 120) / 120 = 12.50%
  ₹140: (140 − 120) / 120 = 16.67%
R (%)ppRR − E(R)(R − E)²p(R − E)²
−4.170.10−0.417−11.250126.56212.656
0.000.100.000−7.08350.1745.017
4.170.200.833−2.9178.5071.701
8.330.302.5001.2501.5620.469
12.500.202.5005.41729.3405.868
16.670.101.6679.58391.8409.184
Total1.007.08334.896
E(R) = 7.08%
σ² = 34.896  →  σ = 5.91%
Check: expected price = Σ pP₁ = ₹128.50 → (128.50 − 120) / 120 = 7.08%

(a) Expected return = 7.08% (b) Standard deviation = 5.91%

Q4NumericalFrom your PDF
An investor has analysed a share for one-year holding period. The share is currently selling for Rs 43 but pays no dividends and there is a fifty-fifty chance that the share will sell for either Rs 55 or Rs 60 by the year-end. What is the expected return and risk if 250 shares are acquired with 80 percent borrowed funds? Assume the cost of borrowed funds to be 12 percent (ignore commissions and taxes).
Formula in words
Return on own funds with borrowing
R = [N(P₁ − P₀) − Interest] / Own funds

In words: Return on your own money equals the gain on all the shares, minus interest on the loan, divided by only the money you put in yourself.

  • N(P₁ − P₀)rupee gain or loss on all shares bought
  • Interestborrowed amount × borrowing rate, paid in every outcome
  • Own fundstotal cost minus borrowed amount (the base for %)
Shortcut for two equally likely outcomes
E(R) = (R₁ + R₂)/2, σ = |R₁ − R₂| / 2

In words: With a fifty-fifty chance, expected return is the simple average of the two returns, and risk is half the gap between them.

  • (R₁ + R₂)/2average of the two returns
  • |R₁ − R₂|/2half the distance between them = σ
Solution
Total cost      = 250 × 43 = ₹10,750
Borrowed (80%)  = ₹8,600
Own funds (20%) = ₹2,150
Interest (12%)  = 0.12 × 8,600 = ₹1,032

Price ₹55: gain = (55 − 43) × 250 = 3,000; net = 3,000 − 1,032 = 1,968 → 1,968 / 2,150 = 91.53%
Price ₹60: gain = (60 − 43) × 250 = 4,250; net = 4,250 − 1,032 = 3,218 → 3,218 / 2,150 = 149.67%

E(R) = 0.5 × 91.53 + 0.5 × 149.67 = 120.60%
σ   = √[0.5(−29.07)² + 0.5(29.07)²] = 29.07%

For comparison, without borrowing: returns 27.91% and 39.53% → E(R) = 33.72%, σ = 5.81%. Borrowing multiplies both by about 5 (cost ÷ own funds) after interest.

With 80% borrowed funds: expected return = 120.60%, risk (σ) = 29.07%

Q5NumericalFrom your PDF
Monthly return data in percent are presented below for ITC stock and BSE National Index for a 12-month period. Calculate beta of ITC stock.
MonthITC (%)BSE (%)
19.437.41
20.00−5.33
3−4.31−7.35
4−18.92−14.64
5−6.671.58
626.5715.19
720.005.11
82.930.76
95.25−0.97
1021.4510.44
1123.1317.47
1232.8320.15
Formula in words
Beta by regression
β = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY] / [nΣX² − (ΣX)²]

In words: Beta equals n times the sum of XY minus the product of the sums, divided by n times the sum of X-squared minus the square of the sum of X.

  • Xmarket (index) return; always the market
  • Ythe stock's return
  • tophow stock and market move together
  • bottomhow much the market moves on its own
  • β% move in the stock for a 1% move in the market
Alpha and the characteristic line
α = Ȳ − βX̄, Rᵢ = α + βR_m

In words: Alpha is the stock's average return minus beta times the market's average return; the stock's predicted return is alpha plus beta times the market return.

  • Ȳ, X̄average stock return and average market return
  • αstock's return when the market return is zero (starting point)
  • β R_mthe extra return that comes from the market move
Solution
#BSE (X)ITC (Y)XYX²
17.419.4369.876354.9081
2−5.330.00−0.000028.4089
3−7.35−4.3131.678554.0225
4−14.64−18.92276.9888214.3296
51.58−6.67−10.53862.4964
615.1926.57403.5983230.7361
75.1120.00102.200026.1121
80.762.932.22680.5776
9−0.975.25−5.09250.9409
1010.4421.45223.9380108.9936
1117.4723.13404.0811305.2009
1220.1532.83661.5245406.0225
Σ49.82111.692,160.48121,432.7492
n = 12
β = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY] / [nΣX² − (ΣX)²]
  = [12 × 2,160.4812 − 49.82 × 111.69] / [12 × 1,432.7492 − 49.82²]
  = 20,361.3786 / 14,710.9580 = 1.3841
X̄ = 49.82/12 = 4.1517    Ȳ = 111.69/12 = 9.3075
α = Ȳ − βX̄ = 9.3075 − 1.3841 × 4.1517 = 3.5612

β(ITC) = 1.38: ITC is an aggressive stock; it moves about 1.38% for each 1% move in the BSE index.

Q7NumericalFrom your PDF
The returns on securities A and B are given below. (Find the expected return and risk of each, and the covariance and correlation between them.)
ProbabilitySecurity A (%)Security B (%)
0.540
0.423
0.103
Formula in words
Expected return
E(R) = Σ pᵢRᵢ

In words: Expected return equals each possible return multiplied by its probability, all added together.

  • Rᵢa possible return
  • pᵢthe chance of that return (all p add to 1)
  • Σadd the products for every outcome
Variance and standard deviation
σ² = Σ pᵢ[Rᵢ − E(R)]², σ = √σ²

In words: Risk is how far each return is from the expected return: square each gap, weight it by its probability, add them up (variance), then take the square root (standard deviation).

  • Rᵢ − E(R)gap between an outcome and the expected return
  • ( )²square it so minus and plus gaps don't cancel
  • × pᵢ, Σweight by probability and add → variance σ²
  • √square root brings risk back to % → σ
Covariance
Cov(A,B) = Σ pᵢ[R_A − E(R_A)][R_B − E(R_B)]

In words: Covariance multiplies A's gap from its average by B's gap from its average in each state, weights by probability, and adds.

  • R_A − E(R_A)how far A is from its own average
  • R_B − E(R_B)how far B is from its own average
  • product × p, Σsame-sign gaps add (move together); opposite signs subtract (move apart)
Correlation coefficient
r = Cov(A,B) / (σ_A × σ_B)

In words: Correlation is the covariance divided by the two standard deviations, which squeezes it into a scale from −1 to +1.

  • Cov(A,B)direction of co-movement
  • σ_A × σ_Bscales away the size of each security's own risk
  • r+1 perfectly together, 0 unrelated, −1 perfectly opposite
Solution
pRARA − EARBRB − EBp × product
0.5041.200−1.50−0.900
0.402−0.8031.50−0.480
0.100−2.8031.50−0.420
Cov−1.800
E(A) = 0.5(4) + 0.4(2) + 0.1(0) = 2.80%     σ_A = √1.760 = 1.327%
E(B) = 0.5(0) + 0.4(3) + 0.1(3) = 1.50%     σ_B = √2.250 = 1.500%
Cov(A,B) = −1.800
r = Cov / (σ_A σ_B) = −1.800 / (1.327 × 1.500) = −0.905

E(A) = 2.80%, σ_A = 1.33%; E(B) = 1.50%, σ_B = 1.50%; Cov = −1.80; r = −0.90. Strong negative correlation: combining A and B reduces risk considerably.

Q8NumericalFrom your PDF
An investor has a choice of four stocks for investment. Their rates of return and probabilities are given below. (a) Are all these stocks attractive investments? Give reasons. (b) Of those that are attractive, how should the investor choose one to buy?
StockReturn (probability) pairs
A−30% (20%), 0% (40%), 30% (30%), 70% (10%)
B−20% (15%), 0% (35%), 20% (45%), 40% (5%)
C−20% (20%), 10% (40%), 40% (30%), 80% (10%)
D−10% (10%), 0% (25%), 10% (40%), 20% (25%)
Formula in words
Expected return
E(R) = Σ pᵢRᵢ

In words: Expected return equals each possible return multiplied by its probability, all added together.

  • Rᵢa possible return
  • pᵢthe chance of that return (all p add to 1)
  • Σadd the products for every outcome
Variance and standard deviation
σ² = Σ pᵢ[Rᵢ − E(R)]², σ = √σ²

In words: Risk is how far each return is from the expected return: square each gap, weight it by its probability, add them up (variance), then take the square root (standard deviation).

  • Rᵢ − E(R)gap between an outcome and the expected return
  • ( )²square it so minus and plus gaps don't cancel
  • × pᵢ, Σweight by probability and add → variance σ²
  • √square root brings risk back to % → σ
Choosing between stocks
Dominance first; CV = σ / E(R)

In words: If two stocks have the same risk, take the higher return; if the same return, take the lower risk; otherwise compare risk per unit of return (CV).

  • σtotal risk
  • E(R)expected return
  • CVrisk per 1% of return; lower is better
Solution
StockE(R) %Varianceσ %CV = σ/E
A10.00840.0028.982.90
B8.00256.0016.002.00
C20.00840.0028.981.45
D8.0086.009.271.16
A: E = −6 + 0 + 9 + 7 = 10%          B: E = −3 + 0 + 9 + 2 = 8%
C: E = −4 + 4 + 12 + 8 = 20%         D: E = −1 + 0 + 4 + 5 = 8%

Dominance:
A vs C → same σ (28.98%), C gives 20% > 10% → A is dominated
B vs D → same E(R) (8%), D has σ 9.27% < 16% → B is dominated

(a) No. A and B are unattractive because they are dominated (C beats A at equal risk; D beats B at equal return). (b) Choose between C and D by risk preference: C for a risk-taking investor (20% return, σ 28.98%); D for a risk-averse investor (lowest σ and lowest CV, 1.16).

Formula in words
Beta by regression
β = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY] / [nΣX² − (ΣX)²]

In words: Beta equals n times the sum of XY minus the product of the sums, divided by n times the sum of X-squared minus the square of the sum of X.

  • Xmarket (index) return; always the market
  • Ythe stock's return
  • tophow stock and market move together
  • bottomhow much the market moves on its own
  • β% move in the stock for a 1% move in the market
Alpha and the characteristic line
α = Ȳ − βX̄, Rᵢ = α + βR_m

In words: Alpha is the stock's average return minus beta times the market's average return; the stock's predicted return is alpha plus beta times the market return.

  • Ȳ, X̄average stock return and average market return

n = 9
β = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY] / [nΣX² − (ΣX)²]

  = 1.0800 / 1.4400 = 0.7500

Q10CaseFrom your PDF
Assume you are a portfolio manager. Mr Raj approaches you to select two securities for him from his bundle of choices. Everyone in the market is anticipating an investor-friendly budget to be announced soon. Within the given information, choose two securities and give reasons for your selection.
ScripBetaR²VolatilityMonthly return
HH1.040.182.9468.63
NT1.230.364.45532.69
CA0.770.273.36824.23
SChem0.440.253.41231.75
TCO1.370.474.70229.52
Formula in words
Share of risk explained by the market
R² = r²; systematic risk = r² × total variance

In words: R-squared is the correlation squared: the fraction of the stock's total risk that comes from the market.

  • r²systematic (market) share of risk
  • 1 − r²unsystematic (company-specific) share
Stock move from beta
ΔRᵢ = β × ΔR_m

In words: A stock is expected to move beta times as much as the market.

  • ΔR_mchange in the market index (keep the minus sign for a fall)
  • βmultiplier: >1 amplifies, <1 dampens
Solution

Market view: an investor-friendly budget is expected to push the market up, so we want aggressive (β > 1) stocks whose movements are well explained by the market (high R²) so they will actually follow the rise, with good returns.

ScripβTypeR² (market-explained share)ReturnDecision
TCO1.37Aggressive (highest)0.47 (highest)29.52Select
NT1.23Aggressive0.36 (2nd)32.69 (highest)Select
HH1.04Neutral0.18 (lowest)8.63 (lowest)Reject: weak link to market, low return
CA0.77Defensive0.2724.23Reject: will lag a rising market
SChem0.44Defensive0.2531.75Reject: very low β; gains depend on company factors

Select TCO and NT: highest betas with the highest R² values, so they should gain most from a budget-led rally, and both offer high returns. Their higher volatility is acceptable given the bullish outlook.

Q11NumericalFrom your PDF
The risk-free rate of return is 6%, and the expected market return is 14%. (a) Calculate the expected rate of return for each company using CAPM. (b) Identify the company with the highest systematic risk and justify. (c) Company Q is currently expected to earn 16%. Based on CAPM, comment on whether the stock appears undervalued or overvalued.
CompanyBeta
P0.6
Q1.2
R1.6
Formula in words
CAPM required return
E(R) = R_f + β(R_m − R_f)

In words: Required return equals the risk-free rate plus beta times the market's extra return over the risk-free rate.

  • R_freward for waiting (risk-free rate)
  • R_m − R_fmarket risk premium: extra return for bearing market risk
  • β ×scaled up or down for this stock's market risk
Undervalued or overvalued
α = Expected return − Required (CAPM) return

In words: If the stock is expected to earn more than CAPM says it should, it is undervalued; if less, it is overvalued.

  • Expectedwhat the stock is forecast to earn
  • RequiredCAPM return for its beta
  • α > 0undervalued, above the SML → buy
  • α < 0overvalued, below the SML → avoid/sell
Solution
E(R) = R_f + β(R_m − R_f);  market risk premium = 14 − 6 = 8%
P: 6 + 0.6 × 8 = 10.8%
Q: 6 + 1.2 × 8 = 15.6%
R: 6 + 1.6 × 8 = 18.8%

(c) Q expected 16% − CAPM 15.6% = +0.4%

(a) P 10.8%, Q 15.6%, R 18.8%. (b) R has the highest systematic risk (β = 1.6, moves 1.6× the market), so it demands the highest return. (c) Q's expected 16% exceeds its required 15.6%: it lies above the SML and is undervalued → buy.

Q12NumericalFrom your PDF
The monthly returns (%) of the Nifty Index and XYZ Ltd. for four months are given below. (1) Calculate the Pearson correlation coefficient (r). (2) Interpret the result.
MonthNifty (X)XYZ (Y)
May57
June911
July79
August1114
Formula in words
Pearson correlation (sums method)
r = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY] / √{[nΣX² − (ΣX)²][nΣY² − (ΣY)²]}

In words: Correlation equals n times the sum of XY minus the product of the two sums, divided by the square root of the X-bracket times the Y-bracket.

  • nΣXY − ΣXΣYtop: how X and Y move together
  • nΣX² − (ΣX)²X-bracket: spread of X
  • nΣY² − (ΣY)²Y-bracket: spread of Y
  • ΣX² vs (ΣX)²square each then add, vs add then square: not the same
Solution
MonthXYXYX²Y²
May57352549
June9119981121
July79634981
Aug1114154121196
Σ3241351276447
r = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY] / √{[nΣX² − (ΣX)²][nΣY² − (ΣY)²]}
  = [4(351) − 32(41)] / √{[4(276) − 32²][4(447) − 41²]}
  = (1,404 − 1,312) / √(80 × 107)
  = 92 / √8,560 = 92 / 92.52 = 0.994

r = +0.994: a very strong positive relationship. XYZ's returns move almost perfectly with the Nifty; r² ≈ 0.99, so about 99% of XYZ's return variation is explained by the market (largely systematic risk).

Q13CaseFrom your PDF
During economic uncertainty, the market index declines by 12%. Stock M has a beta of 0.6 and Stock N has a beta of 1.7. The investor is risk-averse. Analyse the expected movement of M and N, compare their systematic risk, and identify which stock is more suitable for a risk-averse investor during a bear market.
Formula in words
Stock move from beta
ΔRᵢ = β × ΔR_m

In words: A stock is expected to move beta times as much as the market.

  • ΔR_mchange in the market index (keep the minus sign for a fall)
  • βmultiplier: >1 amplifies, <1 dampens
Solution
Expected change = β × market change
Stock M: 0.6 × (−12%) = −7.2%
Stock N: 1.7 × (−12%) = −20.4%

M (defensive, β < 1) is expected to fall only 7.2%, while N (aggressive, β > 1) falls about 20.4%, nearly three times as much. N has much higher systematic risk. A risk-averse investor should prefer Stock M in a bear market because it cushions the market decline.

Q14NumericalFrom your PDF
Calculate the expected return and the standard deviation of returns for a stock having the following probability distribution of returns.
Possible return (%)Probability
−250.05
−100.15
00.15
120.20
180.20
220.15
300.10
Formula in words
Expected return
E(R) = Σ pᵢRᵢ

In words: Expected return equals each possible return multiplied by its probability, all added together.

  • Rᵢa possible return
  • pᵢthe chance of that return (all p add to 1)
  • Σadd the products for every outcome
Variance and standard deviation
σ² = Σ pᵢ[Rᵢ − E(R)]², σ = √σ²

In words: Risk is how far each return is from the expected return: square each gap, weight it by its probability, add them up (variance), then take the square root (standard deviation).

  • Rᵢ − E(R)gap between an outcome and the expected return
  • ( )²square it so minus and plus gaps don't cancel
  • × pᵢ, Σweight by probability and add → variance σ²
  • √square root brings risk back to % → σ
Solution
R (%)ppRR − E(R)(R − E)²p(R − E)²
−25.000.05−1.250−34.5501,193.70259.685
−10.000.15−1.500−19.550382.20357.330
0.000.150.000−9.55091.20313.680
12.000.202.4002.4506.0021.200
18.000.203.6008.45071.40214.280
22.000.153.30012.450155.00223.250
30.000.103.00020.450418.20241.820
Total1.009.550211.247
E(R) = 9.55%
σ = √211.247 = 14.53%

Expected return = 9.55%; standard deviation = 14.53%

Q15NumericalFrom your PDF
A stock costing Rs 250 pays no dividends. The possible year-end prices and probabilities are given. (a) What is the expected return? (b) What is the standard deviation of returns?
Price (₹)Probability

Formula in words
Return from a possible price
Rᵢ = (P₁ᵢ − P₀) / P₀

In words: For each possible year-end price, the return is the price change divided by today's cost.

  • P₁ᵢone possible year-end price
  • P₀today's cost of the share
  • Rᵢthe return if that price happens
Expected return
E(R) = Σ pᵢRᵢ

In words: Expected return equals each possible return multiplied by its probability, all added together.

  • Rᵢa possible return
  • pᵢthe chance of that return (all p add to 1)
  • Σadd the products for every outcome
Variance and standard deviation
σ² = Σ pᵢ[Rᵢ − E(R)]², σ = √σ²

In words: Risk is how far each return is from the expected return: square each gap, weight it by its probability, add them up (variance), then take the square root (standard deviation).

  • Rᵢ − E(R)gap between an outcome and the expected return
  • ( )²square it so minus and plus gaps don't cancel
  • × pᵢ, Σweight by probability and add → variance σ²
  • √square root brings risk back to % → σ
Solution
Step 1: convert each price into a return, R = (P₁ − P₀)/P₀
  ₹200: (200 − 250) / 250 = −20.00%
  ₹230: (230 − 250) / 250 = −8.00%

Q17NumericalFrom your PDF
A stock costing Rs 50 pays no dividends. The possible year-end prices and probabilities are given. (a) Find the expected return. (b) Find the standard deviation of returns.
End-of-year price (₹)Probability

700.4

Return from a possible price
Rᵢ = (P₁ᵢ − P₀) / P₀

In words: For each possible year-end price, the return is the price change divided by today's cost.

  • P₁ᵢone possible year-end price
  • P₀today's cost of the share
  • Rᵢthe return if that price happens
Expected return
E(R) = Σ pᵢRᵢ

In words: Expected return equals each possible return multiplied by its probability, all added together.

  • Rᵢa possible return
  • pᵢthe chance of that return (all p add to 1)
  • Σadd the products for every outcome
Variance and standard deviation
σ² = Σ pᵢ[Rᵢ − E(R)]², σ = √σ²

In words: Risk is how far each return is from the expected return: square each gap, weight it by its probability, add them up (variance), then take the square root (standard deviation).

  • Rᵢ − E(R)gap between an outcome and the expected return
  • ( )²square it so minus and plus gaps don't cancel

Q18NumericalFrom your PDF
There is a fifty-fifty chance that a stock, currently selling at Rs 60, will sell for Rs 55 or Rs 75 by the year-end. The investor can borrow on 40 percent margin from his bank at 10 percent per annum. (a) What are the investor's expected holding period yield and risk if he buys 100 shares and does not borrow? (b) What would be his expected yield and risk if he buys 200 shares paying 60 percent of the cost with borrowed funds?
Formula in words
Return from a possible price
Rᵢ = (P₁ᵢ − P₀) / P₀

In words: For each possible year-end price, the return is the price change divided by today's cost.

  • P₁ᵢone possible year-end price
  • P₀today's cost of the share
  • Rᵢthe return if that price happens
Shortcut for two equally likely outcomes
E(R) = (R₁ + R₂)/2, σ = |R₁ − R₂| / 2

In words: With a fifty-fifty chance, expected return is the simple average of the two returns, and risk is half the gap between them.

  • (R₁ + R₂)/2average of the two returns
  • |R₁ − R₂|/2half the distance between them = σ
Return on own funds with borrowing
R = [N(P₁ − P₀) − Interest] / Own funds

In words: Return on your own money equals the gain on all the shares, minus interest on the loan, divided by only the money you put in yourself.

  • N(P₁ − P₀)rupee gain or loss on all shares bought
  • Interestborrowed amount × borrowing rate, paid in every outcome
  • Own fundstotal cost minus borrowed amount (the base for %)
Solution
(a) No borrowing (100 shares; % results don't depend on number of shares)
    At ₹55: (55 − 60)/60 = −8.33%     At ₹75: (75 − 60)/60 = 25.00%
    E(R) = (−8.33 + 25)/2 = 8.33%     σ = (25 + 8.33)/2 = 16.67%
    (₹ terms: expected gain = 100 × ₹5 = ₹500; σ = 100 × ₹10 = ₹1,000)

(b) 200 shares, 60% borrowed
    Cost = 200 × 60 = ₹12,000; borrowed = ₹7,200; own = ₹4,800; interest = 10% × 7,200 = ₹720
    At ₹55: (−5 × 200 − 720)/4,800 = −1,720/4,800 = −35.83%
    At ₹75: (15 × 200 − 720)/4,800 = 2,280/4,800 = 47.50%
    E(R) = (−35.83 + 47.50)/2 = 5.83%     σ = (47.50 + 35.83)/2 = 41.67%

(a) Expected yield 8.33%, risk 16.67%. (b) Expected yield 5.83%, risk 41.67%. Borrowing raises risk two-and-a-half times and lowers expected yield, because the 10% interest cost exceeds the stock's 8.33% expected return.

Q19NumericalFrom your PDF
The return on a stock is 16 percent. The risk-free rate is 7 percent. The standard deviation of the stock is 27 percent. (i) For an investor with a risk-aversion index of 3, find the utility value of portfolios with 40%, 60% and 80% in the risky stock. (ii) What is the Sharpe ratio for each? Should it be the same or different? (iii) Find the proportion of the stock with the highest utility and the utility of that combined portfolio.
Formula in words
Mixing a risky asset with a risk-free asset
E(r_c) = R_f + y[E(r) − R_f], σ_c = y σ

In words: The mix earns the risk-free rate plus the share in the risky asset times its extra return; its risk is just that share times the risky asset's risk.

  • yproportion put in the risky asset
  • E(r) − R_fextra return of the risky asset
  • yσonly the risky part carries risk
Utility
U = E(r) − ½ A σ² (use decimals)

In words: Utility equals the expected return minus a risk penalty, where the penalty is half the investor's risk-aversion index times the variance.

  • E(r)expected return (as a decimal)
  • Ahow much the investor dislikes risk
  • ½Aσ²the risk penalty (σ as a decimal, then squared)
Sharpe ratio (Return to Variability)
S = (R_p − R_f) / σ_p

In words: Sharpe ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by total risk (standard deviation).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return (reward for risk)
  • σ_ptotal risk
  • Sexcess return per 1% of total risk; higher is better
Best proportion in the risky asset
y* = [E(r) − R_f] / (A σ²)

In words: The best share in the risky asset is its extra return divided by risk aversion times its variance.

  • E(r) − R_freward for taking risk
  • Aσ²how much the investor is hurt by that risk
Solution
E(r_c) = R_f + y[E(r) − R_f] = 7 + 9y      σ_c = 27y
U = E − ½Aσ² (decimals), A = 3 → U = E − 1.5σ²
Sharpe = (E(r_c) − R_f)/σ_c
yE(rc) %σc %Utility workingU %Sharpe
40%10.6010.800.106 − 1.5 × 0.108² = 0.08858.850.333
60%12.4016.200.124 − 1.5 × 0.162² = 0.08468.460.333
80%14.2021.600.142 − 1.5 × 0.216² = 0.07207.200.333
(iii) y* = [E(r) − R_f] / (Aσ²) = 0.09 / (3 × 0.27²) = 0.09 / 0.2187 = 0.4115
     E = 7 + 0.4115 × 9 = 10.70%   σ = 0.4115 × 27 = 11.11%
     U = 0.1070 − 1.5 × 0.1111² = 0.0885 = 8.85%

(i) U = 8.85%, 8.46%, 7.20%. (ii) Sharpe = 0.333 for all three: every mix lies on the same capital allocation line, because excess return and σ both scale with y. (iii) Optimal proportion ≈ 41.15% in the stock, giving the maximum utility ≈ 8.85%.

Q20NumericalFrom your PDF
As a management trainee you have to analyse the share prices of two companies along with the NSE index. (a) Which one has high systematic risk? (b) Based on return, which company's stock will you select?
PeriodCompany ACompany BNSE
124.0050.38857.07

926.7055.28878.53
1027.7041.38807.23
1125.6048.30877.72
1224.5049.50893.82
Formula in words
Return from a possible price
Rᵢ = (P₁ᵢ − P₀) / P₀

In words: For each possible year-end price, the return is the price change divided by today's cost.

  • P₁ᵢone possible year-end price
  • P₀today's cost of the share
  • Rᵢthe return if that price happens
Beta by regression
β = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY] / [nΣX² − (ΣX)²]

In words: Beta equals n times the sum of XY minus the product of the sums, divided by n times the sum of X-squared minus the square of the sum of X.

  • Xmarket (index) return; always the market
  • Ythe stock's return
  • tophow stock and market move together
  • bottomhow much the market moves on its own
  • β% move in the stock for a 1% move in the market
Solution

Step 1: convert prices into period returns, Rt = (Pt − Pt−1)/Pt−1 × 100 (12 prices → 11 returns).

PeriodRA %RB %RNSE % (X)
1→24.17−2.980.63
2→3−5.48−2.31−0.41
3→40.002.370.28
4→55.805.11−0.88
5→67.00−5.612.14
6→71.8714.19−1.42
7→8−4.59−1.811.42
8→92.691.660.76
9→103.75−25.14−8.12
10→11−7.5816.728.73
11→12−4.302.481.83
Mean0.300.430.45
Step 2: regress each company on NSE, β = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY]/[nΣX² − (ΣX)²], n = 11
ΣX = 4.9764   ΣX² = 156.0678
Company A: ΣY = 3.3259  ΣXY = −96.8452  → β_A = −1,081.8487 / 1,691.9810 = −0.639
Company B: ΣY = 4.6843  ΣXY = 316.5710  → β_B = 3,458.9698 / 1,691.9810 = 2.044

(a) Company B has the high systematic risk (β ≈ 2.04); A's beta is negative (≈ −0.64), moving against the index. (b) On return, B is preferred: average return 0.43% vs 0.30% per period (though B also carries far more market risk).

Q21CaseFrom your PDF
A recession wave swept across the economy; stock prices are at their bottom level. The investor feels prices cannot fall further and wants to buy two securities. A deep discount bond yields 14.5 percent. Analyse and state why we would take certain securities and reject others.
DetailsStock AStock BStock CStock D
Alpha0.10−0.070.080.05
Beta1.1910.800.90−0.88
Average relationship (r)1.00.71.0−0.72
Variance0.430.430.190.36
Formula in words
Share of risk explained by the market
R² = r²; systematic risk = r² × total variance

In words: R-squared is the correlation squared: the fraction of the stock's total risk that comes from the market.

  • r²systematic (market) share of risk
  • 1 − r²unsystematic (company-specific) share
Alpha and the characteristic line
α = Ȳ − βX̄, Rᵢ = α + βR_m

In words: Alpha is the stock's average return minus beta times the market's average return; the stock's predicted return is alpha plus beta times the market return.

  • Ȳ, X̄average stock return and average market return
  • αstock's return when the market return is zero (starting point)
  • β R_mthe extra return that comes from the market move
Solution

Outlook: prices are at the bottom, so a market recovery is expected. We want stocks that rise with the market (positive, preferably high β), have positive α, and whose movement is reliably market-driven (high r² = systematic share).

Stockr² (systematic share)Systematic variance = r² × varUnsystematicAssessment
A1.000.430Positive α (0.10, highest), β 1.191 (aggressive), fully market-driven → select
B0.490.210.22Negative α (−0.07) → reject
C1.000.190Positive α (0.08), β 0.9, lowest variance, fully market-driven → select
D0.520.190.17Negative β (−0.88): would fall as the market recovers → reject (useful only as a hedge)

Buy A and C. A gives the highest alpha and the most upside in a recovery; C adds positive alpha with the lowest risk. Reject B (negative alpha) and D (negative beta). The 14.5% deep discount bond is the safe alternative: the chosen stocks are justified only because a recovery should lift their returns above that safe yield.

Q22NumericalFrom your PDF
Mr Mohan wants to buy Ant Company stock, currently selling at Rs 50 with no dividend. There is equal probability that it will sell at Rs 55 or Rs 70 next year. What is the expected return and risk if 300 shares are bought? Transaction cost is ignored.
Formula in words
Return from a possible price
Rᵢ = (P₁ᵢ − P₀) / P₀

In words: For each possible year-end price, the return is the price change divided by today's cost.

  • P₁ᵢone possible year-end price
  • P₀today's cost of the share
  • Rᵢthe return if that price happens
Shortcut for two equally likely outcomes
E(R) = (R₁ + R₂)/2, σ = |R₁ − R₂| / 2

In words: With a fifty-fifty chance, expected return is the simple average of the two returns, and risk is half the gap between them.

  • (R₁ + R₂)/2average of the two returns
  • |R₁ − R₂|/2half the distance between them = σ
Solution
R₁ = (55 − 50)/50 = 10%     R₂ = (70 − 50)/50 = 40%
E(R) = 0.5(10) + 0.5(40) = 25%
σ = √[0.5(10 − 25)² + 0.5(40 − 25)²] = √225 = 15%

In rupees for 300 shares (investment ₹15,000):
Expected gain = 300 × (0.5 × 5 + 0.5 × 20) = 300 × 12.50 = ₹3,750
σ = 300 × ₹7.50 = ₹2,250

Expected return = 25% (₹3,750 on ₹15,000); risk σ = 15% (₹2,250). Buying 300 shares does not change the percentage return or risk.

Q23NumericalFrom your PDF
An investor wants to choose either X or Y company's stock. Neither pays dividends. X sells for Rs 50 and Y for Rs 100. At year end X may sell at Rs 71 or Rs 67 and Y at Rs 127 or Rs 123 (50% each). Which company's scrip should the investor buy? Justify.
StockCurrent pricePossible end pricesProbability
X₹50₹71 or ₹6750% each
Y₹100₹127 or ₹12350% each
Formula in words
Return from a possible price
Rᵢ = (P₁ᵢ − P₀) / P₀

In words: For each possible year-end price, the return is the price change divided by today's cost.

  • P₁ᵢone possible year-end price
  • P₀today's cost of the share
  • Rᵢthe return if that price happens
Shortcut for two equally likely outcomes
E(R) = (R₁ + R₂)/2, σ = |R₁ − R₂| / 2

In words: With a fifty-fifty chance, expected return is the simple average of the two returns, and risk is half the gap between them.

  • (R₁ + R₂)/2average of the two returns
  • |R₁ − R₂|/2half the distance between them = σ
Choosing between stocks
Dominance first; CV = σ / E(R)

In words: If two stocks have the same risk, take the higher return; if the same return, take the lower risk; otherwise compare risk per unit of return (CV).

  • σtotal risk
  • E(R)expected return
  • CVrisk per 1% of return; lower is better
Solution
X: R = 21/50 = 42%, 17/50 = 34% → E(R) = 38%, σ = (42 − 34)/2 = 4%, CV = 4/38 = 0.105
Y: R = 27/100 = 27%, 23/100 = 23% → E(R) = 25%, σ = (27 − 23)/2 = 2%, CV = 2/25 = 0.080

Buy X. Its worst outcome (34%) is higher than Y's best (27%), so X gives a higher return in every state. Y has a slightly lower risk per unit of return (CV 0.08 vs 0.105), but no rational investor would accept 25% when X guarantees at least 34%.

Theory practice (21)

T12-markPractice · from notes
Define return.

Return is the total gain from an investment over a holding period, comprising current income (dividend/interest) and capital appreciation, expressed as a percentage of the amount invested.

T22-markPractice · from notes
What is expected return?

Expected return is the probability-weighted average of all possible returns from an investment: E(R) = Σ piRi.

T32-markPractice · from notes
Define risk.

Risk is the possibility that the actual return on an investment will differ from its expected return. It is measured by variance or standard deviation of returns.

T42-markPractice · from notes
What is systematic risk?

Systematic (market) risk arises from economy-wide factors such as interest rates, inflation and government policy. It affects all securities and cannot be diversified away; it is measured by beta.

T52-markPractice · from notes
What is unsystematic risk?

Unsystematic risk is unique to a company or industry (e.g., strikes, management failure). It can be reduced or eliminated through diversification.

T62-markPractice · from notes
What is beta?

Beta measures the sensitivity of a security's returns to market returns. β > 1 indicates an aggressive security and β < 1 a defensive security.

T72-markPractice · from notes
What is the characteristic line?

The regression line Ri = α + βRm relating a security's return to the market return; α is the intercept and β the slope.

T82-markPractice · from notes
State the CAPM equation.

E(Ri) = Rf + βi(Rm − Rf): required return equals the risk-free rate plus beta times the market risk premium.

T92-markPractice · from notes
What is the correlation coefficient?

A standardised measure (−1 to +1) of the direction and strength of the linear relationship between two variables: r = Cov(X,Y)/(σXσY).

T102-markPractice · from notes
What is R² (coefficient of determination)?

R² = r², the proportion of a security's return variance explained by market movements, i.e., the systematic portion of total risk.

T112-markPractice · from notes
What is margin trading?

Buying securities partly with borrowed funds. It magnifies both gains and losses on the investor's own capital, and interest must be paid on the borrowed portion.

T122-markPractice · from notes
What is the utility value of a portfolio?

A risk-adjusted score U = E(r) − ½Aσ², where A is the investor's index of risk aversion; the portfolio with the highest U is preferred.

T135-markPractice · from notes
Explain the measurement of return and risk of a single security.
Definition

Return is the gain from an investment; risk is the variability of that return around its expected value.

Measuring return

For one period: R = [D1 + (P1 − P0)]/P0. When several outcomes are possible, expected return E(R) = Σ piRi.

Measuring risk

Variance σ² = Σ pi[Ri − E(R)]² and standard deviation σ = √σ². The larger σ, the wider the range of likely returns.

Key points
  • Probabilities must add to 1.
  • When prices are given, convert to returns using the cost price.
  • σ is in % and is the measure of total risk.
Example

Q22: returns 10% and 40% with equal chance → E(R) = 25%, σ = 15%.

T145-markPractice · from notes
Distinguish between systematic and unsystematic risk with examples.
Meaning

Total risk = systematic risk + unsystematic risk.

Systematic risk
  • Market-wide sources: interest rate changes, inflation, recession, budget, political events.
  • Affects all securities; cannot be diversified away.
  • Measured by beta; it is the only risk rewarded under CAPM.
Unsystematic risk
  • Company/industry sources: labour strike, management failure, loss of a key customer, regulation of one industry.
  • Diversifiable by holding many securities.
  • Measured by residual variance σ²e, or (1 − R²) of total variance.
Example

An RBI rate hike affects all stocks (systematic); a fire at one factory affects only that company (unsystematic).

T155-markPractice · from notes
What is beta? How is it calculated and interpreted?
Definition

Beta is the measure of systematic risk: the sensitivity of a security's return to the market return.

Calculation

Regression: β = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY]/[nΣX² − (ΣX)²] with X = market and Y = security returns; or β = Cov(Ri,Rm)/σ²m. Alpha α = Ȳ − βX̄.

Interpretation
  • β = 1: moves with the market.
  • β > 1: aggressive; amplifies market moves (preferred in bull markets).
  • β < 1: defensive; dampens market moves (preferred in bear markets).
  • β < 0: moves opposite to the market.
Example

ITC β ≈ 1.38: a 10% market rise implies about 13.8% rise in ITC.

T165-markPractice · from notes
Explain CAPM and its use in identifying mispriced securities.
Definition

CAPM states that the required return on a security is E(Ri) = Rf + βi(Rm − Rf).

Explanation

Investors are compensated for time (Rf) and for systematic risk (β times the market risk premium). Unsystematic risk earns no premium because it can be diversified.

Use
  • If the forecast return > CAPM required return, the security is undervalued (lies above the SML): buy.
  • If forecast < required, it is overvalued (below the SML): sell/avoid.
Example

Rf = 6%, Rm = 14%, β = 1.2 → required 15.6%. If expected 16%, the stock is undervalued.

T175-markPractice · from notes
How does buying on margin affect risk and return? Illustrate.
Meaning

Margin trading is buying securities partly with borrowed funds.

Effect
  • Gains and losses fall on a smaller equity base, so % return swings widen (higher σ).
  • Interest is payable in every outcome.
  • Expected return on equity rises only if the asset's return exceeds the borrowing rate.
Illustration (Q18)

Without borrowing: E(R) = 8.33%, σ = 16.67%. With 60% borrowed at 10% (200 shares): E(R) = 5.83%, σ = 41.67%. Risk rose about 2.5 times while expected return fell, because the borrowing cost (10%) exceeded the expected return (8.33%).

T185-markPractice · from notes
Explain covariance and correlation and their role in investment analysis.
Covariance

Cov(A,B) = Σ p(RA − EA)(RB − EB); positive when returns move together, negative when they move in opposite directions.

Correlation

r = Cov/(σAσB), lying between −1 and +1, making the strength comparable.

Role
  • Basis of beta (covariance with market ÷ market variance).
  • Diversification is most effective with low or negative correlation.
  • r² gives the proportion of systematic risk.
Example

Q7: Cov(A,B) = −1.8, r = −0.90: combining A and B reduces portfolio risk substantially.

T19Long answerPractice · from notes
Discuss the concepts of risk and return. Explain how expected return, standard deviation and beta are measured, and how CAPM links risk and return.
Introduction

Investment involves sacrificing current consumption for uncertain future benefits. The two central parameters of every investment decision are return (the reward) and risk (the uncertainty of that reward). A rational investor seeks the highest return for a given risk or the lowest risk for a given return.

Definition

Return is the total gain (income plus capital appreciation) expressed as a percentage of the investment. Risk is the possibility that actual return differs from expected return.

Measurement of return
  • Holding period return: R = [D1 + (P1 − P0)]/P0.
  • Expected return: E(R) = Σ piRi.
Measurement of risk
  • Variance σ² = Σ pi[Ri − E(R)]²; standard deviation σ = √σ² measures total risk.
  • Total risk = systematic risk (β²σ²m) + unsystematic risk (σ²e).
  • Beta: β = Cov(Ri,Rm)/σ²m = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY]/[nΣX² − (ΣX)²]; alpha α = Ȳ − βX̄; characteristic line Ri = α + βRm.
Linking risk and return: CAPM

Since unsystematic risk can be diversified away, only systematic risk is rewarded: E(Ri) = Rf + βi(Rm − Rf). The relationship plotted between β and expected return is the Security Market Line.

Example

A stock with possible returns 10% and 40% (equal chance) has E(R) = 25%, σ = 15%. If its β = 1.2, Rf = 6% and Rm = 14%, CAPM requires 15.6%; the expected 25% exceeds this, so the stock appears undervalued.

Advantages
  • Gives objective, comparable measures of risk and return.
  • CAPM provides a benchmark required return for valuation and performance evaluation.
Limitations
  • Probabilities and future prices are subjective estimates.
  • Beta is estimated from past data and may be unstable (low R² makes forecasts unreliable).
  • CAPM assumptions (borrowing at Rf, homogeneous expectations, efficient markets) are simplifications.
Conclusion

Expected return and standard deviation describe the reward and total risk of a security, beta isolates the market-related risk, and CAPM prices that risk. Together they form the foundation for security selection and portfolio management.

T20Long answerPractice · from notes
Explain the use of regression analysis (characteristic line) to estimate alpha and beta. How are these used in security selection?
Introduction

The single-index model assumes that security returns are linearly related to the return on a market index. Regression analysis estimates this relationship from historical data.

Model

Ri = α + βRm + e, where α = intercept, β = slope, e = random error (unsystematic component).

Estimation
  • β = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY]/[nΣX² − (ΣX)²] (X = market, Y = security).
  • α = Ȳ − βX̄.
  • Correlation r and R² = r² show goodness of fit.
Interpretation
  • β > 1 aggressive; β < 1 defensive; β < 0 counter-cyclical.
  • α > 0: the stock earns a positive return even when the market return is zero.
  • High R²: the market explains most of the variation, so predictions are more reliable.
Example

ITC vs BSE (Q5): β ≈ 1.38, α ≈ 3.56. Line: RITC = 3.56 + 1.38Rm. Sun Co. (Q9): β = 0.75, α = 0.05; if Rm = 2, predicted return = 1.55.

Use in selection
  • Bull market expected (e.g., investor-friendly budget): choose high-β, high-R², positive-α stocks (TCO and NT in Q10).
  • Bear market: choose low-β (defensive) stocks (Stock M in Q13).
  • Reject negative-α stocks; avoid negative-β stocks when a recovery is expected (Q21).
Limitations
  • Past relationships may change.
  • Small samples and outliers distort β (e.g., one extreme month).
Conclusion

The characteristic line converts historical data into usable estimates of systematic risk and stock-specific return, which guide timing and selection decisions.

T21Long answerPractice · from notes
Explain risk aversion, utility and the Sharpe ratio with reference to allocation between a risky asset and a risk-free asset.
Introduction

Investors differ in their tolerance for risk. Utility theory measures how an investor trades return against risk, which determines how much to place in risky assets.

Risk aversion and utility

U = E(r) − ½Aσ², where A is the index of risk aversion. Higher A means a heavier penalty for risk.

Complete portfolio

With proportion y in the risky asset: E(rc) = Rf + y[E(r) − Rf], σc = yσ. These portfolios lie on a straight line, the capital allocation line.

Sharpe ratio

S = [E(r) − Rf]/σ; for every y it equals the slope of the CAL, so it is the same for all combinations.

Optimal allocation

y* = [E(r) − Rf]/(Aσ²).

Example (Q19)

E(r) = 16%, Rf = 7%, σ = 27%, A = 3. Utility: 8.85% (y = 0.4), 8.46% (0.6), 7.20% (0.8). Sharpe = 0.333 in all cases. y* = 0.4115 gives E = 10.70%, σ = 11.11%, U = 8.85%, the maximum.

Conclusion

The risk-return opportunity (Sharpe ratio) is the same for all investors, but each investor's risk aversion decides where on the line they sit.

N1NumericalPractice · from notes
A mutual fund has total assets of ₹120 crore, liabilities of ₹5 crore and 5 crore units outstanding. Calculate its NAV.
Formula in words
Net Asset Value
NAV = (Total assets − Total liabilities) / Units outstanding

In words: NAV per unit is what the fund owns minus what it owes, shared equally among all units.

  • Total assetsmarket value of everything the fund holds
  • − Total liabilitiessubtract what the fund owes
  • ÷ Unitsvalue of one unit
Solution
NAV = (Total assets − Total liabilities) / Units outstanding
    = (120 − 5) / 5 = 115 / 5 = ₹23

NAV = ₹23 per unit

N2NumericalPractice · from notes
Mr. Rahul buys 500 equity shares of ABC Ltd. at ₹400. During the year the company declares a dividend of ₹12 per share and the price rises to ₹470. Calculate his dividend income, capital appreciation and total return (₹ and %).
Formula in words
Total return on shares
Total return = N × D + N × (P₁ − P₀); % = Total ÷ (N × P₀)

In words: Shareholders earn dividend income plus the rise in the share price; divide by the amount invested for a percentage.

  • N × Ddividend income
  • N × (P₁ − P₀)capital appreciation
  • N × P₀amount invested (base for %)
Solution
Investment = 500 × 400 = ₹2,00,000
Dividend = 500 × 12 = ₹6,000
Capital appreciation = 500 × (470 − 400) = ₹35,000
Total return = 6,000 + 35,000 = ₹41,000 → 41,000 / 2,00,000 = 20.5%

Total return ₹41,000 (20.5%), from both dividend and capital appreciation. A fall in price would have produced a capital loss instead.

An investor holds shares worth ₹2,00,000, receives dividends of ₹8,000, and the market value rises to ₹2,30,000 after one year. What is the total return?
Formula in words
Total return on shares
Total return = N × D + N × (P₁ − P₀); % = Total ÷ (N × P₀)

In words: Shareholders earn dividend income plus the rise in the share price; divide by the amount invested for a percentage.

  • N × Ddividend income
  • N × (P₁ − P₀)capital appreciation
  • N × P₀amount invested (base for %)
Solution
Capital appreciation = 2,30,000 − 2,00,000 = ₹30,000
Total = 8,000 + 30,000 = ₹38,000 → 19%

Total return ₹38,000 = 19%

N4NumericalPractice · from notes
ABC Ltd. issues secured debentures of ₹1,000 each at 8% for ten years. Mr. Ravi buys 50 debentures. Calculate his annual interest and the amount repaid at maturity.
Formula in words
Interest on debentures / bonds
Annual interest = Face value invested × Coupon rate

In words: Each year the holder receives the face value times the coupon rate, whatever the issuer's profit; the face value is repaid at maturity.

  • Face value investednumber of units × face value of each
  • Coupon ratefixed annual interest rate
Solution
Investment = 50 × 1,000 = ₹50,000
Annual interest = 50,000 × 8% = ₹4,000 (paid regardless of profits)
At maturity: ₹50,000 principal repaid
Total received over 10 years = 40,000 + 50,000 = ₹90,000

₹4,000 a year; ₹50,000 at maturity.

Annual interest = Face value invested × Coupon rate

In words: Each year the holder receives the face value times the coupon rate, whatever the issuer's profit; the face value is repaid at maturity.

  • Face value investednumber of units × face value of each
  • Coupon ratefixed annual interest rate
Solution
Investment = 200 × 1,000 = ₹2,00,000
Annual interest = 2,00,000 × 7% = ₹14,000
At maturity: ₹2,00,000 repaid

₹14,000 a year with sovereign backing (minimal default risk) and assured repayment, ideal for conservative, income-seeking investors.

N6NumericalPractice · from notes
Mr. Anand invests ₹5,00,000 in a ten-year Government Security with a 7% annual coupon. Calculate his yearly income and total cash received over the life.
Formula in words
Interest on debentures / bonds
Annual interest = Face value invested × Coupon rate

In words: Each year the holder receives the face value times the coupon rate, whatever the issuer's profit; the face value is repaid at maturity.

  • Face value investednumber of units × face value of each

Over 10 years = 3,50,000 + 5,00,000 principal = ₹8,50,000

N7NumericalPractice · from notes
Mr. Arjun buys 100 convertible debentures of ₹1,000 at 7% interest, convertible after five years into 20 equity shares each. Calculate the interest received before conversion and the shares received. What is the implied conversion price?
Formula in words
Interest on debentures / bonds
Annual interest = Face value invested × Coupon rate

In words: Each year the holder receives the face value times the coupon rate, whatever the issuer's profit; the face value is repaid at maturity.

  • Face value investednumber of units × face value of each
  • Coupon ratefixed annual interest rate
Convertible debentures
Shares received = Debentures × Conversion ratio; Implied price = Face ÷ Ratio

In words: On conversion each debenture becomes a fixed number of shares; dividing face value by that number gives the effective price paid per share.

  • Conversion ratioshares per debenture
  • Face ÷ Ratioimplied conversion price per share
Solution
Investment = ₹1,00,000
Annual interest = 1,00,000 × 7% = ₹7,000 → 5 years = ₹35,000
Shares on conversion = 100 × 20 = 2,000 shares
Implied conversion price = 1,000 / 20 = ₹50 per share

₹7,000 a year for 5 years, then 2,000 shares. Conversion pays off if the share price exceeds ₹50; interest stops after conversion.

N8NumericalPractice · from notes
An employee's basic salary is ₹50,000 a month. Both employee and employer contribute 12% to the provident fund. Calculate the monthly and annual contribution.
Formula in words
Provident fund contribution
Monthly PF = Employee % × Basic + Employer % × Basic

In words: Each month the employee and the employer each pay a fixed percentage of basic salary into the fund.

  • Basicbasic salary (not gross)
  • 12% + 12%employee share + employer share in the notes' example
Solution
Employee = 12% × 50,000 = ₹6,000
Employer = 12% × 50,000 = ₹6,000
Monthly total = ₹12,000; annual = ₹1,44,000 (before interest)

₹12,000 a month, compounding into a retirement corpus.

N9NumericalPractice · from notes
Mr. Rohan buys an apartment for ₹60 lakh and rents it at ₹25,000 a month. After ten years its value is ₹95 lakh. Calculate the annual rent, rental yield and capital appreciation.
Formula in words
Real estate returns
Annual rent = Monthly rent × 12; Appreciation = Current value − Cost; Yield = Rent ÷ Cost

In words: Property earns rent every year plus the rise in its value when sold.

  • Monthly rent × 12rental income for the year
  • Current value − Costcapital gain
  • Rent ÷ Costrental yield
Solution
Annual rent = 25,000 × 12 = ₹3,00,000
Rental yield = 3,00,000 / 60,00,000 = 5%
Capital appreciation = 95 − 60 = ₹35 lakh (58.3% over 10 years)

₹3 lakh rent a year (5% yield) plus ₹35 lakh appreciation: the dual return of real estate.

N10NumericalPractice · from notes
Mr. Raj has ₹30 lakh and splits it 70% Indian avenues and 30% international. How much goes to each and why?
Formula in words
Asset allocation
Amount in an avenue = Total × Weight; Weight = Amount ÷ Total

In words: Split the total money according to the chosen percentages (or work out each percentage by dividing the amount by the total).

  • Weightshare of total in that avenue
  • Σ weightsmust add to 100%
Solution
India: 30 × 0.70 = ₹21 lakh (equity MFs, G-secs, PPF, Gold ETFs, real estate)
Foreign: 30 × 0.30 = ₹9 lakh (international MFs, global ETFs, MNC shares)

₹21 lakh / ₹9 lakh. He benefits from India's growth while reducing country-specific risk; one market's weakness may be offset by the other.

N13CasePractice · from notes
Mrs. Anita plans to retire in 20 years and wants to put all ₹30 lakh in fixed deposits for safety. Advise her.

FDs are safe but over 20 years their returns may not beat inflation. She should diversify: equity mutual funds for growth, government securities and PPF for safety and tax benefit, Gold ETFs for inflation protection, and some FDs for liquidity. This achieves higher long-term growth while keeping security and liquidity.

Theory practice (24)

T12-markPractice · from notes
What is an investment avenue?

Any financial or physical asset in which surplus funds are invested with the expectation of income, capital appreciation or both, while accepting an appropriate level of risk.

T22-markPractice · from notes
Distinguish liquidity from marketability.

Marketability is the ease of buying or selling an investment in a market at a fair price; liquidity is the speed and ease with which it can be converted into cash with minimal loss of value.

T32-markPractice · from notes
What are gilt-edged securities?

Debt securities issued by the Central or State Governments, carrying a sovereign guarantee, paying periodic coupons and repaying principal at maturity; among the safest investments.

T42-markPractice · from notes
What is a Treasury Bill?

A short-term Government of India debt instrument of 91, 182 or 364 days, issued at a discount and redeemed at par; considered virtually risk-free.

T52-markPractice · from notes
What is Commercial Paper?

An unsecured short-term promissory note issued by financially strong, highly rated companies to meet working capital needs.

T62-markPractice · from notes
What is NAV?

Net Asset Value is the market value of one mutual fund unit: (Total assets − Total liabilities) ÷ Units outstanding, calculated at the end of each trading day.

T72-markPractice · from notes
What is SIP?

A Systematic Investment Plan invests a fixed amount at regular intervals, promoting discipline and benefiting from rupee-cost averaging and compounding.

T82-markPractice · from notes
What is meant by EEE in PPF?

Exempt–Exempt–Exempt: contributions qualify for deduction, interest earned is tax-free and the maturity amount is tax-exempt, subject to prevailing tax laws.

T92-markPractice · from notes
What are Sovereign Gold Bonds?

Government securities issued through RBI whose value is linked to the market price of gold and which also pay periodic interest.

T102-markPractice · from notes
What is a convertible debenture?

A debenture that pays fixed interest initially and can be converted into equity shares after a specified period at a predetermined ratio and price.

T112-markPractice · from notes
What is a REIT?

A Real Estate Investment Trust pools investor funds to acquire income-generating commercial properties and distributes rental income; it offers more liquidity than direct property.

T122-markPractice · from notes
What is an ETF?

An Exchange Traded Fund is a pooled fund tracking an index, sector or commodity whose units trade on a stock exchange throughout the day like shares.

T132-markPractice · from notes
What are ADRs and GDRs?

Depository receipts representing shares of a foreign company: ADRs trade in the US, GDRs in other international markets; they enable cross-border investment.

T142-markPractice · from notes
What is Repo?

A repurchase agreement under which banks borrow short-term funds from RBI by selling government securities with an agreement to repurchase them; Reverse Repo is banks depositing surplus with RBI.

T155-markPractice · from notes
Explain the investment attributes used to evaluate investment avenues.
Introduction

Every avenue is judged on attributes that determine its suitability for an investor.

The six attributes
  • Risk: possibility that actual return differs from expected; managed by diversification. Government securities low, equity high.
  • Return: reward as interest, dividends, rent, bonus or capital appreciation; rises with risk (risk–return trade-off).
  • Security: safety of principal; high for G-secs, T-bills, FDs, PPF.
  • Marketability: ease of buying/selling at a fair price; listed shares sell in minutes, property may take months.
  • Liquidity: speed of conversion into cash with minimal loss; savings accounts, T-bills, liquid funds are highly liquid.
  • Convenience: ease of purchase, management, monitoring and redemption (online platforms, e-KYC, SIP).
Conclusion

No avenue scores highly on all six; investors choose a mix aligned with goals, risk tolerance and horizon (Mrs. Priya's illustration).

T165-markPractice · from notes
Distinguish between equity shares and debentures.
Meaning

Equity shares represent ownership; debentures represent borrowed funds.

Differences
  • Status: shareholders are owners; debenture holders are creditors.
  • Return: variable dividends depending on profit vs fixed interest irrespective of profit.
  • Voting: shareholders vote; debenture holders do not.
  • Priority: debenture holders are repaid before shareholders in liquidation.
  • Risk/return: equity offers greater capital appreciation with higher risk; debentures offer regular income and capital protection.
  • For the company: interest is tax-deductible; dividends are an appropriation of profit.
Example

Mr. Ravi's 8% debentures pay ₹4,000 a year regardless of profits; Mr. Rahul's equity shares earned ₹6,000 dividend plus ₹35,000 appreciation, but could have lost value.

T175-markPractice · from notes
Explain money market instruments.
Meaning

Short-term instruments (one day to one year) used to manage short-term liquidity and working capital.

Instruments
  • Treasury Bills: Government, 91/182/364 days, discount instruments, virtually risk-free.
  • Commercial Paper: unsecured promissory notes of highly rated companies; higher return than T-bills.
  • Certificates of Deposit: negotiable instruments issued by banks; tradable before maturity.
  • Call and Notice Money: inter-bank borrowing for one day (call) or 2–14 days (notice).
  • Repo and Reverse Repo: RBI's liquidity tools.
Advantages

High liquidity, low default risk, efficient cash management.

Limitations

Lower returns, large minimum amounts, limited inflation protection.

T185-markPractice · from notes
Explain the structure and types of mutual funds.
Meaning

A professionally managed scheme pooling investors' money into a diversified portfolio; investors hold units valued at NAV.

Structure (SEBI-regulated trust)
  • Sponsor: establishes the fund and appoints trustees.
  • Trustees: safeguard investors' interests and supervise.
  • AMC: manages the portfolio through fund managers.
  • Custodian: holds the securities.
  • Registrars & Transfer Agents: maintain records and process transactions.
Types

Equity, debt, hybrid, index, liquid, sectoral/thematic and tax-saving (ELSS) funds.

Key formula

NAV = (Total assets − Total liabilities)/Units outstanding.

T195-markPractice · from notes
Describe the forms of gold investment and their merits.
Introduction

Gold is held for wealth preservation, inflation protection and diversification.

Forms
  • Physical gold: jewellery, coins, bars; tangible but has making charges, storage, insurance and purity issues.
  • Gold ETFs: exchange-traded, backed by physical gold; liquid and transparent.
  • Sovereign Gold Bonds: Government securities via RBI; gold-price linked plus periodic interest.
  • Digital Gold: bought online in small quantities; held by custodians.
Merits

Hedge against inflation and crises; moves independently of equities (COVID-19 case).

Limitations

No regular income (except SGB interest); returns depend only on price; lower long-term return than equity.

T205-markPractice · from notes
Distinguish between ETFs and mutual funds.
Common ground

Both provide diversified portfolios.

Differences
  • Trading: ETFs trade continuously on exchanges; MF units are bought/redeemed at end-of-day NAV.
  • Management: ETFs are mostly passive; many MFs are actively managed.
  • Cost: ETFs have lower expense ratios.
  • Account: ETFs need demat and trading accounts; MFs can be bought directly from the AMC.
  • Charges: brokerage on ETF trades.
  • Performance: passive ETFs cannot outperform their benchmark.
T215-markPractice · from notes
Explain government-backed investment schemes.
Introduction

Schemes supported by the Government or employers that offer safety and stable returns.

Schemes
  • Gilt-edged securities: sovereign-guaranteed coupons and principal.
  • Post Office schemes: Savings a/c, RD, TD, MIS, NSC, KVP, SCSS, SSY.
  • Company PF/EPF: employer and employee each contribute (e.g., 12% of basic).
  • PPF: long-term, EEE tax treatment, limited liquidity.
Advantages

Safety, predictable returns, discipline, tax incentives.

Limitations

Lower returns than equity, may not beat inflation, lock-in restrictions.

T22Long answerPractice · from notes
Discuss the various investment avenues available to an investor in India and abroad, and explain how an investor should choose among them.
Introduction

Investment converts savings into productive assets to meet goals such as wealth creation, regular income, retirement and education. Globalization, technology and financial innovation have widened the choices available.

Definition

An investment avenue is any financial or physical asset in which surplus funds are invested for income, capital appreciation or both.

Avenues in India
  • Equity shares: ownership; dividends + capital appreciation; high risk, high long-term return.
  • Preference shares: fixed dividend with priority.
  • Debentures and bonds: fixed income, priority over shareholders; government bonds safest.
  • Convertible/hybrid securities: income now, equity later.
  • Fixed deposits: assured returns, DICGC protection.
  • Money market instruments: T-bills, CP, CD, call/notice money, repo; short-term liquidity.
  • Government-backed schemes: gilt-edged securities, Post Office schemes, EPF, PPF (EEE).
  • Gold: physical, ETFs, SGBs, digital gold; inflation hedge.
  • ETFs and Mutual funds: diversified, professionally managed; NAV, SIP.
  • Real estate and REITs: rent + appreciation; illiquid.
  • Insurance: protection with savings in some plans (ULIPs).
Foreign avenues

International equities (NYSE, NASDAQ, LSE, TSE), global mutual funds and ETFs, ADRs/GDRs, foreign bonds, international REITs; benefits of global diversification against exchange-rate, political, regulatory and tax risks.

How to choose
  • Evaluate on the six attributes: risk, return, security, marketability, liquidity, convenience.
  • Consider personal (age, income, goals, risk tolerance), economic (inflation, interest rates, taxation) and market factors.
  • Follow the planning process: goals → risk tolerance → selection → monitoring → review.
Example

Mrs. Meera (35, ₹25 lakh): FDs for emergencies, equity/MFs for long-term wealth, PPF for retirement, gold as an inflation hedge, real estate for appreciation.

Conclusion

No single avenue is ideal for every investor. A diversified portfolio combining financial and physical, domestic and foreign avenues balances safety, income and growth.

T23Long answerPractice · from notes
Explain bonds and debentures: meaning, features, types, advantages and limitations. Distinguish between the two.
Introduction

Bonds and debentures are fixed-income debt instruments through which issuers borrow long-term funds from investors.

Debentures

A written acknowledgment of debt issued by a company under its common seal. Features: fixed interest, definite maturity, may be secured or unsecured, priority over shareholders, no voting rights, transferable. Types: secured / unsecured (naked); convertible / non-convertible (NCD); redeemable / irredeemable (perpetual).

Bonds

A debt instrument issued by governments, PSUs, institutions or corporations for a specified period at a coupon rate. Types: government, corporate, municipal, zero-coupon, floating-rate, inflation-indexed bonds.

Advantages
  • Regular, predictable income; priority in liquidation; tradable in secondary market.
  • For issuers: finance without dilution; interest tax-deductible.
Limitations
  • Fixed returns do not rise with profits.
  • Interest rate risk: prices fall when rates rise.
  • Credit risk (corporate issuers); inflation erodes real income.
  • Mandatory interest raises issuers' financial risk.
Bonds vs Debentures
  • Issuer: bonds by governments, PSUs, institutions and corporations; debentures mainly by companies.
  • Safety: government bonds carry sovereign backing; debentures depend on the company's strength.
  • Security: bonds may be secured or government-backed; debentures may be secured or unsecured.
  • Return: government bonds usually lower risk and lower return than corporate debentures.
Example

Mrs. Lakshmi's 7% Government bonds (₹14,000 a year) vs Mr. Ravi's 8% secured debentures of ABC Ltd. (₹4,000 a year on ₹50,000).

Conclusion

Both suit income-oriented, conservative investors; the choice depends on risk tolerance, income needs and the issuer's credit quality.

T24Long answerPractice · from notes
Discuss mutual funds as an investment avenue with reference to structure, types, NAV and SIP, along with their advantages and limitations.
Introduction

Mutual funds enable small investors to access professionally managed, diversified portfolios.

Meaning

A professionally managed scheme that pools money from many investors and invests in securities according to stated objectives; investors receive units representing proportionate ownership.

Structure

SEBI-regulated trust: Sponsor, Trustees, AMC, Custodian, Registrars and Transfer Agents.

Types

Equity, debt, hybrid, index, liquid, sectoral/thematic and ELSS funds.

NAV

NAV = (Total assets − Total liabilities)/Units outstanding. Example: (120 − 5)/5 = ₹23.

SIP

Fixed periodic investment; rupee-cost averaging buys more units when prices are low; compounding grows the corpus (Mr. Sandeep's ₹5,000 monthly SIP; Ms. Pooja's ₹10,000 SIP funded her first home).

Advantages

Professional management, diversification, liquidity at NAV, small investment amounts, transparency, SEBI oversight, variety of schemes.

Limitations

Management fees reduce returns, returns not guaranteed, limited investor control, concentration risk in sectoral funds.

Conclusion

With careful scheme selection and disciplined investing, mutual funds offer a favourable balance between risk and return for long-term goals.

Q1NumericalFrom your PDF
A bond has a face value of ₹1,000, carries an annual coupon rate of 8% and has 5 years remaining to maturity. If the required rate of return is 10%, calculate the present value of the bond and state whether it will trade at a premium or discount.
Formula in words
Annual coupon
C = Coupon rate × Face value

In words: The yearly interest in rupees is the coupon rate applied to the face value (never to the market price).

  • Coupon rateprinted interest rate
  • Face valuepar / redemption value
Bond value (present value)
V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In words: A bond is worth today's value of all its coupons plus today's value of the face value it repays at maturity.

  • C × PVIFA(k, n)present value of n yearly coupons (annuity factor)
  • F × PVIF(k, n)present value of the single face-value repayment
  • krequired return / market yield: the discount rate (not the coupon rate)
  • nyears to maturity
Premium or discount
Coupon rate > k → premium; = k → par; < k → discount

In words: If the bond pays more interest than investors require, they pay above face value; if less, below face value.

  • PremiumV₀ − F when V₀ > F
  • DiscountF − V₀ when V₀ < F
Solution
C = 8% × ₹1,000 = ₹80.00
PVIFA(10%, 5) = 3.7908    PVIF(10%, 5) = 0.6209
PV of coupons   = 80.00 × 3.7908 = ₹303.26
PV of principal = 1,000 × 0.6209 = ₹620.92
Bond value V₀   = 303.26 + 620.92 = ₹924.18

Value ₹924.18 < face ₹1,000 → discount of ₹75.82, because the coupon rate (8%) is below the required return (10%).

Q3NumericalFrom your PDF
A ₹1,000 face-value bond carries an annual coupon rate of 8% and is currently selling for ₹950. Calculate the current yield on the bond.
Formula in words
Annual coupon
C = Coupon rate × Face value

In words: The yearly interest in rupees is the coupon rate applied to the face value (never to the market price).

  • Coupon rateprinted interest rate
  • Face valuepar / redemption value
Current yield
CY = Annual coupon ÷ Current market price

In words: Current yield is the yearly coupon as a percentage of the price you pay today.

  • Annual couponrupees of interest a year
  • Market pricewhat you pay now (not face value)
Solution
C = 8% × 1,000 = ₹80
Current yield = C / P = 80 / 950 = 0.0842

Current yield = 8.42% (above the 8% coupon because the bond trades at a discount).

Q4NumericalFrom your PDF
A ₹1,000 bond pays an annual coupon of ₹100 and is currently quoted at ₹1,080. Calculate the current yield and comment on the pricing of the bond.
Formula in words
Current yield
CY = Annual coupon ÷ Current market price

In words: Current yield is the yearly coupon as a percentage of the price you pay today.

  • Annual couponrupees of interest a year
  • Market pricewhat you pay now (not face value)
Coupon, current yield and YTM
Discount bond: coupon < CY < YTM; Premium bond: coupon > CY > YTM

In words: Below face value every yield is above the coupon rate, and YTM is highest because it adds the gain at maturity; above face value it is the reverse.

  • Coupon ratemeasured on face value
  • CYmeasured on market price
  • YTMincludes gain/loss to maturity and time value
Solution
Current yield = 100 / 1,080 = 0.0926

CY = 9.26%. The bond trades at a premium (₹1,080 > ₹1,000) because its 10% coupon is above the market's required yield; for a premium bond coupon (10%) > CY (9.26%) > YTM.

Q5NumericalFrom your PDF
A bond with a face value of ₹5,000 carries an annual coupon rate of 10% and has 4 years remaining to maturity. If the market yield is 12%, calculate its present value.
Formula in words
Annual coupon
C = Coupon rate × Face value

In words: The yearly interest in rupees is the coupon rate applied to the face value (never to the market price).

  • Coupon rateprinted interest rate
  • Face valuepar / redemption value
Bond value (present value)
V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In words: A bond is worth today's value of all its coupons plus today's value of the face value it repays at maturity.

  • C × PVIFA(k, n)present value of n yearly coupons (annuity factor)
  • F × PVIF(k, n)present value of the single face-value repayment
  • krequired return / market yield: the discount rate (not the coupon rate)
  • nyears to maturity
Solution
C = 10% × ₹5,000 = ₹500.00
