Q6NumericalFrom your PDF
A 3-year bond has a face value of ₹1,000, an annual coupon rate of 10% and a YTM of 10%. Calculate its Macaulay Duration.
Formula in words
Macaulay duration
D = Σ [t × PV(CFₜ)] ÷ Price, where Price = Σ PV(CFₜ)

In words: Duration is the average time you wait for the bond's money: multiply each year's present value by its year number, add, then divide by the price.

  • CFₜcash flow in year t (last year = coupon + face value)
  • PV(CFₜ)CFₜ × PVIF at the YTM
  • t × PVeach PV weighted by when it arrives
  • ÷ Priceturns the total into an average time in years
Solution
Year tCash flow (₹)PVIF @ 10%PV (₹)t × PV
1100.000.909190.9190.91
2100.000.826482.64165.29
31,100.000.7513826.452,479.34
Total1,000.002,735.54
Price = Σ PV = ₹1,000.00 (at par, since coupon = YTM)
D = Σ(t × PV) / Price = 2,735.54 / 1,000.00 = 2.7355

Macaulay duration = 2.74 years (less than the 3-year maturity because coupons arrive earlier).

Q7NumericalFrom your PDF
A bond has a Macaulay Duration of 5 years and a YTM of 8%. Calculate its Modified Duration.
Formula in words
Modified duration
MD = Macaulay D ÷ (1 + y)

In words: Modified duration is Macaulay duration divided by one plus the yield.

  • DMacaulay duration in years
  • yYTM as a decimal (1 + 0.08, not 1 + 8)
Solution
MD = D / (1 + y) = 5 / 1.08 = 4.6296

Modified duration = 4.63: price changes by about 4.63% for every 1% change in yield.

Q8NumericalFrom your PDF
A bond has a Modified Duration of 6 years and its current price is ₹1,200. If the yield increases by 0.75%, estimate the percentage change in the bond price and its approximate new price.
Formula in words
Price change from a yield change
%ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy, ₹ΔP = %ΔP × P, New P = P + ₹ΔP

In words: The price changes by about minus modified duration times the change in yield; apply that % to the price for the rupee change.

  • −yield up → price down; yield down → price up
  • Δychange in yield in % points (0.75 for 0.75%)
  • × Pconvert the % change into rupees
Solution
%ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy = −6 × 0.75 = −4.5%
₹ΔP = −4.5% × 1,200 = −₹54
New price ≈ 1,200 − 54 = ₹1,146

Price falls by about 4.5%, to approximately ₹1,146.

Q9NumericalFrom your PDF
A bond has a Modified Duration of 4.5 years. If the market yield increases by 1%, estimate the percentage change in its price.
Formula in words
Price change from a yield change
%ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy, ₹ΔP = %ΔP × P, New P = P + ₹ΔP

In words: The price changes by about minus modified duration times the change in yield; apply that % to the price for the rupee change.

  • −yield up → price down; yield down → price up
  • Δychange in yield in % points (0.75 for 0.75%)
  • × Pconvert the % change into rupees
Solution
%ΔP ≈ −4.5 × 1 = −4.5%

Price falls by approximately 4.5%.

Q10NumericalFrom your PDF
A zero-coupon bond has a maturity period of 6 years. Calculate its Macaulay Duration and explain why its duration is equal to its maturity.
Formula in words
Zero-coupon bond
P = F ÷ (1 + k)ⁿ, D = n

In words: A zero-coupon bond's price is just the face value discounted once; since all its money comes at maturity, its duration equals its maturity.

  • Fface value paid at the end
  • (1 + k)ⁿdiscounting for n years
  • D = nsingle payment → average time = maturity
Solution
Only one cash flow: face value at t = 6
D = (6 × PV) / PV = 6

Macaulay duration = 6 years. A zero-coupon bond pays nothing until maturity, so 100% of its present value is received at year 6; the weighted-average time of its cash flows is therefore exactly its maturity.

Q11NumericalFrom your PDF
A company issues a 5-year bond with a face value of ₹1,000 carrying an annual coupon rate of 10%. The required rate of return is 8%. Calculate: (a) annual coupon payment, (b) present value of coupon payments, (c) present value of principal repayment, (d) current value of the bond, and (e) whether the bond is selling at par, premium or discount.
Formula in words
Annual coupon
C = Coupon rate × Face value

In words: The yearly interest in rupees is the coupon rate applied to the face value (never to the market price).

  • Coupon rateprinted interest rate
  • Face valuepar / redemption value
Bond value (present value)
V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In words: A bond is worth today's value of all its coupons plus today's value of the face value it repays at maturity.

  • C × PVIFA(k, n)present value of n yearly coupons (annuity factor)
  • F × PVIF(k, n)present value of the single face-value repayment
  • krequired return / market yield: the discount rate (not the coupon rate)
  • nyears to maturity
Premium or discount
Coupon rate > k → premium; = k → par; < k → discount

In words: If the bond pays more interest than investors require, they pay above face value; if less, below face value.

  • PremiumV₀ − F when V₀ > F
  • DiscountF − V₀ when V₀ < F
Solution
C = 10% × ₹1,000 = ₹100.00
PVIFA(8%, 5) = 3.9927    PVIF(8%, 5) = 0.6806
PV of coupons   = 100.00 × 3.9927 = ₹399.27
PV of principal = 1,000 × 0.6806 = ₹680.58
Bond value V₀   = 399.27 + 680.58 = ₹1,079.85

(a) ₹100.00 (b) ₹399.27 (c) ₹680.58 (d) ₹1,079.85 (e) Value ₹1,079.85 > face ₹1,000 → premium of ₹79.85, because the coupon rate (10%) exceeds the required return (8%).

Q12NumericalFrom your PDF
A ₹5,000 bond carries a coupon rate of 9% and has 6 years remaining to maturity. The required rate of return is 11%. Calculate the market value of the bond using the present-value approach and explain why the bond trades below its face value.
Formula in words
Annual coupon
C = Coupon rate × Face value

In words: The yearly interest in rupees is the coupon rate applied to the face value (never to the market price).

  • Coupon rateprinted interest rate
  • Face valuepar / redemption value
Bond value (present value)
V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In words: A bond is worth today's value of all its coupons plus today's value of the face value it repays at maturity.

  • C × PVIFA(k, n)present value of n yearly coupons (annuity factor)
  • F × PVIF(k, n)present value of the single face-value repayment
  • krequired return / market yield: the discount rate (not the coupon rate)
  • nyears to maturity
Premium or discount
Coupon rate > k → premium; = k → par; < k → discount

In words: If the bond pays more interest than investors require, they pay above face value; if less, below face value.

  • PremiumV₀ − F when V₀ > F
  • DiscountF − V₀ when V₀ < F
Solution
C = 9% × ₹5,000 = ₹450.00
PVIFA(11%, 6) = 4.2305    PVIF(11%, 6) = 0.5346
PV of coupons   = 450.00 × 4.2305 = ₹1,903.74
PV of principal = 5,000 × 0.5346 = ₹2,673.20
Bond value V₀   = 1,903.74 + 2,673.20 = ₹4,576.95

Value ₹4,576.95 < face ₹5,000 → discount of ₹423.05, because the coupon rate (9%) is below the required return (11%). It trades below face value because investors can earn 11% elsewhere, so they will pay only the price at which the 9% coupons plus ₹5,000 at maturity deliver 11%.

Q13NumericalFrom your PDF
A 10-year bond with a face value of ₹1,000 and coupon rate of 8% is currently selling for ₹920. Calculate: (a) annual coupon, (b) current yield, (c) whether the bond is a premium or discount bond, and (d) explain the relationship between coupon rate, current yield and YTM for the bond.
Formula in words
Annual coupon
C = Coupon rate × Face value

In words: The yearly interest in rupees is the coupon rate applied to the face value (never to the market price).

  • Coupon rateprinted interest rate
  • Face valuepar / redemption value
Current yield
CY = Annual coupon ÷ Current market price

In words: Current yield is the yearly coupon as a percentage of the price you pay today.

  • Annual couponrupees of interest a year
  • Market pricewhat you pay now (not face value)
Approximate yield to maturity
YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n] ÷ [(F + P)/2]

In words: YTM is the yearly coupon plus the yearly share of the gain (or loss) to maturity, divided by the average of face value and price.

  • Cannual coupon
  • (F − P)/ngain per year if bought below face (negative if bought above)
  • (F + P)/2average amount invested over the life
Coupon, current yield and YTM
Discount bond: coupon < CY < YTM; Premium bond: coupon > CY > YTM

In words: Below face value every yield is above the coupon rate, and YTM is highest because it adds the gain at maturity; above face value it is the reverse.

  • Coupon ratemeasured on face value
  • CYmeasured on market price
  • YTMincludes gain/loss to maturity and time value
Solution
(a) C = 8% × 1,000 = ₹80
(b) CY = 80 / 920 = 8.70%
(c) Price ₹920 < face ₹1,000 → discount bond
(d) Approx YTM = [80 + (1,000 − 920)/10] / [(1,000 + 920)/2] = 88 / 960 = 9.17%

Coupon 8% < CY 8.70% < YTM ≈ 9.17%. For a discount bond, CY exceeds the coupon because the price is below face value, and YTM is highest because it also counts the ₹80 gain at maturity.

A bond with a face value of ₹1,000 carries a 12% annual coupon and has 8 years to maturity. An investor's required return is 10%. Calculate: (a) value of the bond using the present-value method, (b) premium per bond over face value, and (c) reason for the premium.
Formula in words
Annual coupon
C = Coupon rate × Face value

In words: The yearly interest in rupees is the coupon rate applied to the face value (never to the market price).

  • Coupon rateprinted interest rate
  • Face valuepar / redemption value
Bond value (present value)
V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In words: A bond is worth today's value of all its coupons plus today's value of the face value it repays at maturity.

  • C × PVIFA(k, n)present value of n yearly coupons (annuity factor)
  • F × PVIF(k, n)present value of the single face-value repayment
  • krequired return / market yield: the discount rate (not the coupon rate)
  • nyears to maturity
Premium or discount
Coupon rate > k → premium; = k → par; < k → discount

In words: If the bond pays more interest than investors require, they pay above face value; if less, below face value.

  • PremiumV₀ − F when V₀ > F
  • DiscountF − V₀ when V₀ < F
Solution
C = 12% × ₹1,000 = ₹120.00
PVIFA(10%, 8) = 5.3349    PVIF(10%, 8) = 0.4665
PV of coupons   = 120.00 × 5.3349 = ₹640.19
PV of principal = 1,000 × 0.4665 = ₹466.51
Bond value V₀   = 640.19 + 466.51 = ₹1,106.70

(a) ₹1,106.70 (b) Premium = ₹106.70 (c) The 12% coupon is higher than the 10% return investors require, so they pay more than face value for the extra interest.

Q15NumericalFrom your PDF
A ₹1,000, 7% coupon bond has 5 years remaining to maturity and is currently quoted at ₹930. Calculate the approximate YTM. Compare the YTM with the coupon rate and explain the result.
Formula in words
Approximate yield to maturity
YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n] ÷ [(F + P)/2]

In words: YTM is the yearly coupon plus the yearly share of the gain (or loss) to maturity, divided by the average of face value and price.

  • Cannual coupon
  • (F − P)/ngain per year if bought below face (negative if bought above)
  • (F + P)/2average amount invested over the life
Solution
C = ₹70
YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n] / [(F + P)/2] = [70 + (1,000 − 930)/5] / [(1,000 + 930)/2]
    = (70 + 14) / 965 = 8.70%
(Exact YTM by trial ≈ 8.79%)

YTM ≈ 8.70% > coupon 7%, because the bond is bought at a ₹70 discount that is gained at maturity in addition to the coupons.

Q16NumericalFrom your PDF
A 5-year bond with a face value of ₹1,000 pays an annual coupon of ₹80 and is currently selling for ₹950. Calculate: (a) annual coupon, (b) current yield, (c) approximate YTM, (d) compare the coupon rate, current yield and YTM, (e) explain why these three rates differ.
Formula in words
Current yield
CY = Annual coupon ÷ Current market price

In words: Current yield is the yearly coupon as a percentage of the price you pay today.

  • Annual couponrupees of interest a year
  • Market pricewhat you pay now (not face value)
Approximate yield to maturity
YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n] ÷ [(F + P)/2]

In words: YTM is the yearly coupon plus the yearly share of the gain (or loss) to maturity, divided by the average of face value and price.

  • Cannual coupon
  • (F − P)/ngain per year if bought below face (negative if bought above)
  • (F + P)/2average amount invested over the life
Coupon, current yield and YTM
Discount bond: coupon < CY < YTM; Premium bond: coupon > CY > YTM

In words: Below face value every yield is above the coupon rate, and YTM is highest because it adds the gain at maturity; above face value it is the reverse.

  • Coupon ratemeasured on face value
  • CYmeasured on market price
  • YTMincludes gain/loss to maturity and time value
Solution
(a) C = ₹80 (coupon rate 8%)
(b) CY = 80 / 950 = 8.42%
(c) YTM ≈ [80 + (1,000 − 950)/5] / [(1,000 + 950)/2] = 90 / 975 = 9.23%
(d) 8% < 8.42% < 9.23%

(e) The coupon rate is measured on face value, current yield on the lower market price, and YTM also includes the ₹50 capital gain spread over 5 years, so YTM is highest for this discount bond.

Q17NumericalFrom your PDF
A callable bond has a face value of ₹1,000, annual coupon rate of 10%, current market price of ₹1,050 and call price of ₹1,020, with 5 years until the call date. Calculate the Yield to Call by equating the current market price with the PV of the coupons and the call price. State whether the investor's return would differ if the bond were held to maturity.
Formula in words
Yield to call
P = C × PVIFA(YTC, n_c) + CP × PVIF(YTC, n_c); YTC ≈ [C + (CP − P)/n_c] ÷ [(CP + P)/2]

In words: Yield to call is the return if the issuer buys the bond back early: same as YTM but with the call price and the years to the call date.

  • CPcall price (replaces face value)
  • n_cyears to call date (replaces maturity)
  • Pcurrent market price
Solution
YTC equation: 1,050 = 100 × PVIFA(YTC, 5) + 1,020 × PVIF(YTC, 5)
Approx YTC = [100 + (1,020 − 1,050)/5] / [(1,020 + 1,050)/2] = 94 / 1,035 = 9.08%
Check at 9%: 100(3.8897) + 1,020(0.6499) = ₹1,051.87 ≈ 1,050 → exact YTC ≈ 9.05%

YTC ≈ 9.05%. Yes, the return would differ if held to maturity: the investor would keep earning ₹100 coupons beyond year 5 and receive ₹1,000 (not ₹1,020) at a later date. (For comparison, a 5-year YTM to ₹1,000 would be ≈ 8.72%.)

Q18NumericalFrom your PDF
A 4-year bond with a face value of ₹1,000 pays an annual coupon of 8%. The YTM is 10%. Calculate: (a) PV of each annual cash flow, (b) bond price, (c) time-weighted PV of each cash flow, and (d) Macaulay Duration.
Formula in words
Macaulay duration
D = Σ [t × PV(CFₜ)] ÷ Price, where Price = Σ PV(CFₜ)

In words: Duration is the average time you wait for the bond's money: multiply each year's present value by its year number, add, then divide by the price.

  • CFₜcash flow in year t (last year = coupon + face value)
  • PV(CFₜ)CFₜ × PVIF at the YTM
  • t × PVeach PV weighted by when it arrives
  • ÷ Priceturns the total into an average time in years
Solution
Year tCash flow (₹)PVIF @ 10%PV (₹)t × PV
180.000.909172.7372.73
280.000.826466.12132.23
380.000.751360.11180.32
41,080.000.6830737.652,950.62
Total936.603,335.89
(b) Price = ₹936.60
(d) D = 3,335.89 / 936.60 = 3.5617 years

(a) and (c) in the table; (b) ₹936.60; (d) Macaulay duration = 3.56 years.

Q19NumericalFrom your PDF
A bond has a Macaulay Duration of 6 years, YTM of 10% and current market price of ₹1,050. Calculate: (a) Modified Duration, (b) estimated % price change if yield increases by 2%, (c) estimated rupee change, (d) estimated new market price.
Formula in words
Modified duration
MD = Macaulay D ÷ (1 + y)

In words: Modified duration is Macaulay duration divided by one plus the yield.

  • DMacaulay duration in years
  • yYTM as a decimal (1 + 0.08, not 1 + 8)
Price change from a yield change
%ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy, ₹ΔP = %ΔP × P, New P = P + ₹ΔP

In words: The price changes by about minus modified duration times the change in yield; apply that % to the price for the rupee change.

  • −yield up → price down; yield down → price up
  • Δychange in yield in % points (0.75 for 0.75%)
  • × Pconvert the % change into rupees
Solution
(a) MD = 6 / 1.10 = 5.4545
(b) %ΔP ≈ −5.4545 × 2 = −10.91%
(c) ₹ΔP = −10.91% × 1,050 = −₹114.55
(d) New price ≈ 1,050 − 114.55 = ₹935.45

MD = 5.45; price falls about 10.91% (₹114.55) to ≈ ₹935.45.

Q20NumericalFrom your PDF
Bond A and Bond B are each priced at ₹1,000. Bond A has a Modified Duration of 3 years and Bond B of 7 years. If the market yield increases by 1%, calculate: (a) % price change for A, (b) for B, (c) rupee change for each, (d) compare their interest-rate sensitivity.
Formula in words
Price change from a yield change
%ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy, ₹ΔP = %ΔP × P, New P = P + ₹ΔP

In words: The price changes by about minus modified duration times the change in yield; apply that % to the price for the rupee change.

  • −yield up → price down; yield down → price up
  • Δychange in yield in % points (0.75 for 0.75%)
  • × Pconvert the % change into rupees
Solution
(a) A: −3 × 1 = −3%    (c) −3% × 1,000 = −₹30 → ≈ ₹970
(b) B: −7 × 1 = −7%    (c) −7% × 1,000 = −₹70 → ≈ ₹930

(d) Bond B is more than twice as sensitive to interest rates: its higher modified duration means a larger price fall for the same yield rise.

Q21NumericalFrom your PDF
Bond P and Bond Q each have face value ₹1,000 and 7 years remaining. P has a 10% annual coupon and Q a 6% annual coupon. The required return is 8%. Calculate the intrinsic value of both bonds and explain the premium/discount position of each.
Formula in words
Bond value (present value)
V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In words: A bond is worth today's value of all its coupons plus today's value of the face value it repays at maturity.

  • C × PVIFA(k, n)present value of n yearly coupons (annuity factor)
  • F × PVIF(k, n)present value of the single face-value repayment
  • krequired return / market yield: the discount rate (not the coupon rate)
  • nyears to maturity
Premium or discount
Coupon rate > k → premium; = k → par; < k → discount

In words: If the bond pays more interest than investors require, they pay above face value; if less, below face value.

  • PremiumV₀ − F when V₀ > F
  • DiscountF − V₀ when V₀ < F
Solution
Bond P: C = 10% × ₹1,000 = ₹100.00
PVIFA(8%, 7) = 5.2064    PVIF(8%, 7) = 0.5835
PV of coupons   = 100.00 × 5.2064 = ₹520.64
PV of principal = 1,000 × 0.5835 = ₹583.49
Bond value V₀   = 520.64 + 583.49 = ₹1,104.13
Bond Q: C = 6% × ₹1,000 = ₹60.00
PVIFA(8%, 7) = 5.2064    PVIF(8%, 7) = 0.5835
PV of coupons   = 60.00 × 5.2064 = ₹312.38
PV of principal = 1,000 × 0.5835 = ₹583.49
Bond value V₀   = 312.38 + 583.49 = ₹895.87

P = ₹1,104.13 (premium: 10% coupon > 8% required). Q = ₹895.87 (discount: 6% coupon < 8% required).

Q22NumericalFrom your PDF
Sunrise Ltd. has just paid a dividend of ₹12 per share. Dividends are expected to grow at 7% indefinitely and the required return is 14%. Calculate the intrinsic value using the Constant Growth DDM. Then recalculate the value if the required return rises to 17%.
Formula in words
Constant growth DDM (Gordon)
P₀ = D₁ ÷ (k − g), D₁ = D₀ × (1 + g)

In words: Share value is next year's dividend divided by the required return minus the growth rate.

  • D₀dividend just paid / currently paid
  • D₁next year's dividend: grow D₀ first
  • k − grequired return minus growth (decimals; k must exceed g)
Solution
D₁ = D₀(1 + g) = 12 × 1.07 = ₹12.84
At k = 14%: P₀ = 12.84 / (0.14 − 0.07) = 12.84 / 0.07 = ₹183.43
At k = 17%: P₀ = 12.84 / (0.17 − 0.07) = 12.84 / 0.10 = ₹128.40

₹183.43 at 14%; ₹128.40 at 17%. A higher required return lowers the value by 30%.

Q23NumericalFrom your PDF
PQR Ltd. reports EPS of ₹6 and the appropriate P/E ratio is 18. Earnings are expected to grow by 12% next year. Calculate the current estimated share price, next year's EPS and the forecast share price using the same P/E ratio.
Formula in words
P/E valuation
P = EPS × P/E; EPS₁ = EPS × (1 + g); P₁ = EPS₁ × P/E

In words: Price is earnings per share times the multiple the market pays; next year's price uses next year's EPS with the same multiple.

  • EPSearnings per share
  • P/Emultiple of comparable companies
  • (1 + g)grows EPS for next year
Solution
Current price = EPS × P/E = 6 × 18 = ₹108
EPS₁ = 6 × 1.12 = ₹6.72
Forecast price = 6.72 × 18 = ₹120.96

₹108 now; EPS ₹6.72 next year; forecast price ₹120.96 (12% higher, same as EPS growth).

Q24NumericalFrom your PDF
A preference share pays a fixed annual dividend of ₹11 indefinitely. The required rate of return is 11% and the market price is ₹90. Calculate intrinsic value and the investor's dividend yield at the market price. Compare intrinsic value with market price.
Formula in words
Preference share value (zero growth)
V = D ÷ k

In words: A share paying the same dividend forever is worth that dividend divided by the return you require.

  • Dfixed annual dividend
  • krequired return (as a decimal)
Return at market price
r = D ÷ P

In words: Buying at the market price, your return is the dividend divided by the price you pay.

  • Dannual dividend
  • Pmarket price
  • r vs kr > k means the share is undervalued
Valuation principle
IV > MP → undervalued → buy; IV < MP → overvalued → sell

In words: Buy a security when it is worth more than its price; sell when its price is above its worth.

  • IVintrinsic value from a valuation model
  • MPmarket price
  • (IV − MP)/MPmargin as a % of price
Solution
Intrinsic value = D / k = 11 / 0.11 = ₹100
Dividend yield at market price = 11 / 90 = 12.22%

IV ₹100 > MP ₹90 → undervalued (buy). Equivalently the 12.22% yield exceeds the 11% required return.

Q25NumericalFrom your PDF
A ₹1,000 face-value bond carries a 9% annual coupon and has 6 years remaining. Calculate its intrinsic value when the required return is 9% and again when it is 11%. Explain the effect of the change in required return.
Formula in words
Bond value (present value)
V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In words: A bond is worth today's value of all its coupons plus today's value of the face value it repays at maturity.

  • C × PVIFA(k, n)present value of n yearly coupons (annuity factor)
  • F × PVIF(k, n)present value of the single face-value repayment
  • krequired return / market yield: the discount rate (not the coupon rate)
  • nyears to maturity
Premium or discount
Coupon rate > k → premium; = k → par; < k → discount

In words: If the bond pays more interest than investors require, they pay above face value; if less, below face value.

  • PremiumV₀ − F when V₀ > F
  • DiscountF − V₀ when V₀ < F
Solution
At 9%: coupon = required return → value = face value = ₹1,000.00
C = 9% × ₹1,000 = ₹90.00
PVIFA(11%, 6) = 4.2305    PVIF(11%, 6) = 0.5346
PV of coupons   = 90.00 × 4.2305 = ₹380.75
PV of principal = 1,000 × 0.5346 = ₹534.64
Bond value V₀   = 380.75 + 534.64 = ₹915.39

₹1,000 at 9% (par) and ₹915.39 at 11% (discount). A higher required return lowers the present value of the fixed cash flows: the inverse price–yield relationship.

Q26NumericalFrom your PDF
An investor buys a bond for ₹960. Face value ₹1,000, annual coupon ₹80, 5 years remaining. Estimate YTM using the approximate formula and compare it with the coupon rate and current yield.
Formula in words
Approximate yield to maturity
YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n] ÷ [(F + P)/2]

In words: YTM is the yearly coupon plus the yearly share of the gain (or loss) to maturity, divided by the average of face value and price.

  • Cannual coupon
  • (F − P)/ngain per year if bought below face (negative if bought above)
  • (F + P)/2average amount invested over the life
Current yield
CY = Annual coupon ÷ Current market price

In words: Current yield is the yearly coupon as a percentage of the price you pay today.

  • Annual couponrupees of interest a year
  • Market pricewhat you pay now (not face value)
Coupon, current yield and YTM
Discount bond: coupon < CY < YTM; Premium bond: coupon > CY > YTM

In words: Below face value every yield is above the coupon rate, and YTM is highest because it adds the gain at maturity; above face value it is the reverse.

  • Coupon ratemeasured on face value
  • CYmeasured on market price
  • YTMincludes gain/loss to maturity and time value
Solution
YTM ≈ [80 + (1,000 − 960)/5] / [(1,000 + 960)/2] = (80 + 8) / 980 = 8.98%
Coupon rate = 80 / 1,000 = 8%
Current yield = 80 / 960 = 8.33%

Coupon 8% < CY 8.33% < YTM 8.98%: the ordering for a discount bond.

Q27NumericalFrom your PDF
A 5-year zero-coupon bond has face value ₹1,000 and a required return of 9%. Calculate its present value and Macaulay Duration. Explain the difference between price determination and duration for a zero-coupon bond.
Formula in words
Zero-coupon bond
P = F ÷ (1 + k)ⁿ, D = n

In words: A zero-coupon bond's price is just the face value discounted once; since all its money comes at maturity, its duration equals its maturity.

  • Fface value paid at the end
  • (1 + k)ⁿdiscounting for n years
  • D = nsingle payment → average time = maturity
Solution
P = 1,000 / (1.09)⁵ = 1,000 × 0.6499 = ₹649.93
D = 5 years (single cash flow at maturity)

Price ₹649.93; duration 5 years. Price depends on the discount rate (it falls as k rises); duration depends only on timing, and with one payment it equals maturity regardless of k.

Two bonds each have a Modified Duration of 4 years; Bond X is priced at ₹900 and Bond Y at ₹1,200. If yield rises by 1.5%, estimate the rupee price change for each and explain why the % change is the same while the rupee change differs.
Formula in words
Price change from a yield change
%ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy, ₹ΔP = %ΔP × P, New P = P + ₹ΔP

In words: The price changes by about minus modified duration times the change in yield; apply that % to the price for the rupee change.

  • −yield up → price down; yield down → price up

Q30NumericalFrom your PDF
A callable bond has face value ₹1,000, coupon 9%, current price ₹1,030, call price ₹1,015 and 4 years to the call date. Set up the YTC equation, estimate the yield to call and state the cash flows used.
Formula in words
Yield to call
P = C × PVIFA(YTC, n_c) + CP × PVIF(YTC, n_c); YTC ≈ [C + (CP − P)/n_c] ÷ [(CP + P)/2]

In words: Yield to call is the return if the issuer buys the bond back early: same as YTM but with the call price and the years to the call date.

  • CPcall price (replaces face value)
  • n_cyears to call date (replaces maturity)
  • Pcurrent market price
Solution
Cash flows: ₹90 at end of years 1–4 plus ₹1,015 (call price) at end of year 4
Equation: 1,030 = 90 × PVIFA(YTC, 4) + 1,015 × PVIF(YTC, 4)
Approx YTC = [90 + (1,015 − 1,030)/4] / [(1,015 + 1,030)/2] = 86.25 / 1,022.5 = 8.44%
Exact by trial ≈ 8.42%

YTC ≈ 8.44% (exact ≈ 8.42%).

Q33NumericalFrom your PDF
An investor purchases a ₹1,000 bond at ₹1,060. The annual coupon is ₹90 and 6 years remain. Calculate the current yield and explain how the premium price affects the relationship between coupon rate and current yield.
Formula in words
Current yield
CY = Annual coupon ÷ Current market price

In words: Current yield is the yearly coupon as a percentage of the price you pay today.

  • Annual couponrupees of interest a year
  • Market pricewhat you pay now (not face value)
Approximate yield to maturity
YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n] ÷ [(F + P)/2]

In words: YTM is the yearly coupon plus the yearly share of the gain (or loss) to maturity, divided by the average of face value and price.

  • Cannual coupon

  • Coupon ratemeasured on face value
  • CYmeasured on market price
  • YTMincludes gain/loss to maturity and time value

Q40NumericalFrom your PDF
A preference share of PQR Ltd. pays a fixed annual dividend of ₹12 indefinitely. The required return is 14%. Apply the zero-growth model to calculate its intrinsic value.

In words: A share paying the same dividend forever is worth that dividend divided by the return you require.

  • Dfixed annual dividend
  • krequired return (as a decimal)
Solution
V = D / k = 12 / 0.14 = ₹85.71

Intrinsic value = ₹85.71

Q41NumericalFrom your PDF
PQR Ltd. recently paid a dividend of ₹7 per share, growing at 8% a year. The investor initially requires 14%, but due to higher perceived risk the required return rises (printed as 14%). Calculate the intrinsic value under both rates and analyse the effect.
Formula in words
Constant growth DDM (Gordon)
P₀ = D₁ ÷ (k − g), D₁ = D₀ × (1 + g)

In words: Share value is next year's dividend divided by the required return minus the growth rate.

  • D₀dividend just paid / currently paid
  • D₁next year's dividend: grow D₀ first
  • k − grequired return minus growth (decimals; k must exceed g)
Solution
D₁ = 7 × 1.08 = ₹7.56
At 14%: P₀ = 7.56 / (0.14 − 0.08) = 7.56 / 0.06 = ₹126.00
If the second rate is 16% (illustration): 7.56 / 0.08 = ₹94.50

The question prints both rates as 14%, which looks like a typo. At 14% the value is ₹126 in both cases; any higher rate lowers the value (e.g., 16% gives ₹94.50).

₹126.00 at 14%. An increase in required return reduces intrinsic value, because future dividends are discounted more heavily.

Q42NumericalFrom your PDF
A ₹2,000 face-value bond carries a 9% annual coupon and has 5 years remaining. Calculate its intrinsic value at required returns of 9% and 11% and analyse the effect.
Formula in words
Bond value (present value)
V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In words: A bond is worth today's value of all its coupons plus today's value of the face value it repays at maturity.

  • C × PVIFA(k, n)present value of n yearly coupons (annuity factor)
  • F × PVIF(k, n)present value of the single face-value repayment
  • krequired return / market yield: the discount rate (not the coupon rate)
  • nyears to maturity
Premium or discount
Coupon rate > k → premium; = k → par; < k → discount

In words: If the bond pays more interest than investors require, they pay above face value; if less, below face value.

  • PremiumV₀ − F when V₀ > F
  • DiscountF − V₀ when V₀ < F
Solution
At 9%: coupon = required return → value = ₹2,000.00 (par)
C = 9% × ₹2,000 = ₹180.00
PVIFA(11%, 5) = 3.6959    PVIF(11%, 5) = 0.5935
PV of coupons   = 180.00 × 3.6959 = ₹665.26
PV of principal = 2,000 × 0.5935 = ₹1,186.90
Bond value V₀   = 665.26 + 1,186.90 = ₹1,852.16

₹2,000 at 9%; ₹1,852.16 at 11% (a fall of ₹147.84, 7.39%). Higher required return → lower bond value.

Q43NumericalFrom your PDF
Two bonds with ₹2,000 face value and 6 years remaining: Bond A 9% coupon, Bond B 6% coupon; market required return 10%. (a) Calculate the values at 10%. (b) Compare and explain why A trades at par while B trades at a discount. (c) Rates rise from 9% to 11%: analyse the impact on both. (d) Which bond declines more in % terms and why?
Formula in words
Bond value (present value)
V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In words: A bond is worth today's value of all its coupons plus today's value of the face value it repays at maturity.

  • C × PVIFA(k, n)present value of n yearly coupons (annuity factor)
  • F × PVIF(k, n)present value of the single face-value repayment
  • krequired return / market yield: the discount rate (not the coupon rate)
  • nyears to maturity
Premium or discount
Coupon rate > k → premium; = k → par; < k → discount

In words: If the bond pays more interest than investors require, they pay above face value; if less, below face value.

  • PremiumV₀ − F when V₀ > F
  • DiscountF − V₀ when V₀ < F
Solution
Bond A @10%: C = 9% × ₹2,000 = ₹180.00
PVIFA(10%, 6) = 4.3553    PVIF(10%, 6) = 0.5645
PV of coupons   = 180.00 × 4.3553 = ₹783.95
PV of principal = 2,000 × 0.5645 = ₹1,128.95
Bond value V₀   = 783.95 + 1,128.95 = ₹1,912.89
Bond B @10%: C = 6% × ₹2,000 = ₹120.00
PVIFA(10%, 6) = 4.3553    PVIF(10%, 6) = 0.5645
PV of coupons   = 120.00 × 4.3553 = ₹522.63
PV of principal = 2,000 × 0.5645 = ₹1,128.95
Bond value V₀   = 522.63 + 1,128.95 = ₹1,651.58
BondValue @ 9%Value @ 11%% change
A (9%)2,000.001,830.78−8.46
B (6%)1,730.841,576.95−8.89

At 10% Bond A is not at par (₹1,912.89, a discount); it is at par only at 9%, its own coupon rate. The question's part (b) appears to assume 9%.

(a) A ₹1,912.89, B ₹1,651.58. (b) Both trade below face at 10%; B's lower coupon makes its discount much larger. At 9%, A is exactly at par. (c) Both fall when rates rise. (d) B falls more (−8.89% vs −8.46%) because its lower coupon gives it a longer duration.

Q44NumericalFrom your PDF
Neha considers a preference share paying ₹10 a year indefinitely. Required return 10%; market price ₹95. (a) Intrinsic value? (b) Undervalued or overvalued? (c) Expected return at ₹95? (d) Interpret against the 10% required return.
Formula in words
Preference share value (zero growth)
V = D ÷ k

In words: A share paying the same dividend forever is worth that dividend divided by the return you require.

  • Dfixed annual dividend
  • krequired return (as a decimal)
Return at market price
r = D ÷ P

In words: Buying at the market price, your return is the dividend divided by the price you pay.

  • Dannual dividend
  • Pmarket price
  • r vs kr > k means the share is undervalued
Valuation principle
IV > MP → undervalued → buy; IV < MP → overvalued → sell

In words: Buy a security when it is worth more than its price; sell when its price is above its worth.

  • IVintrinsic value from a valuation model
  • MPmarket price
  • (IV − MP)/MPmargin as a % of price
Solution
(a) V = 10 / 0.10 = ₹100
(b) ₹100 > ₹95 → undervalued
(c) r = 10 / 95 = 10.53%

(d) 10.53% exceeds the required 10%, so buying at ₹95 is attractive.

Q45NumericalFrom your PDF
PQR Ltd. has EPS ₹6 and a P/E of 14; earnings expected to grow by 11% (part b says 10%) with the P/E unchanged. (a) Current price? (b) Forecast EPS? (c) Forecast price? (d) Effect of EPS growth on price with constant P/E?
Formula in words
P/E valuation
P = EPS × P/E; EPS₁ = EPS × (1 + g); P₁ = EPS₁ × P/E

In words: Price is earnings per share times the multiple the market pays; next year's price uses next year's EPS with the same multiple.

  • EPSearnings per share
  • P/Emultiple of comparable companies
  • (1 + g)grows EPS for next year
Solution
(a) P = 6 × 14 = ₹84
(b) EPS₁ at 10% = 6 × 1.10 = ₹6.60     [at 11% = ₹6.66]
(c) P₁ = 6.60 × 14 = ₹92.40           [at 11% = ₹93.24]

The stem says 11% growth and part (b) says 10%; both are shown.

(d) With a constant P/E, the share price grows at exactly the EPS growth rate.

Q46NumericalFrom your PDF
ST Limited's dividends grow at 7% a year; it recently paid ₹12. Required return 14%, later rising to 17%. (a) Value at 14%? (b) Value at 17%? (c) Explain the effect of higher required return.
Formula in words
Constant growth DDM (Gordon)
P₀ = D₁ ÷ (k − g), D₁ = D₀ × (1 + g)

In words: Share value is next year's dividend divided by the required return minus the growth rate.

  • D₀dividend just paid / currently paid
  • D₁next year's dividend: grow D₀ first
  • k − grequired return minus growth (decimals; k must exceed g)
Solution
D₁ = 12 × 1.07 = ₹12.84
(a) 12.84 / 0.07 = ₹183.43
(b) 12.84 / 0.10 = ₹128.40

(c) Value falls from ₹183.43 to ₹128.40 (−30%). A higher required return widens k − g, so the same dividends are worth less.

Q47NumericalFrom your PDF
A newly issued ₹2,000 bond of LMN Ltd. has a 6-year maturity and a 9% annual coupon. Required return for similar bonds is 11%. (a) Intrinsic value? (b) Par, premium or discount? (c) Explain using the price–yield relationship.
Formula in words
Bond value (present value)
V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In words: A bond is worth today's value of all its coupons plus today's value of the face value it repays at maturity.

  • C × PVIFA(k, n)present value of n yearly coupons (annuity factor)
  • F × PVIF(k, n)present value of the single face-value repayment
  • krequired return / market yield: the discount rate (not the coupon rate)
  • nyears to maturity
Premium or discount
Coupon rate > k → premium; = k → par; < k → discount

In words: If the bond pays more interest than investors require, they pay above face value; if less, below face value.

  • PremiumV₀ − F when V₀ > F
  • DiscountF − V₀ when V₀ < F
Solution
C = 9% × ₹2,000 = ₹180.00
PVIFA(11%, 6) = 4.2305    PVIF(11%, 6) = 0.5346
PV of coupons   = 180.00 × 4.2305 = ₹761.50
PV of principal = 2,000 × 0.5346 = ₹1,069.28
Bond value V₀   = 761.50 + 1,069.28 = ₹1,830.78

Value ₹1,830.78 < face ₹2,000 → discount of ₹169.22, because the coupon rate (9%) is below the required return (11%). (c) Price–yield relationship: when the required yield (11%) is above the coupon (9%), the bond must be priced below face value so that buyers earn 11%.

Theory practice (19)

T12-markPractice · from notes
What is intrinsic value?

The present value of all expected future cash flows from a security, discounted at the investor's required rate of return.

T22-markPractice · from notes
When does a bond sell at a premium?

When its coupon rate exceeds the required rate of return (market yield), its present value exceeds face value.

T32-markPractice · from notes
Define current yield.

Annual coupon divided by the current market price of the bond; it measures income return only.

T42-markPractice · from notes
Define yield to maturity.

The discount rate that equates the present value of a bond's coupons and redemption value with its current market price; the bond's IRR if held to maturity.

T52-markPractice · from notes
What is yield to call?

The return earned if a callable bond is held until the call date and redeemed at the call price.

T62-markPractice · from notes
What is Macaulay duration?

The weighted-average time to receipt of a bond's cash flows, weighted by the present value of each cash flow relative to the bond price.

T72-markPractice · from notes
What is modified duration?

Macaulay duration divided by (1 + YTM); it gives the approximate % change in bond price for a 1 percentage-point change in yield.

T82-markPractice · from notes
Why is a zero-coupon bond's duration equal to its maturity?

Because it has only one cash flow, received at maturity, so the weighted-average time of cash flows equals the maturity.

T92-markPractice · from notes
State the price–yield relationship.

Bond prices move inversely to yields: when market yields rise, bond prices fall, and vice versa.

T102-markPractice · from notes
State the Gordon growth model.

P₀ = D₁/(k − g): share value equals next year's dividend divided by the required return minus the constant growth rate (k > g).

T112-markPractice · from notes
How is a preference share valued?

Under the zero-growth model, V = D/kp, the fixed perpetual dividend divided by the required return.

T122-markPractice · from notes
What is the P/E approach?

Value = EPS × appropriate P/E ratio, typically the multiple of comparable firms in the industry.

T135-markPractice · from notes
Explain bond valuation and the conditions for par, premium and discount.
Definition

The value of a bond is the present value of its coupons and face value discounted at the required return.

Formula

V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n).

Conditions
  • Coupon rate = k → V₀ = F (par).
  • Coupon rate > k → V₀ > F (premium).
  • Coupon rate < k → V₀ < F (discount).
Example

Q14: 12% coupon, k = 10%, 8 years → V₀ = 120(5.3349) + 1,000(0.4665) = ₹1,106.70, a premium of ₹106.70 because the coupon exceeds the required return.

T145-markPractice · from notes
Explain the different measures of bond yield.
Coupon rate

Annual interest as a % of face value.

Current yield

C ÷ market price; ignores capital gain/loss.

Yield to maturity

IRR if held to maturity; approx. [C + (F − P)/n]/[(F + P)/2].

Yield to call

IRR to the call date at the call price.

Relationship

Discount bond: coupon < CY < YTM. Premium bond: coupon > CY > YTM. Par: all equal.

Example

Q16: coupon 8%, CY 8.42%, YTM 9.23%.

T155-markPractice · from notes
What is duration? Explain Macaulay and modified duration with their uses.
Meaning

Duration measures the effective life of a bond and its sensitivity to interest rates.

Macaulay duration

D = Σ t × PV(CFt)/P; weighted-average time of cash flows in years.

Modified duration

MD = D/(1 + y); %ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy.

Properties
  • Zero-coupon: D = n; coupon bonds: D < n.
  • Lower coupon and longer maturity → higher duration.
Example

Q19: D = 6, y = 10% → MD = 5.45; a 2% rise in yield → −10.91% (₹1,050 → ₹935.45).

T165-markPractice · from notes
Explain the constant growth dividend discount model and the effect of required return on value.
Model

P₀ = D₁/(k − g), D₁ = D₀(1 + g).

Assumptions
  • Dividends grow at a constant rate forever.
  • k > g and both are constant.
Effect of k

Value is inversely related to k; since k − g is small, small changes in k cause large value changes.

Example

Q46: D₀ = 12, g = 7%: at 14% value ₹183.43; at 17% value ₹128.40, a 30% fall.

T175-markPractice · from notes
Explain the P/E ratio approach to equity valuation.
Meaning

A relative valuation method: value = EPS × appropriate P/E multiple.

Steps
  • Estimate EPS (current or forecast).
  • Select P/E of comparable firms/industry.
  • Multiply; for next year use EPS₁ = EPS(1 + g).
Example

Q45: EPS 6, P/E 14 → ₹84; EPS₁ at 10% = 6.60 → ₹92.40.

Limitations

Depends on choice of comparables; earnings may be volatile; constant P/E is an assumption.

T18Long answerPractice · from notes
Discuss the valuation of bonds. Explain bond yields, the price–yield relationship and duration with illustrations.
Introduction

A bond is a fixed-income security whose value depends on its promised cash flows and the market's required return. Valuation helps investors decide whether a bond is fairly priced and how it will react to interest-rate changes.

Bond valuation

V₀ = Σ C/(1 + k)t + F/(1 + k)n = C × PVIFA + F × PVIF. Example (Q1): F = ₹1,000, coupon 8%, 5 years, k = 10% → V₀ = 303.26 + 620.92 = ₹924.18 (discount).

Par, premium and discount

Coupon = k → par; coupon > k → premium; coupon < k → discount.

Yields
  • Current yield = C/P.
  • YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n]/[(F + P)/2]; exact YTM solves P = Σ C/(1 + y)t + F/(1 + y)n.
  • YTC uses call price and years to call.
  • Discount bond: coupon < CY < YTM; premium: coupon > CY > YTM.
Price–yield relationship

Prices move inversely to yields (Q42: ₹2,000 bond worth ₹2,000 at 9% but ₹1,852.16 at 11%). Lower coupon and longer maturity bonds are more price-sensitive.

Duration

Macaulay D = Σ t·PV/P (Q18: 3.56 years for a 4-year 8% bond at 10%). Zero-coupon D = n. Modified duration MD = D/(1 + y); %ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy (Q8: MD 6, Δy +0.75% → −4.5%, ₹1,200 → ₹1,146).

Uses
  • Identifying mispriced bonds.
  • Comparing yields across bonds.
  • Measuring and managing interest-rate risk.
Limitations
  • Duration is a linear approximation, accurate only for small yield changes.
  • Assumes coupons are reinvested at the YTM.
  • Approximate YTM is not exact.
Conclusion

Bond value, yields and duration together tell an investor what a bond is worth, what it will return and how much its price can move when interest rates change.

T19Long answerPractice · from notes
Explain the valuation of preference and equity shares using the dividend discount model and the P/E approach. Discuss the role of intrinsic value in investment decisions.
Introduction

Share valuation estimates the intrinsic value of a share so that it can be compared with the market price.

Preference shares (zero-growth)

V = D/kp. Example (Q40): ₹12/0.14 = ₹85.71. Expected return at market price = D/P (Q44: 10/95 = 10.53%).

Equity shares: constant growth DDM

P₀ = D₁/(k − g), D₁ = D₀(1 + g). Assumptions: constant growth forever, k > g. Example (Q37): D₀ = 12, g = 8%, k = 14% → D₁ = 12.96 → ₹216.

Equity shares: P/E approach

P = EPS × P/E; forecast P₁ = EPS(1 + g) × P/E. Example (Q38): 6 × 14 = ₹84.

Role of intrinsic value
  • IV > MP → undervalued → buy (Q32: IV 240, MP 210).
  • IV < MP → overvalued → sell.
  • Higher required return lowers IV (Q46: ₹183.43 → ₹128.40).
Advantages

Grounded in cash flows and earnings; provides a disciplined, objective basis for decisions.

Limitations

Sensitive to estimates of k and g; DDM unsuitable for non-dividend or erratic-growth firms; P/E depends on choice of comparables.

Conclusion

Valuation models translate expected dividends and earnings into intrinsic value; comparing it with market price is the core of fundamental investment decisions (linking to Unit 4).

Fundamental & Technical Analysis

Numerical & case practice (11)

N1NumericalPractice · from notes
Over a 14-day period a stock's average gain on up days is ₹1.80 and average loss on down days is ₹0.60. Calculate RS and RSI and interpret.
Formula in words
Relative Strength Index
RS = Average gain ÷ Average loss; RSI = 100 − [100 ÷ (1 + RS)]

In words: First divide the average gain by the average loss; then RSI is 100 minus 100 divided by one plus that ratio.

  • RShow strong the up-moves are compared with the down-moves
  • 100 ÷ (1 + RS)subtract this from 100 (don't stop here)
  • > 70 / < 30overbought / oversold
Solution
RS = 1.80 / 0.60 = 3.00
RSI = 100 − [100 / (1 + 3)] = 100 − 25 = 75

RSI = 75 > 70 → overbought: caution / potential sell or downward reversal.

N2NumericalPractice · from notes
A stock's 14-day average gain is ₹0.50 and average loss ₹1.35. Calculate RSI and interpret.
Formula in words
Relative Strength Index
RS = Average gain ÷ Average loss; RSI = 100 − [100 ÷ (1 + RS)]

In words: First divide the average gain by the average loss; then RSI is 100 minus 100 divided by one plus that ratio.

  • RShow strong the up-moves are compared with the down-moves
  • 100 ÷ (1 + RS)subtract this from 100 (don't stop here)
  • > 70 / < 30overbought / oversold
Solution
RS = 0.50 / 1.35 = 0.37
RSI = 100 − [100 / 1.37] = 100 − 73.0 = 27.0

RSI = 27 < 30 → oversold: caution / potential buy or upward reversal.

N3CasePractice · from notes
Maya sees ABC Ltd.'s 14-day RSI rise to 74 during a rapid rally, then fall to 27 after a sharp decline. Interpret both readings and the signal she should watch for.
Formula in words
Relative Strength Index
RS = Average gain ÷ Average loss; RSI = 100 − [100 ÷ (1 + RS)]

In words: First divide the average gain by the average loss; then RSI is 100 minus 100 divided by one plus that ratio.

  • RShow strong the up-moves are compared with the down-moves
  • 100 ÷ (1 + RS)subtract this from 100 (don't stop here)
  • > 70 / < 30overbought / oversold
Solution

RSI 74: overbought; the rally may have outpaced fundamentals, so a correction or consolidation is likely (cautionary sell signal). RSI 27: oversold; the decline may be excessive and a rebound is possible (cautionary buy signal). The swing from one extreme to the other means she should watch for RSI turning up from below 30 (bullish) or down from above 70 (bearish), confirmed by price action, volume or a moving-average crossover, before acting.

N4NumericalPractice · from notes
Closing prices over five days are ₹100, 102, 101, 105, 107. Calculate the 5-day SMA and WMA. On day 6 the price is ₹110: calculate the new SMA and the EMA (n = 5, seeded with the SMA). (Illustrative data.)
Formula in words
Simple moving average
SMA = Sum of last n closing prices ÷ n

In words: Add the last n closing prices and divide by n; every day counts equally.

  • nnumber of days in the window
  • slidenext day drop the oldest price and add the newest
Weighted moving average
WMA = Σ(Price × Weight) ÷ Σ Weights

In words: Multiply each price by its weight (newest gets the biggest weight), add them, and divide by the total of the weights.

  • Weights1 for the oldest … n for the newest
  • Σ Weights1 + 2 + … + n
Exponential moving average
EMA = Price × k + Previous EMA × (1 − k), k = 2 ÷ (n + 1)

In words: Today's EMA takes a slice k of today's price and the rest from yesterday's EMA.

  • ksmoothing constant = 2/(n + 1)
  • Previous EMAstart with the SMA if no EMA exists
Solution
SMA(5) = (100 + 102 + 101 + 105 + 107)/5 = 515/5 = 103.00
WMA(5) = (100×1 + 102×2 + 101×3 + 105×4 + 107×5) / (1+2+3+4+5)
       = (100 + 204 + 303 + 420 + 535) / 15 = 1,562 / 15 = 104.13
Day 6 SMA = (102 + 101 + 105 + 107 + 110)/5 = 525/5 = 105.00
k = 2/(5 + 1) = 0.3333
EMA = 110 × 0.3333 + 103 × 0.6667 = 36.67 + 68.67 = 105.33

SMA 103, WMA 104.13; day 6: SMA 105.00, EMA 105.33. The EMA reacts most to the jump, the SMA least.

N5NumericalPractice · from notes
A stock's 50-day moving average rises from 480 to 505 while its 200-day average is 500. Identify the signal. (Illustrative.)
Formula in words
Moving-average crossover
Short MA crosses above long MA → golden cross (buy); below → death cross (sell)

In words: When the faster average climbs over the slower one the trend is turning up; when it drops under, the trend is turning down.

  • Short MAe.g. 50-day
  • Long MAe.g. 200-day
Solution
Before: 50-day (480) < 200-day (500)
After:  50-day (505) > 200-day (500)  → shorter average crossed ABOVE the longer

Golden cross: a bullish (buy) signal. Confirm with volume and check that RSI is not already overbought.

N6NumericalPractice · from notes
Average yield on high-grade bonds is 7.2%; the average yield on lower-grade bonds falls from 8.0% to 7.6%. Calculate the Confidence Index before and after and interpret. (Illustrative.)
Formula in words
Confidence Index
CI = Average yield on high-grade bonds ÷ Average yield on lower-grade bonds

In words: Divide the yield on safe bonds by the yield on riskier bonds; a rising ratio means investors are more confident.

  • High-grade yieldlower, safe-bond yield
  • Low-grade yieldhigher, risky-bond yield
  • Rising CIgap narrowing → bullish
Solution
CI before = 7.2 / 8.0 = 0.900
CI after  = 7.2 / 7.6 = 0.947

The CI is rising (yield gap narrowing): investors are more confident and accept less extra yield for risk, a bullish signal.

N7NumericalPractice · from notes
A stock is at ₹200 when the index is 20,000, and at ₹210 when the index is 20,500. Calculate the relative strength ratio and interpret. (Illustrative.)
Formula in words
Relative strength ratio
RS ratio = Price of security ÷ Price of index

In words: Divide the stock's price by the index on each date; a rising ratio means the stock is beating the market.

  • Trendwhat matters is whether the ratio is rising or falling
Solution
RS ratio = Price ÷ Index
Before: 200 / 20,000 = 0.01000
After:  210 / 20,500 = 0.01024

The ratio is rising: the stock is outperforming the index (gaining relative strength), a basis for momentum selection.

N8NumericalPractice · from notes
A fixed deposit pays 7% while inflation is 6%. What is the real return? What happens if inflation rises to 8%?
Formula in words
Real return
Real return = Nominal return − Inflation rate

In words: Your real gain is the stated return minus the rise in prices.

  • Nominalmoney return
  • Inflationloss of purchasing power
Solution
Real return = Nominal − Inflation = 7 − 6 = 1%
At 8% inflation: 7 − 8 = −1%

1% real return; at 8% inflation the real return turns negative and purchasing power falls.

N9NumericalPractice · from notes
On day 1, 1,200 stocks advance and 800 decline; on day 2, 900 advance and 1,100 decline; on day 3, 1,300 advance and 700 decline. Build the A/D line. (Illustrative.)
Formula in words
Advance–Decline line
A/D today = A/D yesterday + (Advances − Declines)

In words: Each day add the number of rising stocks minus falling stocks to a running total.

  • Advances − Declinesnet breadth for the day
  • running totalthe A/D line
Solution
Day 1: +400 → A/D = 400
Day 2: −200 → A/D = 200
Day 3: +600 → A/D = 800

The A/D line ends higher, so the advance is broad-based; if the index rose while the A/D line fell, the rally would be narrow.

N10CasePractice · from notes
An investor is considering a company in a rapidly expanding industry. The economy shows rising interest rates and inflation. The company has strong profitability and competitive advantage, but its technical indicators show an overbought condition. Give a structured analysis and conclusion.
Formula in words
Top-down analysis
Economy → Industry → Company → Technical timing → Decision

In words: Judge the economy, then the industry's stage, then the company's strength; use technical signals only to decide when to act.

  • FAwhat to buy
  • TAwhen to buy
Relative Strength Index
RS = Average gain ÷ Average loss; RSI = 100 − [100 ÷ (1 + RS)]

In words: First divide the average gain by the average loss; then RSI is 100 minus 100 divided by one plus that ratio.

  • RShow strong the up-moves are compared with the down-moves
  • 100 ÷ (1 + RS)subtract this from 100 (don't stop here)
  • > 70 / < 30overbought / oversold
Solution
a. Economy: rising inflation/rates → higher discount rate and borrowing cost → negative for valuations
b. Industry: Expansion stage → strong growth, above-average competitive and execution risk
c. Company: strong profitability (quantitative) + competitive advantage (qualitative)
d. Effect of rates/inflation: margin pressure, higher cost of capital and debt cost; pricing power helps
e. Technical: RSI > 70 overbought → caution on entry timing, not on quality
f. RSI + MAs: wait for pull-back; MA crossover confirms whether the primary trend is intact
g. Use both: FA decides WHAT to buy, TA decides WHEN

h. The company is a fundamentally attractive long-term investment, but given the overbought condition and tighter macro backdrop, the investor should buy in phases or wait for a technical correction.

N11NumericalPractice · from notes
Using a box size of ₹1 and a 3-box reversal, how would a P&F chart record prices moving 100 → 104 → 102 → 106? (Illustrative.)
Formula in words
Point & figure rule
Plot an X per box rise; start an O column only after a reversal of ≥ 3 boxes

In words: Only meaningful moves are drawn: rises add X's, and the chart switches to O's only when the price reverses by the reversal amount.

  • Box sizeminimum move to plot
  • Reversalusually 3 boxes
Solution
100 → 104: X column at 101, 102, 103, 104
104 → 102: fall of 2 boxes < 3-box reversal → not recorded
102 → 106: continue X column at 105, 106

One unbroken X column from 101 to 106: a strong uptrend, with the minor dip filtered out as noise.

Theory practice (23)

T12-markPractice · from notes
Define fundamental analysis.

The study of economic, industry and company factors that affect a security's intrinsic value, in order to estimate its true worth and compare it with the market price.

T22-markPractice · from notes
What is the E-I-C framework?

A top-down approach to fundamental analysis: Economy analysis → Industry analysis → Company analysis.

T32-markPractice · from notes
State the stages of the industry life cycle.

Pioneering (introduction), Expansion (growth), Stabilization (maturity) and Decay (decline).

T42-markPractice · from notes
What is technical analysis?

The study of past price movements, trading volume and chart patterns to forecast future prices, without reference to intrinsic value.

T52-markPractice · from notes
State the assumptions of technical analysis.

Market price discounts everything; prices move in trends; history tends to repeat itself.

T62-markPractice · from notes
What are the three trends in Dow Theory?

Primary trend (a year or more), secondary/intermediate trend (corrections lasting weeks to months) and minor/daily trends.

T72-markPractice · from notes
What is RSI?

A momentum oscillator, RSI = 100 − 100/(1 + RS), where RS = average gain/average loss; above 70 = overbought, below 30 = oversold.

T82-markPractice · from notes
What is a golden cross?

When a shorter-period moving average (e.g., 50-day) crosses above a longer-period average (e.g., 200-day); a bullish signal. The opposite is a death cross.

T92-markPractice · from notes
What is the Confidence Index?

The ratio of average yield on high-grade bonds to that on lower-grade bonds; a rising index is bullish, a falling index bearish.

T102-markPractice · from notes
What is the theory of contrary opinion?

When the majority becomes extremely bullish or bearish, the market is near a turning point, so contrarians take the opposite position.

T112-markPractice · from notes
What is a point and figure chart?

A chart using columns of X's (rising) and O's (falling) that records only significant price changes and ignores time.

T122-markPractice · from notes
What is algorithmic trading?

The use of computer programs to execute trades automatically according to pre-defined rules without human intervention at execution.

T132-markPractice · from notes
Define chaos theory in markets.

The view that apparently random price movements may follow deterministic non-linear rules highly sensitive to initial conditions (the butterfly effect).

T145-markPractice · from notes
Explain the E-I-C framework of fundamental analysis.
Meaning

A three-stage top-down approach to estimating intrinsic value.

Stages
  • Economy: GDP, monetary and fiscal policy, inflation, interest rates, exchange rates, crude oil, employment, foreign flows, business cycle. Key question: is the climate favourable?
  • Industry: growth, competition, profitability, life-cycle stage, policy, technology. Key question: will the industry grow and remain profitable?
  • Company: ratio analysis plus management, governance, moat, strategy. Key question: is this company sound within its industry?
Logic

The economy shapes industries and industries shape companies; the framework filters the universe and avoids good companies in bad industries or weak economies.

T155-markPractice · from notes
Explain the industry life cycle and its investment implications.
Introduction

Industries, like products, pass through four stages.

StageCharacteristicsImplication
PioneeringRapid but uncertain growth, high failure risk, heavy R&D, few unprofitable playersHigh risk, high potential; risk-seeking, long horizon
ExpansionRising sales and profits, new entrants, consolidationGrowth investors; capital appreciation
StabilizationGrowth = economy, stable shares, steady profits and dividendsIncome/conservative investors
DecayShrinking demand, obsolescence, exitsAvoid, except turnarounds

Growth-stage companies command higher P/E multiples; mature companies are valued on dividend yield.

T165-markPractice · from notes
Explain Dow Theory and its relevance today.
Meaning

Developed by Charles Dow; foundation of technical analysis.

Principles
  • Three trends: primary, secondary, minor.
  • Market averages must confirm each other.
  • A trend continues until a definite reversal signal.
  • Volume confirms the trend.
  • Averages discount everything.
Relevance

Trend classification, confirmation and persistence remain the conceptual basis of modern chart and indicator analysis.

T175-markPractice · from notes
Explain RSI and its interpretation.
Meaning

A momentum oscillator (0–100), usually over 14 days.

Formula

RS = average gain/average loss; RSI = 100 − 100/(1 + RS).

Interpretation
  • >70: overbought; potential sell/reversal down.
  • <30: oversold; potential buy/reversal up.
  • 30–70: neutral.
Example

Gain 1.80, loss 0.60 → RS 3 → RSI 75 (overbought). Gain 0.50, loss 1.35 → RSI 27 (oversold).

Use

Confirm with price action, volume or MA crossover.

T185-markPractice · from notes
Compare SMA, WMA and EMA.
Formulas

SMA = ΣP/n; WMA = Σ(P·w)/Σw; EMA = P·k + EMAy(1 − k), k = 2/(n + 1).

FeatureSMAWMAEMA
WeightingEqualLinearly increasingExponentially increasing
ResponsivenessSlowestModerateFastest
LagHighestModerateLowest
Old dataDrops out abruptlyDrops out, low weightNever fully drops; decays
Signals

Price/MA crossovers; golden and death crosses; dynamic support/resistance.

T195-markPractice · from notes
Discuss algorithmic trading: features, advantages and risks.
Meaning

Automated execution of trades under pre-defined rules.

Features

Speed, rule-based, multi-market, backtestable, includes HFT.

Applications

Trend-following, arbitrage, market making, rebalancing, slicing large orders.

Advantages

Efficient execution, no emotional errors, rigorous backtesting, lower costs, consistency.

Limitations and risks

Infrastructure and expertise needed; model risk; reduced human judgement; system failures; flash crashes; systemic and regulatory risk.

T205-markPractice · from notes
Explain chart patterns.
Meaning

Recurring price formations used to anticipate trend continuation or reversal.

Reversal patterns

Head and shoulders (top) and inverse (bottom); double top/bottom; triple top/bottom (stronger); rounding top/bottom.

Continuation patterns

Triangles (symmetrical, ascending, descending), flags and pennants, rectangles; wedges can be either.

Confirmation

Decisive breakout beyond the pattern boundary, ideally on higher volume.

T21Long answerPractice · from notes
Explain fundamental analysis in detail with reference to economy, industry and company analysis.
Introduction

Fundamental analysis seeks the intrinsic value of a security so that investors can buy undervalued and sell overvalued securities, supporting long-term decisions rather than speculation.

Definition

The study of all factors affecting a security's intrinsic value (economic conditions, industry characteristics, and company-specific financial and qualitative factors) to compare its true worth with market price.

Objectives
  • Estimate intrinsic value; identify mispriced securities.
  • Assess long-term earning capacity, soundness and growth.
  • Understand macro and industry impacts on company performance.
Economy analysis

GDP (rising growth bullish); monetary policy (tight: rates up, prices down; easy: supportive); inflation (Real return = Nominal − Inflation; high inflation hurts margins and prompts rate hikes); interest rates (discount-rate and earnings effects; rate-sensitive sectors: banking, real estate, autos); exchange rates (weak rupee helps exporters like IT and pharma); crude oil (India imports; rising crude hurts aviation, paints, tyres); fiscal policy, employment, FDI/FPI, international influences; business cycle phases.

Industry analysis

Growth and demand, competitive structure (Porter's Five Forces), profitability, life-cycle stage (pioneering, expansion, stabilization, decay), government policy (industrial, tax, trade, regulation, subsidies, FDI), technology, inputs, cyclical vs defensive.

Company analysis

Quantitative: profitability (margins, ROE, ROA, EPS), liquidity (current, quick), solvency (D/E, interest coverage), efficiency (turnover ratios). Qualitative: management, governance, image, moat, strategy, statutory controls, industry stage.

Decision rule

Buy if IV > MP; sell if IV < MP (valuation models from Unit 3).

Advantages

Evidence-based; suits long-term wealth creation; avoids unsound companies.

Limitations

Time-consuming; relies on uncertain estimates; price may take long to converge; less useful for short-term trading.

Conclusion

The E-I-C framework systematically narrows the investment universe to sound companies in growing industries within a supportive economy.

T22Long answerPractice · from notes
Explain technical analysis: assumptions, Dow Theory, charts, patterns and indicators. Compare it with fundamental analysis.
Introduction

Technical analysis forecasts prices from past price and volume data, believing all information is reflected in price.

Assumptions
  1. Market price discounts everything.
  2. Prices move in trends.
  3. History repeats itself.
Dow Theory

Primary, secondary and minor trends; averages must confirm; trends persist until reversed; volume confirms; averages discount everything.

Charts

Line, bar, point & figure (X's and O's, no time axis, box size and 3-box reversal), Japanese candlestick (body and wicks; Doji, Hammer, Engulfing).

Patterns

Reversal: head and shoulders, double/triple tops and bottoms, rounding. Continuation: triangles, flags, pennants, rectangles; wedges either.

Indicators
  • Contrary opinion; Confidence Index.
  • Relative Strength Analysis: Stock ÷ Index.
  • RSI = 100 − 100/(1 + RS); 70/30 thresholds.
  • SMA, WMA, EMA (k = 2/(n + 1)); golden/death cross.
  • Breadth: A/D line, new highs–lows, volume, put–call ratio.
Comparison with fundamental analysis
BasisFundamentalTechnical
FocusIntrinsic valuePrice trends, patterns, volume
HorizonMedium to longShort to medium
DataFinancials, economy, industryPrices, volume, indicators
DecisionIV vs MPPatterns, signals
BeliefPrice converges to valueHistory repeats; trends persist
Advantages of TA

Timing; quick to apply to many securities; captures sentiment.

Limitations of TA

Ignores fundamentals; false signals in sideways markets; history may not repeat.

Conclusion

For long-term investors fundamental analysis is primary, but technical analysis is valuable for timing; used together, FA decides what to buy and TA when.

T23Long answerPractice · from notes
Discuss the role of algorithmic trading and artificial intelligence (neural networks, chaos theory) in investment analysis, with their limitations.
Introduction

Technology has extended technical analysis from manual charting to automated, data-driven decision-making.

Algorithmic trading

Computer programs execute trades on pre-defined rules. Features: speed, rule-based, multi-market, backtestable, HFT. Applications: trend-following, arbitrage, market making, rebalancing, order slicing. Advantages: efficiency, no emotional errors, lower costs, consistency.

Artificial intelligence

Models that learn and adapt: market prediction (prices, news, social sentiment), pattern recognition, robo-advisory portfolio management, automated decisions, risk management and fraud detection.

Neural networks

Layered interconnected nodes that learn non-linear relationships from historical data and generate probability-based forecasts; data-driven.

Chaos theory

Markets as deterministic non-linear systems sensitive to initial conditions; seeks hidden rules behind apparently random moves; theory-driven.

Neural networks vs chaos theory

Both seek structure in complex data; neural networks infer patterns statistically, chaos theory seeks the underlying mathematical rules. Both complement FA and TA.

Limitations and risks
  • Overfitting.
  • Black-box opacity.
  • Data quality and bias.
  • Regime change (crises not in training data).
  • Over-reliance and herding; flash crashes; system failures; systemic/regulatory risk.
Conclusion

Algo trading and AI improve speed and pattern detection, but they require human oversight and should complement, not replace, sound fundamental and technical judgement.

Questions from your PDF (33)

Q1NumericalFrom your PDF
A portfolio has an expected return of 15%, standard deviation of 9% and risk-free rate of 6%. Calculate the Return to Variability Ratio (Sharpe Ratio) and interpret the result.
Formula in words
Sharpe ratio (Return to Variability)
S = (R_p − R_f) / σ_p

In words: Sharpe ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by total risk (standard deviation).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return (reward for risk)
  • σ_ptotal risk
  • Sexcess return per 1% of total risk; higher is better
Solution
Sharpe = (R_p − R_f) / σ_p = (15 − 6) / 9 = 9 / 9 = 1.000

Sharpe = 1.00: the portfolio earns 1 percentage point of excess return for every 1% of total risk.

Q2NumericalFrom your PDF
A portfolio earns 17%, has a beta of 1.25 and the risk-free rate is 6%. Calculate the Return to Volatility Ratio (Treynor Ratio) and explain its meaning.
Formula in words
Treynor ratio (Return to Volatility)
T = (R_p − R_f) ÷ β_p

In words: Treynor ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by beta (market risk).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return
  • β_psystematic risk
  • Market benchmarkR_m − R_f, because the market's β = 1
Solution
Treynor = (R_p − R_f) / β_p = (17 − 6) / 1.25 = 11 / 1.25 = 8.800

Treynor = 8.8: 8.8% excess return per unit of systematic risk (beta). Compare with the market's Rm − Rf to judge performance.

Q3NumericalFrom your PDF
A security has a beta of 1.40, risk-free rate of 5% and market return of 13%. Calculate the required return using CAPM and interpret.
Formula in words
CAPM required return
E(R) = R_f + β(R_m − R_f)

In words: Required return equals the risk-free rate plus beta times the market's extra return over the risk-free rate.

  • R_freward for waiting (risk-free rate)
  • R_m − R_fmarket risk premium: extra return for bearing market risk
  • β ×scaled up or down for this stock's market risk
Solution
Required = R_f + β(R_m − R_f) = 5 + 1.40 × (13 − 5) = 5 + 11.20 = 16.20%

Required return = 16.20%. Because β > 1 the stock is aggressive, so investors demand more than the market's 13%.

Q4NumericalFrom your PDF
Two securities have expected returns of 14% and 18%, with portfolio weights of 40% and 60%. Calculate the expected portfolio return and explain how the weights influence the return.
Formula in words
Expected portfolio return
R_p = Σ wᵢRᵢ

In words: Portfolio return is each security's return times its weight, added together.

  • wᵢweight (decimal)
  • Rᵢexpected return of the security
  • wᵢRᵢthat security's contribution
Solution
R_p = 0.40 × 14 + 0.60 × 18 = 5.6 + 10.8 = 16.4%

Rp = 16.4%. The heavier 60% weight on the 18% security pulls the result above the simple average of 16%.

Q5NumericalFrom your PDF
A portfolio has an opening NAV of ₹25, closing NAV of ₹28 and distributes ₹1.20 per unit. Calculate the return using the NAV approach.
Formula in words
NAV-based return
R = (Closing NAV − Opening NAV + Distribution) ÷ Opening NAV

In words: A fund's return is the rise in NAV plus the payout per unit, divided by the starting NAV.

  • Closing − Opening NAVchange in unit value
  • Distributioncash paid out per unit
  • ÷ Opening NAVbase for the %
Solution
R = (NAV₁ − NAV₀ + D) / NAV₀ = (28 − 25 + 1.20) / 25 = 4.20 / 25 = 0.168

Return = 16.8%

Q6NumericalFrom your PDF
An investor invests ₹4,00,000 in Security A and ₹6,00,000 in Security B, with expected returns of 12% and 17%. Calculate the expected portfolio return and analyse each security's contribution.
Formula in words
Portfolio weights
wᵢ = Amount in i ÷ Total investment

In words: Each weight is the money in that security divided by the total money invested.

  • Σ wweights must add to 1
Expected portfolio return
R_p = Σ wᵢRᵢ

In words: Portfolio return is each security's return times its weight, added together.

  • wᵢweight (decimal)
  • Rᵢexpected return of the security
  • wᵢRᵢthat security's contribution
Solution
Total = ₹10,00,000 → w_A = 0.40, w_B = 0.60
Contribution A = 0.40 × 12 = 4.8%
Contribution B = 0.60 × 17 = 10.2%
R_p = 4.8 + 10.2 = 15.0%

Rp = 15%. B contributes 10.2 of the 15 points (68%) because it has both the larger weight and the higher return.

Q7NumericalFrom your PDF
A security has an expected return of 16%, beta 1.10, risk-free rate 6% and market return 14%. Calculate the CAPM required return and determine whether it appears underpriced or overpriced.
Formula in words
CAPM required return
E(R) = R_f + β(R_m − R_f)

In words: Required return equals the risk-free rate plus beta times the market's extra return over the risk-free rate.

  • R_freward for waiting (risk-free rate)
  • R_m − R_fmarket risk premium: extra return for bearing market risk
  • β ×scaled up or down for this stock's market risk
Undervalued or overvalued
α = Expected return − Required (CAPM) return

In words: If the stock is expected to earn more than CAPM says it should, it is undervalued; if less, it is overvalued.

  • Expectedwhat the stock is forecast to earn
  • RequiredCAPM return for its beta
  • α > 0undervalued, above the SML → buy
  • α < 0overvalued, below the SML → avoid/sell
Solution
Required = R_f + β(R_m − R_f) = 6 + 1.10 × (14 − 6) = 6 + 8.80 = 14.80%
α = Expected − Required = 16.00 − 14.80 = 1.20%

Required 14.80% < expected 16% → positive alpha (+1.2%) → underpriced (above the SML): buy.

A16%10%6%

  • σ_ptotal risk
  • Sexcess return per 1% of total risk; higher is better

A: (16 − 6) / 10 = 1.000

Portfolio A (Sharpe 1.0) is better on a risk-adjusted basis even though B has a higher return.

Q17NumericalFrom your PDF
Security P (18%, 25% weight), Q (12%, 45%) and R (9%, remaining 30%). Calculate the expected return of the portfolio and analyse contributions.
Formula in words
Expected portfolio return
R_p = Σ wᵢRᵢ

  • Rᵢexpected return of the security
  • wᵢRᵢthat security's contribution

Q: 0.45 × 12 = 5.4
R: 0.30 × 9 = 2.7
R_p = 12.6%

Rp = 12.6%. Q contributes most (5.4) because of its large weight, even though P has the highest return; R pulls the portfolio down.

Q18NumericalFrom your PDF
A security has a beta of 1.6. Risk-free rate 7%, expected market return 12%. Using CAPM, calculate the required return.
Formula in words
CAPM required return
E(R) = R_f + β(R_m − R_f)

In words: Required return equals the risk-free rate plus beta times the market's extra return over the risk-free rate.

  • R_freward for waiting (risk-free rate)
  • R_m − R_fmarket risk premium: extra return for bearing market risk
  • β ×scaled up or down for this stock's market risk
Solution
Required = R_f + β(R_m − R_f) = 7 + 1.60 × (12 − 7) = 7 + 8.00 = 15.00%

Required return = 15.00%

Q19CaseFrom your PDF
An investor holds a diversified portfolio and wants to add one asset to reduce risk. Asset M has correlation +0.75 with the portfolio; Asset N has −0.20. Evaluate the diversification benefits and recommend one using Markowitz's principle.
Formula in words
Markowitz diversification
Lower ρ → smaller covariance term → lower σ_p

In words: Adding an asset that moves differently from what you hold reduces risk; the lower (or more negative) the correlation, the bigger the reduction.

  • ρcorrelation with the existing portfolio
Two-asset portfolio risk
σ²_p = w_A²σ_A² + w_B²σ_B² + 2 w_A w_B ρ σ_A σ_B, σ_p = √σ²_p

In words: Portfolio variance is A's weight-squared times its variance, plus B's, plus twice both weights times the correlation times both standard deviations; take the square root for risk.

  • w_A²σ_A²A's own risk contribution
  • w_B²σ_B²B's own risk contribution
  • 2w_Aw_Bρσ_Aσ_Bhow they move together; smaller when ρ is low
  • √square root gives σ_p in %
Solution
σ²_p = w₁²σ₁² + w₂²σ₂² + 2w₁w₂ρσ₁σ₂
The cross-term shrinks as ρ falls, and turns negative when ρ < 0.

Recommend Asset N. With ρ = −0.20 it tends to move against the existing portfolio, making the covariance term negative and cutting total risk. Asset M (ρ = +0.75) moves largely with the portfolio and gives only a small benefit. Markowitz: diversification depends on low correlation, not on the number of assets.

Q20NumericalFrom your PDF
Calculate the expected return using the weighted-average approach, analyse contributions, and explain what happens if the allocation to B increases from 40% to 50% (others adjusted proportionately).
SecurityReturnWeight
A12%30%
B15%40%
C10%30%
Formula in words
Expected portfolio return
R_p = Σ wᵢRᵢ

In words: Portfolio return is each security's return times its weight, added together.

  • wᵢweight (decimal)
  • Rᵢexpected return of the security
  • wᵢRᵢthat security's contribution
Solution

Q21NumericalFrom your PDF
Two securities with correlation 0.30. Calculate (a) expected portfolio return, (b) portfolio variance, (c) portfolio SD; explain the effect of correlation, analyse the diversification benefit and evaluate the risk-return combination.
Security ASecurity B
Weight40%60%
Expected return14%18%
SD10%16%
Formula in words
Expected portfolio return
R_p = Σ wᵢRᵢ

In words: Portfolio return is each security's return times its weight, added together.

  • wᵢweight (decimal)
  • Rᵢexpected return of the security
  • wᵢRᵢthat security's contribution
Two-asset portfolio risk
σ²_p = w_A²σ_A² + w_B²σ_B² + 2 w_A w_B ρ σ_A σ_B, σ_p = √σ²_p

In words: Portfolio variance is A's weight-squared times its variance, plus B's, plus twice both weights times the correlation times both standard deviations; take the square root for risk.

  • w_A²σ_A²A's own risk contribution
  • w_B²σ_B²B's own risk contribution
  • 2w_Aw_Bρσ_Aσ_Bhow they move together; smaller when ρ is low
  • √square root gives σ_p in %
Diversification benefit
Benefit = (w_Aσ_A + w_Bσ_B) − σ_p

In words: The benefit is how much lower the portfolio's risk is than the simple weighted average of the two risks.

  • w_Aσ_A + w_Bσ_Bportfolio risk if correlation were +1
  • σ_pactual portfolio risk
Solution
(a) R_p = 0.4 × 14 + 0.6 × 18 = 16.40%
(b) σ²_p = w²σ² + w²σ² + 2wwρσσ
    = 0.4² × 10.0² + 0.6² × 16.0² + 2 × 0.4 × 0.6 × 0.3 × 10.0 × 16.0
    = 16.00 + 92.16 + 23.04 = 131.20
    σ_p = √131.20 = 11.45%
Weighted average σ (if ρ were +1) = 0.4 × 10.0 + 0.6 × 16.0 = 13.60%
Diversification benefit = 13.60 − 11.45 = 2.15 percentage points

Rp = 16.40%, σp = 11.45%. Because ρ = 0.30 is well below 1, risk is 2.15 points lower than the weighted average, while return is unaffected. Return per unit risk (1.43) is better than either security alone (A 1.40, B 1.13): a suitable combination.

Q24NumericalFrom your PDF
Rf = 5%, Rm = 13%. Calculate (a) CAPM required return, (b) Jensen's alpha, (c) Return to Variability, (d) Return to Volatility for each; (e) rank under each measure; (f) explain why rankings differ; (g) recommend for a conservative and an aggressive investor.
PortfolioReturnBetaSD
A18%1.1020%
B20%1.4018%
C14%0.7512%
Formula in words
CAPM required return
E(R) = R_f + β(R_m − R_f)

In words: Required return equals the risk-free rate plus beta times the market's extra return over the risk-free rate.

  • R_freward for waiting (risk-free rate)
  • R_m − R_fmarket risk premium: extra return for bearing market risk
  • β ×scaled up or down for this stock's market risk
Jensen's alpha
α = R_p − [R_f + β(R_m − R_f)]

In words: Jensen's alpha is the actual return minus the return CAPM says the portfolio should have earned for its beta.

  • R_pactual return
  • R_f + β(R_m − R_f)CAPM return for that beta
  • α > 0manager added value
Sharpe ratio (Return to Variability)
S = (R_p − R_f) / σ_p

In words: Sharpe ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by total risk (standard deviation).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return (reward for risk)
  • σ_ptotal risk
  • Sexcess return per 1% of total risk; higher is better
Treynor ratio (Return to Volatility)
T = (R_p − R_f) ÷ β_p

In words: Treynor ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by beta (market risk).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return
  • β_psystematic risk
  • Market benchmarkR_m − R_f, because the market's β = 1
Solution
Required = 5 + β × 8;  Jensen α = R − Required;  Sharpe = (R − 5)/σ;  Treynor = (R − 5)/β
Portfolio(a) Required %(b) Jensen α(c) Sharpe(d) Treynor
A13.804.200.65011.82
B16.203.800.83310.71
C11.003.000.75012.00
Measure1st2nd3rd
JensenABC
SharpeBCA
TreynorCAB

(f) Sharpe uses total risk (σ), Treynor only systematic risk (β), and Jensen measures absolute excess return. A has a high σ (20%) for its β (1.10), i.e. it carries unsystematic risk, so it ranks last on Sharpe but well on Treynor and Jensen. (g) Conservative: C (lowest β 0.75, lowest σ, best Treynor). Aggressive: B (highest return 20%, best Sharpe).

NAV-based return
R = (Closing NAV − Opening NAV + Distribution) ÷ Opening NAV

In words: A fund's return is the rise in NAV plus the payout per unit, divided by the starting NAV.

  • Closing − Opening NAVchange in unit value
  • Distributioncash paid out per unit
  • ÷ Opening NAVbase for the %
