Q26NumericalFrom your PDF
Return 18%, beta 1.30, SD 12%, Rf 6%, Rm 14%. Calculate (a) CAPM required return, (b) market risk premium, (c) portfolio risk premium, (d) Return to Variability, (e) Return to Volatility, (f) compare actual with required, (g) evaluate adequacy for systematic and total risk.
Formula in words
CAPM required return
E(R) = R_f + β(R_m − R_f)

In words: Required return equals the risk-free rate plus beta times the market's extra return over the risk-free rate.

  • R_freward for waiting (risk-free rate)
  • R_m − R_fmarket risk premium: extra return for bearing market risk
  • β ×scaled up or down for this stock's market risk
Risk premiums
Market risk premium = R_m − R_f; Portfolio risk premium = R_p − R_f

In words: A risk premium is how much a return exceeds the risk-free rate.

  • R_m − R_fmarket's extra return
  • R_p − R_fportfolio's extra return
Sharpe ratio (Return to Variability)
S = (R_p − R_f) / σ_p

In words: Sharpe ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by total risk (standard deviation).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return (reward for risk)
  • σ_ptotal risk
  • Sexcess return per 1% of total risk; higher is better
Treynor ratio (Return to Volatility)
T = (R_p − R_f) ÷ β_p

In words: Treynor ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by beta (market risk).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return
  • β_psystematic risk
  • Market benchmarkR_m − R_f, because the market's β = 1
Jensen's alpha
α = R_p − [R_f + β(R_m − R_f)]

In words: Jensen's alpha is the actual return minus the return CAPM says the portfolio should have earned for its beta.

  • R_pactual return
  • R_f + β(R_m − R_f)CAPM return for that beta
  • α > 0manager added value
Solution
(a) 6 + 1.30 × 8 = 16.4%
(b) R_m − R_f = 14 − 6 = 8%
(c) R_p − R_f = 18 − 6 = 12%
(d) 12 / 12 = 1.00
(e) 12 / 1.30 = 9.23
(f) 18 − 16.4 = +1.6% (Jensen's alpha)
Market: Sharpe benchmark n/a (σ_m not given); Treynor benchmark = 8

The portfolio beat its CAPM return by 1.6% and its Treynor ratio (9.23) exceeds the market's 8, so it earned more than adequate reward for systematic risk. Its Sharpe ratio of 1.0 shows one point of excess return per point of total risk, a sound result.

Q29NumericalFrom your PDF
An institutional investor uses APT with three factors and Rf = 7%. Market-implied return 22%. (a) APT required return. (b) Compare and analyse mispricing. (c) Explain an arbitrage strategy.
FactorBetaRisk premium
GDP growth0.74%
Interest rate0.63.5%
Inflation0.42.5%
Formula in words
Arbitrage Pricing Theory
E(R) = R_f + b₁λ₁ + b₂λ₂ + b₃λ₃

In words: Required return is the risk-free rate plus, for every factor, the security's sensitivity to that factor times the factor's risk premium.

  • bⱼsensitivity (beta) to factor j
  • λⱼrisk premium of factor j
  • bⱼλⱼreward for that factor's risk
Solution
(a) E(R) = R_f + Σ bλ = 7 + 0.7(4) + 0.6(3.5) + 0.4(2.5) = 7 + 2.8 + 2.1 + 1.0 = 12.9%
(b) 22% − 12.9% = 9.1% above the APT return → underpriced

(c) Buy the security and short a portfolio with the same factor betas (0.7, 0.6, 0.4) that earns 12.9%. Factor risk cancels, locking in the 9.1% spread with no net investment; buying pressure raises the price until the expected return falls to 12.9%.

Q30NumericalFrom your PDF
Ten years of data for a mutual fund, the market index and government securities. Calculate the Treynor and Sharpe ratios, rank the fund under both, analyse differences, evaluate performance relative to systematic and total risk, and say whether the manager's performance is satisfactory.

1017.650.9520.006.00
Formula in words
Multi-year averages
R̄ = ΣR ÷ n, σ = √[Σ(R − R̄)² ÷ n]

In words: Average each column over the years, then measure spread around the average for the standard deviation.

  • nnumber of years
  • σuse n (or n − 1 for a sample) consistently
Treynor ratio (Return to Volatility)
T = (R_p − R_f) ÷ β_p

In words: Treynor ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by beta (market risk).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return
  • β_psystematic risk
  • Market benchmarkR_m − R_f, because the market's β = 1
Sharpe ratio (Return to Variability)
S = (R_p − R_f) / σ_p

In words: Sharpe ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by total risk (standard deviation).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return (reward for risk)
  • σ_ptotal risk
  • Sexcess return per 1% of total risk; higher is better
Jensen's alpha
α = R_p − [R_f + β(R_m − R_f)]

In words: Jensen's alpha is the actual return minus the return CAPM says the portfolio should have earned for its beta.

  • R_pactual return
  • R_f + β(R_m − R_f)CAPM return for that beta
  • α > 0manager added value
Solution

σ_p = 9.76%   σ_m = 10.40%   (population SD, ÷ n)

Treynor fund   = (16.898 − 5.453) / 1.194 = 9.59
Treynor market = 9.556 − 5.453 = 4.10   (β_m = 1)
Sharpe fund    = 11.445 / 9.76 = 1.173
Sharpe market  = 4.103 / 10.40 = 0.394
Jensen α       = 16.898 − [5.453 + 1.194 × 4.103] = 6.55%

With sample SDs (÷ n − 1): fund Sharpe 1.113, market 0.374. Same conclusion.

The fund ranks above the market on both measures (Treynor 9.59 vs 4.10; Sharpe 1.17 vs 0.39), so rankings agree. Both total and systematic risk were well rewarded, alpha is strongly positive, and the manager's performance is satisfactory.

Q31CaseFrom your PDF
Ritika's advisor considers Security M (expected 10%, SD 11.2%) and N (9%, SD 15.8%), correlation 0.42, allocation 70:30. (Five-year history: M 11, 6, 4, 13, 16; N 12, −5, 14, 10, 14.) Calculate the expected return and SD of the 70:30 portfolio; explain the efficient frontier and the role of diversification and correlation; analyse whether the 70:30 portfolio beats investing entirely in N.
Formula in words
Expected portfolio return
R_p = Σ wᵢRᵢ

In words: Portfolio return is each security's return times its weight, added together.

  • wᵢweight (decimal)
  • Rᵢexpected return of the security
  • wᵢRᵢthat security's contribution
Two-asset portfolio risk
σ²_p = w_A²σ_A² + w_B²σ_B² + 2 w_A w_B ρ σ_A σ_B, σ_p = √σ²_p

In words: Portfolio variance is A's weight-squared times its variance, plus B's, plus twice both weights times the correlation times both standard deviations; take the square root for risk.

  • w_A²σ_A²A's own risk contribution
  • w_B²σ_B²B's own risk contribution
  • 2w_Aw_Bρσ_Aσ_Bhow they move together; smaller when ρ is low
  • √square root gives σ_p in %
Solution
(a) R_p = 0.7 × 10 + 0.3 × 9 = 9.70%
(b) σ²_p = w²σ² + w²σ² + 2wwρσσ
    = 0.7² × 11.2² + 0.3² × 15.8² + 2 × 0.7 × 0.3 × 0.42 × 11.2 × 15.8
    = 61.47 + 22.47 + 31.22 = 115.15
    σ_p = √115.15 = 10.73%
Weighted average σ (if ρ were +1) = 0.7 × 11.2 + 0.3 × 15.8 = 12.58%
Diversification benefit = 12.58 − 10.73 = 1.85 percentage points

Use the given SDs and correlation. (The five-year data give means of 10% and 9%, matching the stated returns.)

Efficient frontier: the set of portfolios offering the highest return for each level of risk. Combining assets with correlation below +1 bends the frontier to the left; the lower the correlation, the larger the risk reduction and the more valuable the frontier's portfolios.

70:30 portfolio: Rp = 9.70%, σp = 10.73%. Versus N alone (9%, 15.8%) it gives a higher return with much lower risk, so it dominates N and is preferable for Ritika.

Fund Alpha's σ of 11% against a β of 0.6 hints at such unsystematic risk.

Q33CaseFrom your PDF
Vikram evaluates Falcon Ltd. (β 0.90, expected 10.70%) and Phoenix Ltd. (β 1.30, expected 10.80%). Rf = 6%, expected market return = 11%. Apply CAPM, explain the SML and position the two stocks, and recommend one.
Formula in words
CAPM required return
E(R) = R_f + β(R_m − R_f)

In words: Required return equals the risk-free rate plus beta times the market's extra return over the risk-free rate.

  • R_freward for waiting (risk-free rate)
  • R_m − R_fmarket risk premium: extra return for bearing market risk
  • β ×scaled up or down for this stock's market risk
Security Market Line
Required = R_f + β(R_m − R_f); above the line if Expected > Required

In words: Plot required return against beta; stocks expected to earn more than the line are underpriced, less are overpriced.

  • InterceptR_f
  • Slopemarket risk premium R_m − R_f
Solution
StockβExpectedRequiredAlphaPosition
Falcon0.9010.70%6 + 0.90 × 5 = 10.50%0.20%Above SML (underpriced)
Phoenix1.3010.80%6 + 1.30 × 5 = 12.50%−1.70%Below SML (overpriced)

SML: plots required return against beta, starting at Rf = 6% with slope equal to the market premium (5%). Stocks above it offer more than their risk requires; stocks below it offer less.

Prefer Falcon: it lies slightly above the SML (+0.2%), adequately compensating its lower systematic risk. Phoenix lies 1.7% below the SML: its higher beta is not rewarded.

Theory practice (20)

T12-markPractice · from notes
What is a portfolio?

A combination of securities held together to achieve a desired balance of risk and return.

T22-markPractice · from notes
State the formula for expected portfolio return.

Rp = Σ wiRi, the weighted average of the expected returns of the securities.

T32-markPractice · from notes
What is diversification?

Spreading investment across securities whose returns are not perfectly positively correlated, so that portfolio risk falls; it removes unsystematic risk.

T42-markPractice · from notes
What is the efficient frontier?

The set of portfolios offering the maximum expected return for each level of risk (or minimum risk for each level of return).

T52-markPractice · from notes
What is the Security Market Line?

The graphical representation of CAPM, showing the linear relationship between beta and required return.

T62-markPractice · from notes
What is the market risk premium?

The excess of the expected market return over the risk-free rate, Rm − Rf.

T72-markPractice · from notes
Define the Sharpe ratio.

The reward-to-variability ratio: (Rp − Rf)/σp, excess return per unit of total risk.

T82-markPractice · from notes
Define the Treynor ratio.

The reward-to-volatility ratio: (Rp − Rf)/βp, excess return per unit of systematic risk.

T92-markPractice · from notes
What is Jensen's alpha?

The difference between a portfolio's actual return and its CAPM-predicted return: Rp − [Rf + β(Rm − Rf)].

T102-markPractice · from notes
State the APT equation.

E(R) = Rf + b1λ1 + … + bkλk, where b = factor sensitivity and λ = factor risk premium.

T112-markPractice · from notes
How is NAV-based return calculated?

(Closing NAV − Opening NAV + Distribution per unit) ÷ Opening NAV.

T122-markPractice · from notes
When is a security underpriced under CAPM?

When its expected return exceeds its CAPM required return (positive alpha); it plots above the SML.

T135-markPractice · from notes
Explain the effect of correlation on portfolio risk.
Formula

σ²p = wA²σA² + wB²σB² + 2wAwBρσAσB.

Effect
  • ρ = +1: no risk reduction; σp = weighted average σ.
  • 0 < ρ < 1: partial risk reduction.
  • ρ = 0: substantial reduction.
  • ρ = −1: risk can be eliminated with suitable weights.
Example

Q21 (ρ = 0.30): σp = 11.45% vs weighted average 13.6%; the 2.15-point saving is the diversification benefit, while return stays at 16.4%.

T145-markPractice · from notes
Explain the Markowitz model and the efficient frontier.
Meaning

Markowitz's mean-variance model selects portfolios based on expected return and variance.

Assumptions
  • Investors are risk-averse and prefer more return to less.
  • Decisions are based on expected return and variance.
  • Returns' covariances are known.
Efficient frontier

Plotting all feasible portfolios, the upper-left boundary contains efficient portfolios (maximum return for given risk). Lower correlations bend it further left, increasing diversification benefit.

Choice

The investor picks the efficient portfolio that best matches personal risk tolerance.

Example

Q31: the 70:30 M–N portfolio (9.7%, 10.73%) dominates holding N alone (9%, 15.8%).

T155-markPractice · from notes
Explain CAPM and the SML in identifying mispriced securities.
CAPM

E(Ri) = Rf + βi(Rm − Rf).

SML

A straight line with β on the x-axis and required return on the y-axis; intercept Rf, slope = market risk premium.

Mispricing
  • Expected > required → above SML → underpriced → buy.
  • Expected < required → below SML → overpriced → sell.
Example

Q33: Falcon (β 0.9) required 10.5%, expected 10.7% → above SML; Phoenix (β 1.3) required 12.5%, expected 10.8% → below SML. Prefer Falcon.

T165-markPractice · from notes
Compare the Sharpe, Treynor and Jensen measures.
Sharpe

(Rp − Rf)/σp; total risk; suits undiversified/entire portfolios.

Treynor

(Rp − Rf)/βp; systematic risk; suits well-diversified portfolios.

Jensen

Rp − CAPM return; absolute excess return; positive = superior manager.

Why rankings differ

A poorly diversified fund has high σ relative to β, lowering Sharpe but not Treynor.

Example

Q32: Fund Alpha S = 0.73, T = 13.33, α = +2%; Fund Beta S = 1.78, T = 14.55, α = +5%. Fund Beta is better on all three.

T175-markPractice · from notes
Explain Arbitrage Pricing Theory.
Meaning

APT (Ross) states that returns are driven by several systematic factors, and that arbitrage eliminates mispricing.

Equation

E(R) = Rf + Σ bjλj.

Features
  • Multi-factor (GDP, interest rates, inflation, etc.).
  • Does not require the market portfolio.
  • Based on no-arbitrage.
Example

Q29: 7 + 2.8 + 2.1 + 1.0 = 12.9% vs market-implied 22% → underpriced; buy it and short a factor-matched portfolio.

Comparison

CAPM is the single-factor special case.

T18Long answerPractice · from notes
Discuss portfolio construction using the Markowitz model. Explain portfolio return, portfolio risk, diversification and the efficient frontier with an illustration.
Introduction

Portfolio construction combines securities to achieve the best trade-off between risk and return. Harry Markowitz showed that risk depends not only on individual securities but on how they move together.

Assumptions
  • Investors are rational and risk-averse.
  • They evaluate portfolios by expected return and variance.
  • For a given risk they prefer higher return; for a given return, lower risk.
Portfolio return

Rp = Σ wiRi.

Portfolio risk

σ²p = wA²σA² + wB²σB² + 2wAwBρσAσB; σp = √σ²p.

Diversification

When ρ < 1, σp is less than the weighted average σ; the lower ρ, the greater the benefit. Diversification removes unsystematic risk; systematic risk remains.

Illustration (Q31)

M: 10%, σ 11.2%; N: 9%, σ 15.8%; ρ = 0.42; weights 70:30.
Rp = 0.7(10) + 0.3(9) = 9.7%.
σ²p = 0.49(125.44) + 0.09(249.64) + 2(0.7)(0.3)(0.42)(11.2)(15.8) = 61.47 + 22.47 + 31.22 = 115.15 → σp = 10.73%.
Compared with N alone (9%, 15.8%), the portfolio gives higher return and lower risk, so it dominates N.

Efficient frontier

Of all feasible portfolios, those offering the highest return for each risk level form the efficient frontier (upper-left boundary). Lower correlations make the frontier bow further to the left. The investor chooses the point matching personal risk preference.

Limitations
  • Requires many estimates (returns, variances, covariances).
  • Estimates from history may not hold.
  • Variance treats upside and downside equally.
Conclusion

The Markowitz model shows that intelligent diversification, based on correlation rather than number of securities, delivers better risk-return combinations and is the foundation of modern portfolio theory.

T19Long answerPractice · from notes
Explain the methods of evaluating portfolio performance: Sharpe, Treynor and Jensen. Why may rankings differ? Illustrate.
Introduction

Absolute returns can mislead because higher returns may simply reflect higher risk. Performance must therefore be risk-adjusted.

NAV return

R = (NAV1 − NAV0 + D)/NAV0 gives the absolute return (Q25: Fund A 27.5%, Fund B 16.67%).

Sharpe ratio (Return to Variability)

S = (Rp − Rf)/σp. Excess return per unit of total risk; compare with market Sharpe.

Treynor ratio (Return to Volatility)

T = (Rp − Rf)/βp. Excess return per unit of systematic risk; compare with Rm − Rf.

Jensen's alpha

α = Rp − [Rf + βp(Rm − Rf)]. Positive α = manager outperformed CAPM expectation.

Illustration (Q24: Rf 5%, Rm 13%)
PortfolioReturnβσJensen αSharpeTreynor
A18%1.1020%+4.20.65011.82
B20%1.4018%+3.80.83310.71
C14%0.7512%+3.00.75012.00
Why rankings differ

Sharpe ranks B first; Treynor ranks C first; Jensen ranks A first. Sharpe penalises total risk, so A, with high σ relative to its β (poorly diversified), ranks last. Treynor ignores unsystematic risk. Jensen measures absolute excess return, favouring larger portfolios of risk.

Which to use
  • Sharpe: when the fund is the investor's entire portfolio.
  • Treynor: when the fund is one part of a diversified holding.
  • Jensen: to judge manager skill relative to CAPM.
Conclusion

Using all three measures gives a complete view of performance; conservative investors may prefer C (lowest β and σ), aggressive investors B.

T20Long answerPractice · from notes
Explain CAPM, the SML and APT. How are they used to identify mispriced securities?
Introduction

Asset pricing models determine the return a security should earn for its risk, providing a benchmark against which expected returns are judged.

CAPM

E(Ri) = Rf + βi(Rm − Rf). Assumptions: diversified risk-averse investors, borrowing/lending at Rf, homogeneous expectations, no taxes/transaction costs. Only systematic risk is priced.

SML

Plots required return against β. Securities above the SML (positive α) are underpriced; below it, overpriced.

Illustration

Q23 (Rf 5%, Rm 12%): X α +4.2, Y α +4.4, Z α +1.8 → all underpriced; Y most attractive. Q28: all three securities have negative α → overpriced; B least overpriced.

APT

E(R) = Rf + Σ bjλj; multi-factor and based on no-arbitrage. Q29: required 12.9% vs implied 22% → buy the security and short a factor-matched portfolio until prices adjust.

CAPM vs APT

CAPM: single factor (market), relies on market portfolio. APT: multiple factors, fewer assumptions, factors not specified by theory.

Limitations

Beta instability; unrealistic assumptions; choice of factors in APT is empirical.

Conclusion

CAPM and APT convert risk into required return; comparing expected with required return identifies mispriced securities and guides portfolio allocation.

Unit 1

Expected return & standard deviation (price tables)

E(R) = Σ pR, σ = √Σ p(R − E)²

In wordsTurn each price into a return; expected return is each return times its probability, added up. Risk is the square root of the probability-weighted squared gaps from that expected return.

H1U1 · Q3NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 1 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Prices, not returns, are given, so you must first convert six prices using the ₹120 cost. Every return is a recurring decimal (4.1667%, 8.3333%), so rounding early throws σ off; it needs the longest table in the unit.

A stock costing Rs 120 pays no dividends. The possible prices that the stock might sell for at the end of the year with the respective probabilities are as follows. Calculate (a) the expected return and (b) the standard deviation of returns.
Price (₹)Probability
1150.1
1200.1
1250.2
1300.3
1350.2
1400.1
Formula in words
Return from a possible price
Rᵢ = (P₁ᵢ − P₀) / P₀

In words: For each possible year-end price, the return is the price change divided by today's cost.

  • P₁ᵢone possible year-end price
  • P₀today's cost of the share
  • Rᵢthe return if that price happens
Expected return
E(R) = Σ pᵢRᵢ

In words: Expected return equals each possible return multiplied by its probability, all added together.

  • Rᵢa possible return
  • pᵢthe chance of that return (all p add to 1)
  • Σadd the products for every outcome
Variance and standard deviation
σ² = Σ pᵢ[Rᵢ − E(R)]², σ = √σ²

In words: Risk is how far each return is from the expected return: square each gap, weight it by its probability, add them up (variance), then take the square root (standard deviation).

  • Rᵢ − E(R)gap between an outcome and the expected return
  • ( )²square it so minus and plus gaps don't cancel
  • × pᵢ, Σweight by probability and add → variance σ²
  • √square root brings risk back to % → σ
Solution
Step 1: convert each price into a return, R = (P₁ − P₀)/P₀
  ₹115: (115 − 120) / 120 = −4.17%
  ₹120: (120 − 120) / 120 = 0.00%
  ₹125: (125 − 120) / 120 = 4.17%
  ₹130: (130 − 120) / 120 = 8.33%
  ₹135: (135 − 120) / 120 = 12.50%
  ₹140: (140 − 120) / 120 = 16.67%
R (%)ppRR − E(R)(R − E)²p(R − E)²
−4.170.10−0.417−11.250126.56212.656
0.000.100.000−7.08350.1745.017
4.170.200.833−2.9178.5071.701
8.330.302.5001.2501.5620.469
12.500.202.5005.41729.3405.868
16.670.101.6679.58391.8409.184
Total1.007.08334.896
E(R) = 7.08%
σ² = 34.896  →  σ = 5.91%
Check: expected price = Σ pP₁ = ₹128.50 → (128.50 − 120) / 120 = 7.08%

(a) Expected return = 7.08% (b) Standard deviation = 5.91%

Unit 1

Return and risk with borrowed funds (margin)

R = [N(P₁ − P₀) − Interest] ÷ Own funds

In wordsTake the rupee gain on all the shares, subtract the interest on the loan, and divide by only the money you put in yourself.

H3U1 · Q18NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 1 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Two complete scenarios (no borrowing vs 60% borrowed with 200 shares). The bad outcome is a loss that interest makes worse (−₹1,720), and the result is counter-intuitive: borrowing lowers expected return while multiplying risk, which you must explain.

There is a fifty-fifty chance that a stock, currently selling at Rs 60, will sell for Rs 55 or Rs 75 by the year-end. The investor can borrow on 40 percent margin from his bank at 10 percent per annum. (a) What are the investor's expected holding period yield and risk if he buys 100 shares and does not borrow? (b) What would be his expected yield and risk if he buys 200 shares paying 60 percent of the cost with borrowed funds?
Formula in words
Return from a possible price
Rᵢ = (P₁ᵢ − P₀) / P₀

In words: For each possible year-end price, the return is the price change divided by today's cost.

  • P₁ᵢone possible year-end price
  • P₀today's cost of the share
  • Rᵢthe return if that price happens
Shortcut for two equally likely outcomes
E(R) = (R₁ + R₂)/2, σ = |R₁ − R₂| / 2

In words: With a fifty-fifty chance, expected return is the simple average of the two returns, and risk is half the gap between them.

  • (R₁ + R₂)/2average of the two returns
  • |R₁ − R₂|/2half the distance between them = σ
Return on own funds with borrowing
R = [N(P₁ − P₀) − Interest] / Own funds

In words: Return on your own money equals the gain on all the shares, minus interest on the loan, divided by only the money you put in yourself.

  • N(P₁ − P₀)rupee gain or loss on all shares bought
  • Interestborrowed amount × borrowing rate, paid in every outcome
  • Own fundstotal cost minus borrowed amount (the base for %)
Solution
(a) No borrowing (100 shares; % results don't depend on number of shares)
    At ₹55: (55 − 60)/60 = −8.33%     At ₹75: (75 − 60)/60 = 25.00%
    E(R) = (−8.33 + 25)/2 = 8.33%     σ = (25 + 8.33)/2 = 16.67%
    (₹ terms: expected gain = 100 × ₹5 = ₹500; σ = 100 × ₹10 = ₹1,000)

(b) 200 shares, 60% borrowed
    Cost = 200 × 60 = ₹12,000; borrowed = ₹7,200; own = ₹4,800; interest = 10% × 7,200 = ₹720
    At ₹55: (−5 × 200 − 720)/4,800 = −1,720/4,800 = −35.83%
    At ₹75: (15 × 200 − 720)/4,800 = 2,280/4,800 = 47.50%
    E(R) = (−35.83 + 47.50)/2 = 5.83%     σ = (47.50 + 35.83)/2 = 41.67%

(a) Expected yield 8.33%, risk 16.67%. (b) Expected yield 5.83%, risk 41.67%. Borrowing raises risk two-and-a-half times and lowers expected yield, because the 10% interest cost exceeds the stock's 8.33% expected return.

H4U1 · Q4NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 1 →
Why it's one of the hardest

With 80% borrowed, the own-funds base is tiny (₹2,150), so returns explode above 90% and 140%. Students habitually divide by total cost (₹10,750) or forget the ₹1,032 interest, and both errors give a believable but wrong answer.

An investor has analysed a share for one-year holding period. The share is currently selling for Rs 43 but pays no dividends and there is a fifty-fifty chance that the share will sell for either Rs 55 or Rs 60 by the year-end. What is the expected return and risk if 250 shares are acquired with 80 percent borrowed funds? Assume the cost of borrowed funds to be 12 percent (ignore commissions and taxes).
Formula in words
Return on own funds with borrowing
R = [N(P₁ − P₀) − Interest] / Own funds

In words: Return on your own money equals the gain on all the shares, minus interest on the loan, divided by only the money you put in yourself.

  • N(P₁ − P₀)rupee gain or loss on all shares bought
  • Interestborrowed amount × borrowing rate, paid in every outcome
  • Own fundstotal cost minus borrowed amount (the base for %)
Shortcut for two equally likely outcomes
E(R) = (R₁ + R₂)/2, σ = |R₁ − R₂| / 2

In words: With a fifty-fifty chance, expected return is the simple average of the two returns, and risk is half the gap between them.

  • (R₁ + R₂)/2average of the two returns
  • |R₁ − R₂|/2half the distance between them = σ
Solution
Total cost      = 250 × 43 = ₹10,750
Borrowed (80%)  = ₹8,600
Own funds (20%) = ₹2,150
Interest (12%)  = 0.12 × 8,600 = ₹1,032

Price ₹55: gain = (55 − 43) × 250 = 3,000; net = 3,000 − 1,032 = 1,968 → 1,968 / 2,150 = 91.53%
Price ₹60: gain = (60 − 43) × 250 = 4,250; net = 4,250 − 1,032 = 3,218 → 3,218 / 2,150 = 149.67%

E(R) = 0.5 × 91.53 + 0.5 × 149.67 = 120.60%
σ   = √[0.5(−29.07)² + 0.5(29.07)²] = 29.07%

For comparison, without borrowing: returns 27.91% and 39.53% → E(R) = 33.72%, σ = 5.81%. Borrowing multiplies both by about 5 (cost ÷ own funds) after interest.

With 80% borrowed funds: expected return = 120.60%, risk (σ) = 29.07%

Unit 1

Beta and alpha by regression

β = (nΣXY − ΣXΣY) ÷ (nΣX² − (ΣX)²), α = Ȳ − βX̄

In wordsBeta is n times the sum of XY minus the product of the sums, over n times the sum of X-squared minus the sum of X squared (X = market). Alpha is the stock's average minus beta times the market's average.

H5U1 · Q6NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 1 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Two full 12-row regressions (ITC and ONGC), a negative beta that looks like an error, an outlier in month 12, and then a forecast that needs both α and β. Most arithmetic per mark in the unit.

With the data given in Question 5, calculate beta of ITC stock using the regression model. Monthly return data in per cent for ONGC stock and the NSE index for a 12-month period are presented below. (a) Calculate alpha and beta for the ONGC stock. (b) Suppose NSE index is expected to move up by 15 per cent next month. How much return would you expect from ONGC?
MonthONGC (%)NSE (%)
1−0.75−0.35
25.45−0.49
3−3.05−1.03
43.411.64
59.136.67
62.361.13
7−0.420.72
85.510.84
96.804.05
102.601.21
11−3.810.29
1232.83−1.96
Formula in words
Beta by regression
β = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY] / [nΣX² − (ΣX)²]

In words: Beta equals n times the sum of XY minus the product of the sums, divided by n times the sum of X-squared minus the square of the sum of X.

  • Xmarket (index) return; always the market
  • Ythe stock's return
  • tophow stock and market move together
  • bottomhow much the market moves on its own
  • β% move in the stock for a 1% move in the market
Alpha and the characteristic line
α = Ȳ − βX̄, Rᵢ = α + βR_m

In words: Alpha is the stock's average return minus beta times the market's average return; the stock's predicted return is alpha plus beta times the market return.

  • Ȳ, X̄average stock return and average market return
  • αstock's return when the market return is zero (starting point)
  • β R_mthe extra return that comes from the market move
Solution

ITC by regression: using the Q5 table, β = 1.3841 and α = 3.5612. Characteristic line: RITC = 3.56 + 1.38Rm.

ONGC:

#NSE (X)ONGC (Y)XYX²

n = 12
β = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY] / [nΣX² − (ΣX)²]
  = [12 × 39.4501 − 12.72 × 60.06] / [12 × 72.8952 − 12.72²]
  = −290.5620 / 712.9440 = −0.4076

α = Ȳ − βX̄ = 5.0050 − −0.4076 × 1.0600 = 5.4370
(b) R_ONGC = α + β × R_m = 5.4370 + (−0.4076 × 15) = −0.68%

Month 12 for ONGC (32.83) is the same figure as ITC's month 12 and is an extreme outlier against an NSE fall of −1.96%. It produces the negative beta; it may be a copying error in the question bank. Solved as printed.

(a) α = 5.44, β = −0.41 (b) Expected ONGC return ≈ −0.68% for a 15% rise in NSE

H6U1 · Q20NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 1 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Prices, not returns: you must first turn three price series into 11 returns each (33 divisions), then run two regressions on messy decimals. One wrong return contaminates both betas.

As a management trainee you have to analyse the share prices of two companies along with the NSE index. (a) Which one has high systematic risk? (b) Based on return, which company's stock will you select?
PeriodCompany ACompany BNSE
124.0050.38857.07

727.2555.38859.68
826.0054.38871.91
926.7055.28878.53
1027.7041.38807.23
1125.6048.30877.72

Formula in words
Return from a possible price
Rᵢ = (P₁ᵢ − P₀) / P₀

In words: For each possible year-end price, the return is the price change divided by today's cost.

  • P₁ᵢone possible year-end price
  • P₀today's cost of the share
  • Rᵢthe return if that price happens
Beta by regression
β = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY] / [nΣX² − (ΣX)²]

In words: Beta equals n times the sum of XY minus the product of the sums, divided by n times the sum of X-squared minus the square of the sum of X.

  • Xmarket (index) return; always the market
  • Ythe stock's return
  • tophow stock and market move together
  • bottomhow much the market moves on its own
  • β% move in the stock for a 1% move in the market

Unit 1

Covariance and correlation

Cov = Σ p(RA − EA)(RB − EB), r = Cov ÷ (σAσB)

In wordsCovariance multiplies the two securities' gaps from their averages, weights by probability and adds. Correlation divides that by both standard deviations, giving a number from −1 to +1.

H7U1 · Q7NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 1 →
Why it's one of the hardest

The question does not even say what to compute. You need two expected returns, two SDs, a covariance with mixed signs and then r, and every product of deviations is negative, which is easy to mishandle.

The returns on securities A and B are given below. (Find the expected return and risk of each, and the covariance and correlation between them.)
ProbabilitySecurity A (%)Security B (%)
0.540
0.423
0.103
Formula in words
Expected return
E(R) = Σ pᵢRᵢ

In words: Expected return equals each possible return multiplied by its probability, all added together.

  • Rᵢa possible return
  • pᵢthe chance of that return (all p add to 1)
  • Σadd the products for every outcome
Variance and standard deviation
σ² = Σ pᵢ[Rᵢ − E(R)]², σ = √σ²

In words: Risk is how far each return is from the expected return: square each gap, weight it by its probability, add them up (variance), then take the square root (standard deviation).

  • Rᵢ − E(R)gap between an outcome and the expected return
  • ( )²square it so minus and plus gaps don't cancel
  • × pᵢ, Σweight by probability and add → variance σ²
  • √square root brings risk back to % → σ
Covariance
Cov(A,B) = Σ pᵢ[R_A − E(R_A)][R_B − E(R_B)]

In words: Covariance multiplies A's gap from its average by B's gap from its average in each state, weights by probability, and adds.

  • R_A − E(R_A)how far A is from its own average
  • R_B − E(R_B)how far B is from its own average
  • product × p, Σsame-sign gaps add (move together); opposite signs subtract (move apart)
Correlation coefficient
r = Cov(A,B) / (σ_A × σ_B)

In words: Correlation is the covariance divided by the two standard deviations, which squeezes it into a scale from −1 to +1.

  • Cov(A,B)direction of co-movement
  • σ_A × σ_Bscales away the size of each security's own risk
  • r+1 perfectly together, 0 unrelated, −1 perfectly opposite
Solution
pRARA − EARBRB − EBp × product
0.5041.200−1.50−0.900
0.402−0.8031.50−0.480
0.100−2.8031.50−0.420
Cov−1.800
E(A) = 0.5(4) + 0.4(2) + 0.1(0) = 2.80%     σ_A = √1.760 = 1.327%
E(B) = 0.5(0) + 0.4(3) + 0.1(3) = 1.50%     σ_B = √2.250 = 1.500%
Cov(A,B) = −1.800
r = Cov / (σ_A σ_B) = −1.800 / (1.327 × 1.500) = −0.905

E(A) = 2.80%, σ_A = 1.33%; E(B) = 1.50%, σ_B = 1.50%; Cov = −1.80; r = −0.90. Strong negative correlation: combining A and B reduces risk considerably.

H8U1 · Q12NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 1 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Pearson's sums formula has five columns and the classic ΣX² vs (ΣX)² trap; the denominator needs a square root of a product of two brackets.

The monthly returns (%) of the Nifty Index and XYZ Ltd. for four months are given below. (1) Calculate the Pearson correlation coefficient (r). (2) Interpret the result.
MonthNifty (X)XYZ (Y)
May57
June911
July79
August1114
Formula in words
Pearson correlation (sums method)
r = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY] / √{[nΣX² − (ΣX)²][nΣY² − (ΣY)²]}

In words: Correlation equals n times the sum of XY minus the product of the two sums, divided by the square root of the X-bracket times the Y-bracket.

  • nΣXY − ΣXΣYtop: how X and Y move together
  • nΣX² − (ΣX)²X-bracket: spread of X
  • nΣY² − (ΣY)²Y-bracket: spread of Y
  • ΣX² vs (ΣX)²square each then add, vs add then square: not the same
Solution
MonthXYXYX²Y²
May57352549
June9119981121
July79634981
Aug1114154121196
Σ3241351276447
r = [nΣXY − ΣXΣY] / √{[nΣX² − (ΣX)²][nΣY² − (ΣY)²]}
  = [4(351) − 32(41)] / √{[4(276) − 32²][4(447) − 41²]}
  = (1,404 − 1,312) / √(80 × 107)
  = 92 / √8,560 = 92 / 92.52 = 0.994

r = +0.994: a very strong positive relationship. XYZ's returns move almost perfectly with the Nifty; r² ≈ 0.99, so about 99% of XYZ's return variation is explained by the market (largely systematic risk).

Unit 1

Choosing between stocks (dominance, CV)

Same σ → higher E; same E → lower σ; CV = σ ÷ E(R)

In wordsAt equal risk pick the higher return; at equal return pick the lower risk. Otherwise compare risk per unit of return (σ divided by expected return; lower is better).

H9U1 · Q8NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 1 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Four full probability distributions (16 outcomes) before you can even begin, then you must spot that A and C have identical σ and B and D identical E(R), and argue dominance rather than just pick the highest return.

An investor has a choice of four stocks for investment. Their rates of return and probabilities are given below. (a) Are all these stocks attractive investments? Give reasons. (b) Of those that are attractive, how should the investor choose one to buy?
StockReturn (probability) pairs
A−30% (20%), 0% (40%), 30% (30%), 70% (10%)
B−20% (15%), 0% (35%), 20% (45%), 40% (5%)
C−20% (20%), 10% (40%), 40% (30%), 80% (10%)
D−10% (10%), 0% (25%), 10% (40%), 20% (25%)
Formula in words
Expected return
E(R) = Σ pᵢRᵢ

In words: Expected return equals each possible return multiplied by its probability, all added together.

  • Rᵢa possible return
  • pᵢthe chance of that return (all p add to 1)
  • Σadd the products for every outcome
Variance and standard deviation
σ² = Σ pᵢ[Rᵢ − E(R)]², σ = √σ²

In words: Risk is how far each return is from the expected return: square each gap, weight it by its probability, add them up (variance), then take the square root (standard deviation).

  • Rᵢ − E(R)gap between an outcome and the expected return
  • ( )²square it so minus and plus gaps don't cancel
  • × pᵢ, Σweight by probability and add → variance σ²
  • √square root brings risk back to % → σ
Choosing between stocks
Dominance first; CV = σ / E(R)

In words: If two stocks have the same risk, take the higher return; if the same return, take the lower risk; otherwise compare risk per unit of return (CV).

  • σtotal risk
  • E(R)expected return
  • CVrisk per 1% of return; lower is better
Solution
StockE(R) %Varianceσ %CV = σ/E
A10.00840.0028.982.90
B8.00256.0016.002.00
C20.00840.0028.981.45
D8.0086.009.271.16
A: E = −6 + 0 + 9 + 7 = 10%          B: E = −3 + 0 + 9 + 2 = 8%
C: E = −4 + 4 + 12 + 8 = 20%         D: E = −1 + 0 + 4 + 5 = 8%

Dominance:
A vs C → same σ (28.98%), C gives 20% > 10% → A is dominated
B vs D → same E(R) (8%), D has σ 9.27% < 16% → B is dominated

(a) No. A and B are unattractive because they are dominated (C beats A at equal risk; D beats B at equal return). (b) Choose between C and D by risk preference: C for a risk-taking investor (20% return, σ 28.98%); D for a risk-averse investor (lowest σ and lowest CV, 1.16).

H10U1 · Q23NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 1 →
Why it's one of the hardest

The CV points to Y (lower risk per unit of return) but the correct answer is X, because X's worst outcome beats Y's best. You must recognise when dominance overrides CV.

An investor wants to choose either X or Y company's stock. Neither pays dividends. X sells for Rs 50 and Y for Rs 100. At year end X may sell at Rs 71 or Rs 67 and Y at Rs 127 or Rs 123 (50% each). Which company's scrip should the investor buy? Justify.
StockCurrent pricePossible end pricesProbability
X₹50₹71 or ₹6750% each
Y₹100₹127 or ₹12350% each
Formula in words
Return from a possible price
Rᵢ = (P₁ᵢ − P₀) / P₀

In words: For each possible year-end price, the return is the price change divided by today's cost.

  • P₁ᵢone possible year-end price
  • P₀today's cost of the share
  • Rᵢthe return if that price happens
Shortcut for two equally likely outcomes
E(R) = (R₁ + R₂)/2, σ = |R₁ − R₂| / 2

In words: With a fifty-fifty chance, expected return is the simple average of the two returns, and risk is half the gap between them.

  • (R₁ + R₂)/2average of the two returns
  • |R₁ − R₂|/2half the distance between them = σ
Choosing between stocks
Dominance first; CV = σ / E(R)

In words: If two stocks have the same risk, take the higher return; if the same return, take the lower risk; otherwise compare risk per unit of return (CV).

  • σtotal risk
  • E(R)expected return
  • CVrisk per 1% of return; lower is better
Solution
X: R = 21/50 = 42%, 17/50 = 34% → E(R) = 38%, σ = (42 − 34)/2 = 4%, CV = 4/38 = 0.105
Y: R = 27/100 = 27%, 23/100 = 23% → E(R) = 25%, σ = (27 − 23)/2 = 2%, CV = 2/25 = 0.080

Buy X. Its worst outcome (34%) is higher than Y's best (27%), so X gives a higher return in every state. Y has a slightly lower risk per unit of return (CV 0.08 vs 0.105), but no rational investor would accept 25% when X guarantees at least 34%.

Unit 1

Selection using β, R² and α (case)

R² = r², stock move = β × market move

In wordsR² (correlation squared) is the share of risk that comes from the market; a stock moves about beta times whatever the market does.

H11U1 · Q21CaseFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 1 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Four stocks, four statistics, a recession scenario and a bond yield to weigh. You must convert 'average relationship' into r², split variance into systematic and unsystematic, and reject a negative-beta stock that looks attractive.

A recession wave swept across the economy; stock prices are at their bottom level. The investor feels prices cannot fall further and wants to buy two securities. A deep discount bond yields 14.5 percent. Analyse and state why we would take certain securities and reject others.
DetailsStock AStock BStock CStock D
Alpha0.10−0.070.080.05
Beta1.1910.800.90−0.88
Average relationship (r)1.00.71.0−0.72
Variance0.430.430.190.36
Formula in words
Share of risk explained by the market
R² = r²; systematic risk = r² × total variance

In words: R-squared is the correlation squared: the fraction of the stock's total risk that comes from the market.

  • r²systematic (market) share of risk
  • 1 − r²unsystematic (company-specific) share
Alpha and the characteristic line
α = Ȳ − βX̄, Rᵢ = α + βR_m

In words: Alpha is the stock's average return minus beta times the market's average return; the stock's predicted return is alpha plus beta times the market return.

  • Ȳ, X̄average stock return and average market return
  • αstock's return when the market return is zero (starting point)
  • β R_mthe extra return that comes from the market move
Solution

Outlook: prices are at the bottom, so a market recovery is expected. We want stocks that rise with the market (positive, preferably high β), have positive α, and whose movement is reliably market-driven (high r² = systematic share).

Stockr² (systematic share)Systematic variance = r² × varUnsystematicAssessment
A1.000.430Positive α (0.10, highest), β 1.191 (aggressive), fully market-driven → select
B0.490.210.22Negative α (−0.07) → reject
C1.000.190Positive α (0.08), β 0.9, lowest variance, fully market-driven → select
D0.520.190.17Negative β (−0.88): would fall as the market recovers → reject (useful only as a hedge)

Buy A and C. A gives the highest alpha and the most upside in a recovery; C adds positive alpha with the lowest risk. Reject B (negative alpha) and D (negative beta). The 14.5% deep discount bond is the safe alternative: the chosen stocks are justified only because a recovery should lift their returns above that safe yield.

Formula in words
Share of risk explained by the market
R² = r²; systematic risk = r² × total variance

In words: R-squared is the correlation squared: the fraction of the stock's total risk that comes from the market.

  • r²systematic (market) share of risk
  • 1 − r²unsystematic (company-specific) share
Stock move from beta
ΔRᵢ = β × ΔR_m

In words: A stock is expected to move beta times as much as the market.

  • ΔR_mchange in the market index (keep the minus sign for a fall)
  • βmultiplier: >1 amplifies, <1 dampens

Unit 1

Utility, Sharpe and optimal allocation

U = E − ½Aσ², y* = (E − Rf) ÷ (Aσ²)

In wordsUtility is expected return minus half the risk-aversion index times the variance (in decimals). The best share in the risky asset is its extra return divided by risk aversion times its variance.

H13U1 · Q19NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 1 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Three portfolios, decimals throughout (σ = 0.27, not 27), an explanation of why Sharpe stays constant, and an optimisation for y*. Every percentage-vs-decimal slip ruins the utility values.

The return on a stock is 16 percent. The risk-free rate is 7 percent. The standard deviation of the stock is 27 percent. (i) For an investor with a risk-aversion index of 3, find the utility value of portfolios with 40%, 60% and 80% in the risky stock. (ii) What is the Sharpe ratio for each? Should it be the same or different? (iii) Find the proportion of the stock with the highest utility and the utility of that combined portfolio.
Formula in words
Mixing a risky asset with a risk-free asset
E(r_c) = R_f + y[E(r) − R_f], σ_c = y σ

In words: The mix earns the risk-free rate plus the share in the risky asset times its extra return; its risk is just that share times the risky asset's risk.

  • yproportion put in the risky asset
  • E(r) − R_fextra return of the risky asset
  • yσonly the risky part carries risk
Utility
U = E(r) − ½ A σ² (use decimals)

In words: Utility equals the expected return minus a risk penalty, where the penalty is half the investor's risk-aversion index times the variance.

  • E(r)expected return (as a decimal)
  • Ahow much the investor dislikes risk
  • ½Aσ²the risk penalty (σ as a decimal, then squared)
Sharpe ratio (Return to Variability)
S = (R_p − R_f) / σ_p

In words: Sharpe ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by total risk (standard deviation).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return (reward for risk)
  • σ_ptotal risk
  • Sexcess return per 1% of total risk; higher is better
Best proportion in the risky asset
y* = [E(r) − R_f] / (A σ²)

In words: The best share in the risky asset is its extra return divided by risk aversion times its variance.

  • E(r) − R_freward for taking risk
  • Aσ²how much the investor is hurt by that risk
Solution
E(r_c) = R_f + y[E(r) − R_f] = 7 + 9y      σ_c = 27y
U = E − ½Aσ² (decimals), A = 3 → U = E − 1.5σ²
Sharpe = (E(r_c) − R_f)/σ_c
yE(rc) %σc %Utility workingU %Sharpe
40%10.6010.800.106 − 1.5 × 0.108² = 0.08858.850.333
60%12.4016.200.124 − 1.5 × 0.162² = 0.08468.460.333
80%14.2021.600.142 − 1.5 × 0.216² = 0.07207.200.333
(iii) y* = [E(r) − R_f] / (Aσ²) = 0.09 / (3 × 0.27²) = 0.09 / 0.2187 = 0.4115
     E = 7 + 0.4115 × 9 = 10.70%   σ = 0.4115 × 27 = 11.11%
     U = 0.1070 − 1.5 × 0.1111² = 0.0885 = 8.85%

(i) U = 8.85%, 8.46%, 7.20%. (ii) Sharpe = 0.333 for all three: every mix lies on the same capital allocation line, because excess return and σ both scale with y. (iii) Optimal proportion ≈ 41.15% in the stock, giving the maximum utility ≈ 8.85%.

H14NewNumericalNew challenge
Why it's one of the hardest

Your PDF has only one utility question, so this harder one is added. It changes A to 4 (penalty becomes 2σ²) and includes a borrowing case (y = 125%), where students wrongly expect higher utility.

New challenge (not in your PDF). A stock has an expected return of 18% and a standard deviation of 24%; the risk-free rate is 6%. An investor has a risk-aversion index of 4 and can borrow or lend at the risk-free rate. (i) Find the utility of putting 50%, 100% and 125% (borrowing 25%) in the stock. (ii) Show the Sharpe ratio of each. (iii) Find the optimal proportion and its utility, and say whether this investor should borrow.
Formula in words
Mixing a risky asset with a risk-free asset
E(r_c) = R_f + y[E(r) − R_f], σ_c = y σ

In words: The mix earns the risk-free rate plus the share in the risky asset times its extra return; its risk is just that share times the risky asset's risk.

  • yproportion put in the risky asset
  • E(r) − R_fextra return of the risky asset
  • yσonly the risky part carries risk
Utility
U = E(r) − ½ A σ² (use decimals)

In words: Utility equals the expected return minus a risk penalty, where the penalty is half the investor's risk-aversion index times the variance.

  • E(r)expected return (as a decimal)
  • Ahow much the investor dislikes risk
  • ½Aσ²the risk penalty (σ as a decimal, then squared)
Sharpe ratio (Return to Variability)
S = (R_p − R_f) / σ_p

In words: Sharpe ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by total risk (standard deviation).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return (reward for risk)
  • σ_ptotal risk
  • Sexcess return per 1% of total risk; higher is better
Best proportion in the risky asset
y* = [E(r) − R_f] / (A σ²)

In words: The best share in the risky asset is its extra return divided by risk aversion times its variance.

  • E(r) − R_freward for taking risk
  • Aσ²how much the investor is hurt by that risk
Solution
E(r_c) = 6 + 12y      σ_c = 24y      U = E − ½ × 4 × σ² = E − 2σ²  (decimals)

y = 0.50: E = 12%, σ = 12% → U = 0.12 − 2(0.0144) = 0.0912 = 9.12%
y = 1.00: E = 18%, σ = 24% → U = 0.18 − 2(0.0576) = 0.0648 = 6.48%
y = 1.25: E = 21%, σ = 30% → U = 0.21 − 2(0.0900) = 0.0300 = 3.00%
Sharpe = (E − 6)/σ = 12y/24y = 0.50 in every case

y* = (0.18 − 0.06) / (4 × 0.24²) = 0.12 / 0.2304 = 0.5208
E = 6 + 0.5208 × 12 = 12.25%   σ = 0.5208 × 24 = 12.50%
U = 0.1225 − 2 × 0.125² = 0.1225 − 0.03125 = 0.09125 = 9.13%

Utility: 9.12%, 6.48%, 3.00%. Sharpe is 0.50 for all three (same line). The best proportion is about 52% in the stock (U ≈ 9.13%), so this investor should lend (hold 48% risk-free), not borrow: borrowing to hold 125% cuts utility to 3%.

Unit 2

Returns from shares and property

Return = Income + Capital gain; % = Return ÷ Amount invested

In wordsTotal return is the income received (dividend or rent) plus the rise in value, divided by the amount invested.

H15U2 · N2NumericalPractice · from notesOpen in Unit 2 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Three linked figures (dividend income, capital appreciation, total) built from per-share data, then a % on the total investment; mixing per-share and total amounts is the usual slip.

Mr. Rahul buys 500 equity shares of ABC Ltd. at ₹400. During the year the company declares a dividend of ₹12 per share and the price rises to ₹470. Calculate his dividend income, capital appreciation and total return (₹ and %).
Formula in words
Total return on shares
Total return = N × D + N × (P₁ − P₀); % = Total ÷ (N × P₀)

In words: Shareholders earn dividend income plus the rise in the share price; divide by the amount invested for a percentage.

  • N × Ddividend income
  • N × (P₁ − P₀)capital appreciation
  • N × P₀amount invested (base for %)
Solution
Investment = 500 × 400 = ₹2,00,000
Dividend = 500 × 12 = ₹6,000
Capital appreciation = 500 × (470 − 400) = ₹35,000
Total return = 6,000 + 35,000 = ₹41,000 → 41,000 / 2,00,000 = 20.5%

Total return ₹41,000 (20.5%), from both dividend and capital appreciation. A fall in price would have produced a capital loss instead.

H16U2 · N9NumericalPractice · from notesOpen in Unit 2 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Monthly rent must be annualised, amounts are in lakh, and rental yield (5% a year) must be kept separate from capital appreciation earned over ten years.

Mr. Rohan buys an apartment for ₹60 lakh and rents it at ₹25,000 a month. After ten years its value is ₹95 lakh. Calculate the annual rent, rental yield and capital appreciation.
Formula in words
Real estate returns
Annual rent = Monthly rent × 12; Appreciation = Current value − Cost; Yield = Rent ÷ Cost

In words: Property earns rent every year plus the rise in its value when sold.

  • Monthly rent × 12rental income for the year
  • Current value − Costcapital gain
  • Rent ÷ Costrental yield
Solution
Annual rent = 25,000 × 12 = ₹3,00,000
Rental yield = 3,00,000 / 60,00,000 = 5%
Capital appreciation = 95 − 60 = ₹35 lakh (58.3% over 10 years)

₹3 lakh rent a year (5% yield) plus ₹35 lakh appreciation: the dual return of real estate.

Unit 2

Interest and conversion of debt securities

Interest = Face × Coupon, Shares = Debentures × Ratio

In wordsYearly interest is face value times the coupon rate; on conversion, shares received equal debentures times the conversion ratio.

H17U2 · N7NumericalPractice · from notesOpen in Unit 2 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Two phases (interest, then conversion) plus an implied conversion price you must derive yourself; interest stops after conversion.

Mr. Arjun buys 100 convertible debentures of ₹1,000 at 7% interest, convertible after five years into 20 equity shares each. Calculate the interest received before conversion and the shares received. What is the implied conversion price?
Formula in words
Interest on debentures / bonds
Annual interest = Face value invested × Coupon rate

In words: Each year the holder receives the face value times the coupon rate, whatever the issuer's profit; the face value is repaid at maturity.

  • Face value investednumber of units × face value of each
  • Coupon ratefixed annual interest rate
Convertible debentures
Shares received = Debentures × Conversion ratio; Implied price = Face ÷ Ratio

In words: On conversion each debenture becomes a fixed number of shares; dividing face value by that number gives the effective price paid per share.

  • Conversion ratioshares per debenture
  • Face ÷ Ratioimplied conversion price per share
Solution
Investment = ₹1,00,000
Annual interest = 1,00,000 × 7% = ₹7,000 → 5 years = ₹35,000
Shares on conversion = 100 × 20 = 2,000 shares
Implied conversion price = 1,000 / 20 = ₹50 per share

₹7,000 a year for 5 years, then 2,000 shares. Conversion pays off if the share price exceeds ₹50; interest stops after conversion.

H18U2 · N6NumericalPractice · from notesOpen in Unit 2 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Requires total cash over the life, not just the annual coupon, so principal must be added at the end.

Mr. Anand invests ₹5,00,000 in a ten-year Government Security with a 7% annual coupon. Calculate his yearly income and total cash received over the life.
Formula in words
Interest on debentures / bonds
Annual interest = Face value invested × Coupon rate

In words: Each year the holder receives the face value times the coupon rate, whatever the issuer's profit; the face value is repaid at maturity.

  • Face value investednumber of units × face value of each
  • Coupon ratefixed annual interest rate
Solution
Annual coupon = 5,00,000 × 7% = ₹35,000
Over 10 years = 3,50,000 + 5,00,000 principal = ₹8,50,000

₹35,000 a year; principal repaid at maturity: why gilt-edged securities are among the safest long-term avenues.

Unit 3

Bond valuation (present value)

V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In wordsA bond is worth the coupon times the annuity factor plus the face value times the single-sum factor, both at the required return.

H1U3 · Q43NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Two bonds valued at three different rates (six valuations), percentage-sensitivity comparison, and a question that wrongly assumes Bond A is at par at 10%. You must catch the inconsistency.

Two bonds with ₹2,000 face value and 6 years remaining: Bond A 9% coupon, Bond B 6% coupon; market required return 10%. (a) Calculate the values at 10%. (b) Compare and explain why A trades at par while B trades at a discount. (c) Rates rise from 9% to 11%: analyse the impact on both. (d) Which bond declines more in % terms and why?
Formula in words
Bond value (present value)
V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In words: A bond is worth today's value of all its coupons plus today's value of the face value it repays at maturity.

  • C × PVIFA(k, n)present value of n yearly coupons (annuity factor)
  • F × PVIF(k, n)present value of the single face-value repayment
  • krequired return / market yield: the discount rate (not the coupon rate)
  • nyears to maturity
Premium or discount
Coupon rate > k → premium; = k → par; < k → discount

In words: If the bond pays more interest than investors require, they pay above face value; if less, below face value.

  • PremiumV₀ − F when V₀ > F
  • DiscountF − V₀ when V₀ < F
Solution
Bond A @10%: C = 9% × ₹2,000 = ₹180.00
PVIFA(10%, 6) = 4.3553    PVIF(10%, 6) = 0.5645
PV of coupons   = 180.00 × 4.3553 = ₹783.95
PV of principal = 2,000 × 0.5645 = ₹1,128.95
Bond value V₀   = 783.95 + 1,128.95 = ₹1,912.89
Bond B @10%: C = 6% × ₹2,000 = ₹120.00
PVIFA(10%, 6) = 4.3553    PVIF(10%, 6) = 0.5645
PV of coupons   = 120.00 × 4.3553 = ₹522.63
PV of principal = 2,000 × 0.5645 = ₹1,128.95
Bond value V₀   = 522.63 + 1,128.95 = ₹1,651.58
BondValue @ 9%Value @ 11%% change
A (9%)2,000.001,830.78−8.46
B (6%)1,730.841,576.95−8.89

At 10% Bond A is not at par (₹1,912.89, a discount); it is at par only at 9%, its own coupon rate. The question's part (b) appears to assume 9%.

(a) A ₹1,912.89, B ₹1,651.58. (b) Both trade below face at 10%; B's lower coupon makes its discount much larger. At 9%, A is exactly at par. (c) Both fall when rates rise. (d) B falls more (−8.89% vs −8.46%) because its lower coupon gives it a longer duration.

H2U3 · Q36NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Non-standard face value (₹12,000), 10-year factors and coupon/required rates only 1% apart, so the discount is small and any factor misread shows up immediately.

A 10-year corporate bond of LMN Ltd. has a face value of ₹12,000 and an annual coupon rate of 11%. The required return for bonds of similar risk is 12%. Calculate the intrinsic value.
Formula in words
Annual coupon
C = Coupon rate × Face value

In words: The yearly interest in rupees is the coupon rate applied to the face value (never to the market price).

  • Coupon rateprinted interest rate
  • Face valuepar / redemption value
Bond value (present value)
V₀ = C × PVIFA(k, n) + F × PVIF(k, n)

In words: A bond is worth today's value of all its coupons plus today's value of the face value it repays at maturity.

  • C × PVIFA(k, n)present value of n yearly coupons (annuity factor)
  • F × PVIF(k, n)present value of the single face-value repayment
  • krequired return / market yield: the discount rate (not the coupon rate)
  • nyears to maturity
Solution
C = 11% × ₹12,000 = ₹1,320.00
PVIFA(12%, 10) = 5.6502    PVIF(12%, 10) = 0.3220
PV of coupons   = 1,320.00 × 5.6502 = ₹7,458.29
PV of principal = 12,000 × 0.3220 = ₹3,863.68
Bond value V₀   = 7,458.29 + 3,863.68 = ₹11,321.97

Value ₹11,321.97 < face ₹12,000 → discount of ₹678.03, because the coupon rate (11%) is below the required return (12%).

Unit 3

Current yield, YTM and their ordering

CY = C ÷ P, YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n] ÷ [(F + P)/2]

In wordsCurrent yield is coupon divided by price. YTM is coupon plus the yearly share of the gain to maturity, divided by the average of face value and price.

H3U3 · Q33NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

A premium bond, so the familiar ordering reverses (coupon > CY > YTM) and the YTM numerator has a negative amortisation (−₹10). Most practice is on discount bonds, so instinct points the wrong way.

An investor purchases a ₹1,000 bond at ₹1,060. The annual coupon is ₹90 and 6 years remain. Calculate the current yield and explain how the premium price affects the relationship between coupon rate and current yield.
Formula in words
Current yield
CY = Annual coupon ÷ Current market price

In words: Current yield is the yearly coupon as a percentage of the price you pay today.

  • Annual couponrupees of interest a year
  • Market pricewhat you pay now (not face value)
Approximate yield to maturity
YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n] ÷ [(F + P)/2]

In words: YTM is the yearly coupon plus the yearly share of the gain (or loss) to maturity, divided by the average of face value and price.

  • Cannual coupon
  • (F − P)/ngain per year if bought below face (negative if bought above)
  • (F + P)/2average amount invested over the life
Coupon, current yield and YTM
Discount bond: coupon < CY < YTM; Premium bond: coupon > CY > YTM

In words: Below face value every yield is above the coupon rate, and YTM is highest because it adds the gain at maturity; above face value it is the reverse.

  • Coupon ratemeasured on face value
  • CYmeasured on market price
  • YTMincludes gain/loss to maturity and time value
Solution
CY = 90 / 1,060 = 8.49%
Coupon rate = 90 / 1,000 = 9%
Approx YTM = [90 + (1,000 − 1,060)/6] / [(1,000 + 1,060)/2] = 80 / 1,030 = 7.77%

CY 8.49% < coupon 9%. Paying a premium spreads the same ₹90 over a bigger price, so CY falls below the coupon; YTM (7.77%) is lower still because the ₹60 premium is lost at maturity.

H4U3 · Q16NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Five parts in one: coupon, CY, approximate YTM, comparison, and an explanation of why three different rates exist for the same bond.

A 5-year bond with a face value of ₹1,000 pays an annual coupon of ₹80 and is currently selling for ₹950. Calculate: (a) annual coupon, (b) current yield, (c) approximate YTM, (d) compare the coupon rate, current yield and YTM, (e) explain why these three rates differ.
Formula in words
Current yield
CY = Annual coupon ÷ Current market price

In words: Current yield is the yearly coupon as a percentage of the price you pay today.

  • Annual couponrupees of interest a year
  • Market pricewhat you pay now (not face value)
Approximate yield to maturity
YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n] ÷ [(F + P)/2]

In words: YTM is the yearly coupon plus the yearly share of the gain (or loss) to maturity, divided by the average of face value and price.

  • Cannual coupon
  • (F − P)/ngain per year if bought below face (negative if bought above)
  • (F + P)/2average amount invested over the life
Coupon, current yield and YTM
Discount bond: coupon < CY < YTM; Premium bond: coupon > CY > YTM

In words: Below face value every yield is above the coupon rate, and YTM is highest because it adds the gain at maturity; above face value it is the reverse.

  • Coupon ratemeasured on face value
  • CYmeasured on market price
  • YTMincludes gain/loss to maturity and time value
Solution
(a) C = ₹80 (coupon rate 8%)
(b) CY = 80 / 950 = 8.42%
(c) YTM ≈ [80 + (1,000 − 950)/5] / [(1,000 + 950)/2] = 90 / 975 = 9.23%
(d) 8% < 8.42% < 9.23%

(e) The coupon rate is measured on face value, current yield on the lower market price, and YTM also includes the ₹50 capital gain spread over 5 years, so YTM is highest for this discount bond.

Unit 3

Yield to call

P = C × PVIFA(YTC, nc) + CP × PVIF(YTC, nc)

In wordsYield to call is the rate that makes the coupons up to the call date plus the call price worth exactly today's price.

H5U3 · Q17NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

You must set up the equation with the call price (₹1,020, not ₹1,000), solve by trial and interpolation, and then explain how the return would differ if held to maturity.

A callable bond has a face value of ₹1,000, annual coupon rate of 10%, current market price of ₹1,050 and call price of ₹1,020, with 5 years until the call date. Calculate the Yield to Call by equating the current market price with the PV of the coupons and the call price. State whether the investor's return would differ if the bond were held to maturity.
Formula in words
Yield to call
P = C × PVIFA(YTC, n_c) + CP × PVIF(YTC, n_c); YTC ≈ [C + (CP − P)/n_c] ÷ [(CP + P)/2]

In words: Yield to call is the return if the issuer buys the bond back early: same as YTM but with the call price and the years to the call date.

  • CPcall price (replaces face value)
  • n_cyears to call date (replaces maturity)
  • Pcurrent market price
Solution
YTC equation: 1,050 = 100 × PVIFA(YTC, 5) + 1,020 × PVIF(YTC, 5)
Approx YTC = [100 + (1,020 − 1,050)/5] / [(1,020 + 1,050)/2] = 94 / 1,035 = 9.08%
Check at 9%: 100(3.8897) + 1,020(0.6499) = ₹1,051.87 ≈ 1,050 → exact YTC ≈ 9.05%

YTC ≈ 9.05%. Yes, the return would differ if held to maturity: the investor would keep earning ₹100 coupons beyond year 5 and receive ₹1,000 (not ₹1,020) at a later date. (For comparison, a 5-year YTM to ₹1,000 would be ≈ 8.72%.)

H6U3 · Q30NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Same structure with a 4-year call horizon and a premium of only ₹15 over the call price; the negative amortisation (−₹3.75) is small and easy to drop.

A callable bond has face value ₹1,000, coupon 9%, current price ₹1,030, call price ₹1,015 and 4 years to the call date. Set up the YTC equation, estimate the yield to call and state the cash flows used.
Formula in words
Yield to call
P = C × PVIFA(YTC, n_c) + CP × PVIF(YTC, n_c); YTC ≈ [C + (CP − P)/n_c] ÷ [(CP + P)/2]

In words: Yield to call is the return if the issuer buys the bond back early: same as YTM but with the call price and the years to the call date.

  • CPcall price (replaces face value)
  • n_cyears to call date (replaces maturity)
  • Pcurrent market price
Solution
Cash flows: ₹90 at end of years 1–4 plus ₹1,015 (call price) at end of year 4
Equation: 1,030 = 90 × PVIFA(YTC, 4) + 1,015 × PVIF(YTC, 4)
Approx YTC = [90 + (1,015 − 1,030)/4] / [(1,015 + 1,030)/2] = 86.25 / 1,022.5 = 8.44%
Exact by trial ≈ 8.42%

YTC ≈ 8.44% (exact ≈ 8.42%).

Unit 3

Macaulay duration

D = Σ[t × PV(CFₜ)] ÷ Price

In wordsMultiply each year's present value by its year number, add them, and divide by the bond's price: the average waiting time in years.

H7U3 · Q34NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

The cash flows are worded unusually (₹90 in years 1–4 and ₹1,090 in year 4), so students double-count year 4; then a full 5-column table and division.

A bond has annual cash flows of ₹90 at the end of years 1–4 and ₹1,000 principal at the end of year 4 (₹1,090 in year 4). If the discount rate is 10%, calculate the bond price and Macaulay Duration by constructing a cash-flow table.
Formula in words
Macaulay duration
D = Σ [t × PV(CFₜ)] ÷ Price, where Price = Σ PV(CFₜ)

In words: Duration is the average time you wait for the bond's money: multiply each year's present value by its year number, add, then divide by the price.

  • CFₜcash flow in year t (last year = coupon + face value)
  • PV(CFₜ)CFₜ × PVIF at the YTM
  • t × PVeach PV weighted by when it arrives
  • ÷ Priceturns the total into an average time in years
Solution
Year tCash flow (₹)PVIF @ 10%PV (₹)t × PV
190.000.909181.8281.82
290.000.826474.38148.76
390.000.751367.62202.85
41,090.000.6830744.482,977.94
Total968.303,411.37
Price = ₹968.30
D = 3,411.37 / 968.30 = 3.5230

Price ₹968.30; Macaulay duration 3.52 years.

Why it's one of the hardest

Four separate deliverables (PV of each flow, price, time-weighted PVs, duration) and the bond is not at par, so you cannot shortcut the price to ₹1,000.

A 4-year bond with a face value of ₹1,000 pays an annual coupon of 8%. The YTM is 10%. Calculate: (a) PV of each annual cash flow, (b) bond price, (c) time-weighted PV of each cash flow, and (d) Macaulay Duration.

Macaulay duration
D = Σ [t × PV(CFₜ)] ÷ Price, where Price = Σ PV(CFₜ)

In words: Duration is the average time you wait for the bond's money: multiply each year's present value by its year number, add, then divide by the price.

  • CFₜcash flow in year t (last year = coupon + face value)
  • PV(CFₜ)CFₜ × PVIF at the YTM
  • t × PVeach PV weighted by when it arrives
  • ÷ Priceturns the total into an average time in years
Solution

Unit 3

Modified duration and price change

MD = D ÷ (1 + y), %ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy

In wordsModified duration is Macaulay duration divided by one plus the yield; the price changes by about minus that number times the change in yield.

H9U3 · Q19NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

A four-link chain: Macaulay → modified → % change → rupee change → new price. An error in the first link carries through all four answers.

A bond has a Macaulay Duration of 6 years, YTM of 10% and current market price of ₹1,050. Calculate: (a) Modified Duration, (b) estimated % price change if yield increases by 2%, (c) estimated rupee change, (d) estimated new market price.
Formula in words
Modified duration
MD = Macaulay D ÷ (1 + y)

In words: Modified duration is Macaulay duration divided by one plus the yield.

  • DMacaulay duration in years
  • yYTM as a decimal (1 + 0.08, not 1 + 8)
Price change from a yield change
%ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy, ₹ΔP = %ΔP × P, New P = P + ₹ΔP

In words: The price changes by about minus modified duration times the change in yield; apply that % to the price for the rupee change.

  • −yield up → price down; yield down → price up
  • Δychange in yield in % points (0.75 for 0.75%)
  • × Pconvert the % change into rupees
Solution
(a) MD = 6 / 1.10 = 5.4545
(b) %ΔP ≈ −5.4545 × 2 = −10.91%
(c) ₹ΔP = −10.91% × 1,050 = −₹114.55
(d) New price ≈ 1,050 − 114.55 = ₹935.45

MD = 5.45; price falls about 10.91% (₹114.55) to ≈ ₹935.45.

H10U3 · Q28NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

The yield falls, so the minus sign in the formula produces a price rise; students used to falling prices often report −4.125%.

A bond has a Modified Duration of 5.5 years and current price ₹1,100. If yield falls by 0.75%, estimate the % price change, rupee change and approximate new price.
Formula in words
Price change from a yield change
%ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy, ₹ΔP = %ΔP × P, New P = P + ₹ΔP

In words: The price changes by about minus modified duration times the change in yield; apply that % to the price for the rupee change.

  • −yield up → price down; yield down → price up
  • Δychange in yield in % points (0.75 for 0.75%)
  • × Pconvert the % change into rupees
Solution
%ΔP ≈ −5.5 × (−0.75) = +4.125%
₹ΔP = 4.125% × 1,100 = +₹45.38
New price ≈ 1,100 + 45.38 = ₹1,145.38

Price rises about 4.125% (₹45.38) to ≈ ₹1,145.38.

Unit 3

Zero-coupon bonds

P = F ÷ (1 + k)ⁿ, D = n

In wordsPrice is the face value discounted once for n years; with a single payment, duration equals maturity.

H11U3 · Q27NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Needs both price and duration, plus a conceptual explanation of why price depends on k but duration does not.

A 5-year zero-coupon bond has face value ₹1,000 and a required return of 9%. Calculate its present value and Macaulay Duration. Explain the difference between price determination and duration for a zero-coupon bond.
Formula in words
Zero-coupon bond
P = F ÷ (1 + k)ⁿ, D = n

In words: A zero-coupon bond's price is just the face value discounted once; since all its money comes at maturity, its duration equals its maturity.

  • Fface value paid at the end
  • (1 + k)ⁿdiscounting for n years
  • D = nsingle payment → average time = maturity
Solution
P = 1,000 / (1.09)⁵ = 1,000 × 0.6499 = ₹649.93
D = 5 years (single cash flow at maturity)

Price ₹649.93; duration 5 years. Price depends on the discount rate (it falls as k rises); duration depends only on timing, and with one payment it equals maturity regardless of k.

H12U3 · Q10NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

No numbers to crunch: the marks are entirely for explaining why duration equals maturity, which many students cannot put into words.

A zero-coupon bond has a maturity period of 6 years. Calculate its Macaulay Duration and explain why its duration is equal to its maturity.
Formula in words
Zero-coupon bond
P = F ÷ (1 + k)ⁿ, D = n

In words: A zero-coupon bond's price is just the face value discounted once; since all its money comes at maturity, its duration equals its maturity.

  • Fface value paid at the end
  • (1 + k)ⁿdiscounting for n years
  • D = nsingle payment → average time = maturity
Solution
Only one cash flow: face value at t = 6
D = (6 × PV) / PV = 6

Macaulay duration = 6 years. A zero-coupon bond pays nothing until maturity, so 100% of its present value is received at year 6; the weighted-average time of its cash flows is therefore exactly its maturity.

Unit 3

Constant growth DDM (Gordon)

P₀ = D₀(1 + g) ÷ (k − g)

In wordsGrow the last dividend by one year, then divide by the required return minus the growth rate.

H13U3 · Q41NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

The printed second rate equals the first (14%), so you must spot the typo, still give the correct value, and explain the effect of a higher required return.

PQR Ltd. recently paid a dividend of ₹7 per share, growing at 8% a year. The investor initially requires 14%, but due to higher perceived risk the required return rises (printed as 14%). Calculate the intrinsic value under both rates and analyse the effect.
Formula in words
Constant growth DDM (Gordon)
P₀ = D₁ ÷ (k − g), D₁ = D₀ × (1 + g)

In words: Share value is next year's dividend divided by the required return minus the growth rate.

  • D₀dividend just paid / currently paid
  • D₁next year's dividend: grow D₀ first
  • k − grequired return minus growth (decimals; k must exceed g)
Solution
D₁ = 7 × 1.08 = ₹7.56
At 14%: P₀ = 7.56 / (0.14 − 0.08) = 7.56 / 0.06 = ₹126.00
If the second rate is 16% (illustration): 7.56 / 0.08 = ₹94.50

The question prints both rates as 14%, which looks like a typo. At 14% the value is ₹126 in both cases; any higher rate lowers the value (e.g., 16% gives ₹94.50).

₹126.00 at 14%. An increase in required return reduces intrinsic value, because future dividends are discounted more heavily.

H14U3 · Q46NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Two valuations and a comparison: because k − g is small, a 3-point change in k cuts value by 30%, which you must explain as well as compute.

ST Limited's dividends grow at 7% a year; it recently paid ₹12. Required return 14%, later rising to 17%. (a) Value at 14%? (b) Value at 17%? (c) Explain the effect of higher required return.
Formula in words
Constant growth DDM (Gordon)
P₀ = D₁ ÷ (k − g), D₁ = D₀ × (1 + g)

In words: Share value is next year's dividend divided by the required return minus the growth rate.

  • D₀dividend just paid / currently paid
  • D₁next year's dividend: grow D₀ first
  • k − grequired return minus growth (decimals; k must exceed g)
Solution
D₁ = 12 × 1.07 = ₹12.84
(a) 12.84 / 0.07 = ₹183.43
(b) 12.84 / 0.10 = ₹128.40

(c) Value falls from ₹183.43 to ₹128.40 (−30%). A higher required return widens k − g, so the same dividends are worth less.

Unit 3

Preference shares and the valuation principle

V = D ÷ k, r = D ÷ P, IV vs MP

In wordsValue is the fixed dividend divided by the required return; return at market price is the dividend divided by the price. Buy if value exceeds price.

H15U3 · Q44NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Four parts linking two tests (IV vs MP, and yield vs required return) that must agree; you must interpret, not just compute.

Neha considers a preference share paying ₹10 a year indefinitely. Required return 10%; market price ₹95. (a) Intrinsic value? (b) Undervalued or overvalued? (c) Expected return at ₹95? (d) Interpret against the 10% required return.
Formula in words
Preference share value (zero growth)
V = D ÷ k

In words: A share paying the same dividend forever is worth that dividend divided by the return you require.

  • Dfixed annual dividend
  • krequired return (as a decimal)
Return at market price
r = D ÷ P

In words: Buying at the market price, your return is the dividend divided by the price you pay.

  • Dannual dividend
  • Pmarket price
  • r vs kr > k means the share is undervalued
Valuation principle
IV > MP → undervalued → buy; IV < MP → overvalued → sell

In words: Buy a security when it is worth more than its price; sell when its price is above its worth.

  • IVintrinsic value from a valuation model
  • MPmarket price
  • (IV − MP)/MPmargin as a % of price
Solution
(a) V = 10 / 0.10 = ₹100
(b) ₹100 > ₹95 → undervalued
(c) r = 10 / 95 = 10.53%

(d) 10.53% exceeds the required 10%, so buying at ₹95 is attractive.

H16U3 · Q24NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Combines value, dividend yield at market price and the buy/sell verdict; the 12.22% yield vs 11% requirement must be connected to IV > MP.

A preference share pays a fixed annual dividend of ₹11 indefinitely. The required rate of return is 11% and the market price is ₹90. Calculate intrinsic value and the investor's dividend yield at the market price. Compare intrinsic value with market price.
Formula in words
Preference share value (zero growth)
V = D ÷ k

In words: A share paying the same dividend forever is worth that dividend divided by the return you require.

  • Dfixed annual dividend
  • krequired return (as a decimal)
Return at market price
r = D ÷ P

In words: Buying at the market price, your return is the dividend divided by the price you pay.

  • Dannual dividend
  • Pmarket price
  • r vs kr > k means the share is undervalued
Valuation principle
IV > MP → undervalued → buy; IV < MP → overvalued → sell

In words: Buy a security when it is worth more than its price; sell when its price is above its worth.

  • IVintrinsic value from a valuation model
  • MPmarket price
  • (IV − MP)/MPmargin as a % of price
Solution
Intrinsic value = D / k = 11 / 0.11 = ₹100
Dividend yield at market price = 11 / 90 = 12.22%
