Unit 3

P/E valuation

P = EPS × P/E, P₁ = EPS(1 + g) × P/E

In wordsPrice is earnings per share times the P/E multiple; next year's price is next year's EPS (grown by g) times the same multiple.

H17U3 · Q45NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

The stem says 11% growth but part (b) says 10%. You must notice the conflict, answer both, and explain why price growth equals EPS growth at a constant P/E.

PQR Ltd. has EPS ₹6 and a P/E of 14; earnings expected to grow by 11% (part b says 10%) with the P/E unchanged. (a) Current price? (b) Forecast EPS? (c) Forecast price? (d) Effect of EPS growth on price with constant P/E?
Formula in words
P/E valuation
P = EPS × P/E; EPS₁ = EPS × (1 + g); P₁ = EPS₁ × P/E

In words: Price is earnings per share times the multiple the market pays; next year's price uses next year's EPS with the same multiple.

  • EPSearnings per share
  • P/Emultiple of comparable companies
  • (1 + g)grows EPS for next year
Solution
(a) P = 6 × 14 = ₹84
(b) EPS₁ at 10% = 6 × 1.10 = ₹6.60     [at 11% = ₹6.66]
(c) P₁ = 6.60 × 14 = ₹92.40           [at 11% = ₹93.24]

The stem says 11% growth and part (b) says 10%; both are shown.

(d) With a constant P/E, the share price grows at exactly the EPS growth rate.

H18U3 · Q23NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 3 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Three linked figures (current price, next EPS, forecast price) where using EPS₁ for the current price is a common slip.

PQR Ltd. reports EPS of ₹6 and the appropriate P/E ratio is 18. Earnings are expected to grow by 12% next year. Calculate the current estimated share price, next year's EPS and the forecast share price using the same P/E ratio.
Formula in words
P/E valuation
P = EPS × P/E; EPS₁ = EPS × (1 + g); P₁ = EPS₁ × P/E

In words: Price is earnings per share times the multiple the market pays; next year's price uses next year's EPS with the same multiple.

  • EPSearnings per share
  • P/Emultiple of comparable companies
  • (1 + g)grows EPS for next year
Solution
Current price = EPS × P/E = 6 × 18 = ₹108
EPS₁ = 6 × 1.12 = ₹6.72
Forecast price = 6.72 × 18 = ₹120.96

₹108 now; EPS ₹6.72 next year; forecast price ₹120.96 (12% higher, same as EPS growth).

Unit 4

RSI

RS = Avg gain ÷ Avg loss, RSI = 100 − 100 ÷ (1 + RS)

In wordsDivide average gain by average loss to get RS; RSI is 100 minus 100 divided by one plus RS (above 70 overbought, below 30 oversold).

H19U4 · N3CasePractice · from notesOpen in Unit 4 →
Why it's one of the hardest

No calculation, just two readings and a trend-reversal judgement with confirmation signals: the marks are for precise interpretation, which is harder than the arithmetic.

Maya sees ABC Ltd.'s 14-day RSI rise to 74 during a rapid rally, then fall to 27 after a sharp decline. Interpret both readings and the signal she should watch for.
Formula in words
Relative Strength Index
RS = Average gain ÷ Average loss; RSI = 100 − [100 ÷ (1 + RS)]

In words: First divide the average gain by the average loss; then RSI is 100 minus 100 divided by one plus that ratio.

  • RShow strong the up-moves are compared with the down-moves
  • 100 ÷ (1 + RS)subtract this from 100 (don't stop here)
  • > 70 / < 30overbought / oversold
Solution

RSI 74: overbought; the rally may have outpaced fundamentals, so a correction or consolidation is likely (cautionary sell signal). RSI 27: oversold; the decline may be excessive and a rebound is possible (cautionary buy signal). The swing from one extreme to the other means she should watch for RSI turning up from below 30 (bullish) or down from above 70 (bearish), confirmed by price action, volume or a moving-average crossover, before acting.

H20U4 · N2NumericalPractice · from notesOpen in Unit 4 →
Why it's one of the hardest

RS is a fraction (0.37), so 100 ÷ 1.37 must be computed carefully; students who stop at 73 report the wrong zone.

A stock's 14-day average gain is ₹0.50 and average loss ₹1.35. Calculate RSI and interpret.
Formula in words
Relative Strength Index
RS = Average gain ÷ Average loss; RSI = 100 − [100 ÷ (1 + RS)]

In words: First divide the average gain by the average loss; then RSI is 100 minus 100 divided by one plus that ratio.

  • RShow strong the up-moves are compared with the down-moves
  • 100 ÷ (1 + RS)subtract this from 100 (don't stop here)
  • > 70 / < 30overbought / oversold
Solution
RS = 0.50 / 1.35 = 0.37
RSI = 100 − [100 / 1.37] = 100 − 73.0 = 27.0

RSI = 27 < 30 → oversold: caution / potential buy or upward reversal.

Unit 4

Moving averages

SMA = ΣP ÷ n, WMA = Σ(P × w) ÷ Σw, EMA = Pk + EMAy(1 − k)

In wordsSMA adds the last n prices and divides by n; WMA weights newer prices more; EMA takes a k-share of today's price and the rest from yesterday's EMA, with k = 2 ÷ (n + 1).

H21U4 · N4NumericalPractice · from notesOpen in Unit 4 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Three different averages plus a rolled-forward SMA and an EMA that must be seeded; WMA weights must run oldest = 1 to newest = 5.

Closing prices over five days are ₹100, 102, 101, 105, 107. Calculate the 5-day SMA and WMA. On day 6 the price is ₹110: calculate the new SMA and the EMA (n = 5, seeded with the SMA). (Illustrative data.)
Formula in words
Simple moving average
SMA = Sum of last n closing prices ÷ n

In words: Add the last n closing prices and divide by n; every day counts equally.

  • nnumber of days in the window
  • slidenext day drop the oldest price and add the newest
Weighted moving average
WMA = Σ(Price × Weight) ÷ Σ Weights

In words: Multiply each price by its weight (newest gets the biggest weight), add them, and divide by the total of the weights.

  • Weights1 for the oldest … n for the newest
  • Σ Weights1 + 2 + … + n
Exponential moving average
EMA = Price × k + Previous EMA × (1 − k), k = 2 ÷ (n + 1)

In words: Today's EMA takes a slice k of today's price and the rest from yesterday's EMA.

  • ksmoothing constant = 2/(n + 1)
  • Previous EMAstart with the SMA if no EMA exists
Solution
SMA(5) = (100 + 102 + 101 + 105 + 107)/5 = 515/5 = 103.00
WMA(5) = (100×1 + 102×2 + 101×3 + 105×4 + 107×5) / (1+2+3+4+5)
       = (100 + 204 + 303 + 420 + 535) / 15 = 1,562 / 15 = 104.13
Day 6 SMA = (102 + 101 + 105 + 107 + 110)/5 = 525/5 = 105.00
k = 2/(5 + 1) = 0.3333
EMA = 110 × 0.3333 + 103 × 0.6667 = 36.67 + 68.67 = 105.33

SMA 103, WMA 104.13; day 6: SMA 105.00, EMA 105.33. The EMA reacts most to the jump, the SMA least.

H22U4 · N5NumericalPractice · from notesOpen in Unit 4 →
Why it's one of the hardest

You must identify which average crossed which, in which direction, and name the signal; reversing short and long gives the opposite verdict.

A stock's 50-day moving average rises from 480 to 505 while its 200-day average is 500. Identify the signal. (Illustrative.)
Formula in words
Moving-average crossover
Short MA crosses above long MA → golden cross (buy); below → death cross (sell)

In words: When the faster average climbs over the slower one the trend is turning up; when it drops under, the trend is turning down.

  • Short MAe.g. 50-day
  • Long MAe.g. 200-day
Solution
Before: 50-day (480) < 200-day (500)
After:  50-day (505) > 200-day (500)  → shorter average crossed ABOVE the longer

Golden cross: a bullish (buy) signal. Confirm with volume and check that RSI is not already overbought.

Unit 4

Sentiment and strength ratios

CI = High-grade yield ÷ Low-grade yield, RS ratio = Stock ÷ Index

In wordsConfidence index is the safe-bond yield divided by the risky-bond yield; relative strength is the stock's price divided by the index. Watch whether each ratio is rising or falling.

H23U4 · N6NumericalPractice · from notesOpen in Unit 4 →
Why it's one of the hardest

The ratio is easy to invert, and a falling low-grade yield produces a rising index, the opposite of what intuition suggests.

Average yield on high-grade bonds is 7.2%; the average yield on lower-grade bonds falls from 8.0% to 7.6%. Calculate the Confidence Index before and after and interpret. (Illustrative.)
Formula in words
Confidence Index
CI = Average yield on high-grade bonds ÷ Average yield on lower-grade bonds

In words: Divide the yield on safe bonds by the yield on riskier bonds; a rising ratio means investors are more confident.

  • High-grade yieldlower, safe-bond yield
  • Low-grade yieldhigher, risky-bond yield
  • Rising CIgap narrowing → bullish
Solution
CI before = 7.2 / 8.0 = 0.900
CI after  = 7.2 / 7.6 = 0.947

The CI is rising (yield gap narrowing): investors are more confident and accept less extra yield for risk, a bullish signal.

H24U4 · N7NumericalPractice · from notesOpen in Unit 4 →
Why it's one of the hardest

The ratios change only in the fourth decimal place, so you must compute precisely and read the trend, not the level.

A stock is at ₹200 when the index is 20,000, and at ₹210 when the index is 20,500. Calculate the relative strength ratio and interpret. (Illustrative.)
Formula in words
Relative strength ratio
RS ratio = Price of security ÷ Price of index

In words: Divide the stock's price by the index on each date; a rising ratio means the stock is beating the market.

  • Trendwhat matters is whether the ratio is rising or falling
Solution
RS ratio = Price ÷ Index
Before: 200 / 20,000 = 0.01000
After:  210 / 20,500 = 0.01024

The ratio is rising: the stock is outperforming the index (gaining relative strength), a basis for momentum selection.

Unit 5 (CAPM also in Unit 1)

CAPM and mispricing

Required = Rf + β(Rm − Rf), α = Expected − Required

In wordsRequired return is the risk-free rate plus beta times the market's extra return; alpha is expected minus required (positive = underpriced).

H25U5 · Q28NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 5 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Every security comes out overpriced, yet you must still recommend an allocation. Students expect at least one 'buy' and second-guess correct arithmetic.

Rf 6%, Rm 14%. Calculate the required return and alpha for each security, identify under/overpriced, analyse implications and recommend which should get greater allocation.
SecurityBetaExpected return
A0.8010%
B1.2015%
C1.5017%
Formula in words
CAPM required return
E(R) = R_f + β(R_m − R_f)

In words: Required return equals the risk-free rate plus beta times the market's extra return over the risk-free rate.

  • R_freward for waiting (risk-free rate)
  • R_m − R_fmarket risk premium: extra return for bearing market risk
  • β ×scaled up or down for this stock's market risk
Undervalued or overvalued
α = Expected return − Required (CAPM) return

In words: If the stock is expected to earn more than CAPM says it should, it is undervalued; if less, it is overvalued.

  • Expectedwhat the stock is forecast to earn
  • RequiredCAPM return for its beta
  • α > 0undervalued, above the SML → buy
  • α < 0overvalued, below the SML → avoid/sell
Solution
SecurityβExpectedRequiredAlphaVerdict
A0.8010%12.40%−2.40%Overpriced
B1.2015%15.60%−0.60%Overpriced
C1.5017%18.00%−1.00%Overpriced

All three lie below the SML (negative alpha), so all are overpriced; none earns enough for its systematic risk. If one must be held, B is least overpriced (−0.6%) and deserves the larger allocation; A (−2.4%) is the least attractive.

H26U5 · Q33CaseFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 5 →
Why it's one of the hardest

The alphas are tiny (+0.2% and −1.7%) and the market premium is only 5%, so one slip flips the verdict; you also have to explain and position both stocks on the SML in words.

Vikram evaluates Falcon Ltd. (β 0.90, expected 10.70%) and Phoenix Ltd. (β 1.30, expected 10.80%). Rf = 6%, expected market return = 11%. Apply CAPM, explain the SML and position the two stocks, and recommend one.
Formula in words
CAPM required return
E(R) = R_f + β(R_m − R_f)

In words: Required return equals the risk-free rate plus beta times the market's extra return over the risk-free rate.

  • R_freward for waiting (risk-free rate)
  • R_m − R_fmarket risk premium: extra return for bearing market risk
  • β ×scaled up or down for this stock's market risk
Security Market Line
Required = R_f + β(R_m − R_f); above the line if Expected > Required

In words: Plot required return against beta; stocks expected to earn more than the line are underpriced, less are overpriced.

  • InterceptR_f
  • Slopemarket risk premium R_m − R_f
Solution
StockβExpectedRequiredAlphaPosition
Falcon0.9010.70%6 + 0.90 × 5 = 10.50%0.20%Above SML (underpriced)
Phoenix1.3010.80%6 + 1.30 × 5 = 12.50%−1.70%Below SML (overpriced)

SML: plots required return against beta, starting at Rf = 6% with slope equal to the market premium (5%). Stocks above it offer more than their risk requires; stocks below it offer less.

Prefer Falcon: it lies slightly above the SML (+0.2%), adequately compensating its lower systematic risk. Phoenix lies 1.7% below the SML: its higher beta is not rewarded.

Unit 5 (NAV also in Unit 2)

NAV-based fund return

R = (NAV₁ − NAV₀ + D) ÷ NAV₀

In wordsFund return is the change in NAV plus the payout per unit, divided by the opening NAV.

H27U5 · Q25NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 5 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Two funds plus four discussion parts: you must argue that the higher-return fund is not necessarily the better investment and name the risk-adjusted measures that should be used.

(a) Return of each fund using the NAV approach; (b) compare absolute returns; (c) does the higher-return fund necessarily represent the better investment? (d) importance of risk-adjusted measures; (e) other measures to consider.
Fund AFund B
Opening NAV₹20₹30
Closing NAV₹24₹34
Distribution₹1.50₹1.00
Formula in words
NAV-based return
R = (Closing NAV − Opening NAV + Distribution) ÷ Opening NAV

In words: A fund's return is the rise in NAV plus the payout per unit, divided by the starting NAV.

  • Closing − Opening NAVchange in unit value
  • Distributioncash paid out per unit
  • ÷ Opening NAVbase for the %
Solution
A: (24 − 20 + 1.50) / 20 = 5.50 / 20 = 27.50%
B: (34 − 30 + 1.00) / 30 = 5.00 / 30 = 16.67%

(b) A earned 10.83 points more. (c) Not necessarily: A may simply have taken more risk. (d) Risk-adjusted measures show return per unit of risk and whether the manager added value beyond what the risk warrants. (e) Sharpe ratio, Treynor ratio, Jensen's alpha, and comparison with a benchmark index.

H28U5 · Q5NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 5 →
Why it's one of the hardest

The distribution (₹1.20) is easy to leave out, and dividing by the closing NAV instead of the opening NAV gives a believable but wrong 15%.

A portfolio has an opening NAV of ₹25, closing NAV of ₹28 and distributes ₹1.20 per unit. Calculate the return using the NAV approach.
Formula in words
NAV-based return
R = (Closing NAV − Opening NAV + Distribution) ÷ Opening NAV

In words: A fund's return is the rise in NAV plus the payout per unit, divided by the starting NAV.

  • Closing − Opening NAVchange in unit value
  • Distributioncash paid out per unit
  • ÷ Opening NAVbase for the %
Solution
R = (NAV₁ − NAV₀ + D) / NAV₀ = (28 − 25 + 1.20) / 25 = 4.20 / 25 = 0.168

Return = 16.8%

Unit 5

Portfolio weights and expected return

w = Amount ÷ Total, Rp = Σ wR

In wordsEach weight is the money in that security divided by the total; portfolio return is each return times its weight, added up.

H29U5 · Q20NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 5 →
Why it's one of the hardest

After the first answer, the weights change and the remaining 50% must be re-split proportionately; students often leave A and C at 30% and the weights no longer add to 1.

Calculate the expected return using the weighted-average approach, analyse contributions, and explain what happens if the allocation to B increases from 40% to 50% (others adjusted proportionately).
SecurityReturnWeight
A12%30%
B15%40%
C10%30%
Formula in words
Expected portfolio return
R_p = Σ wᵢRᵢ

In words: Portfolio return is each security's return times its weight, added together.

  • wᵢweight (decimal)
  • Rᵢexpected return of the security
  • wᵢRᵢthat security's contribution
Solution
A: 0.30 × 12 = 3.6   B: 0.40 × 15 = 6.0   C: 0.30 × 10 = 3.0   → R_p = 12.6%

New: B = 50%; remaining 50% split A:C in 30:30 → A = 25%, C = 25%
R_p = 0.25 × 12 + 0.50 × 15 + 0.25 × 10 = 3.0 + 7.5 + 2.5 = 13.0%

Rp = 12.6%, rising to 13.0% because more money moves into B, the highest-return security.

H30U5 · Q16NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 5 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Weights come out as recurring fractions (5/12, 7/12); rounding too early shifts the answer.

An investor invests ₹5,00,000 in Security P and ₹7,00,000 in Security Q, with expected returns of 13% and 16%. Calculate the expected portfolio return and analyse each contribution.
Formula in words
Portfolio weights
wᵢ = Amount in i ÷ Total investment

In words: Each weight is the money in that security divided by the total money invested.

  • Σ wweights must add to 1
Expected portfolio return
R_p = Σ wᵢRᵢ

In words: Portfolio return is each security's return times its weight, added together.

  • wᵢweight (decimal)
  • Rᵢexpected return of the security
  • wᵢRᵢthat security's contribution
Solution
Total = ₹12,00,000 → w_P = 5/12 = 0.4167, w_Q = 7/12 = 0.5833
P: 0.4167 × 13 = 5.42%
Q: 0.5833 × 16 = 9.33%
R_p = 14.75%

Rp = 14.75%; Q supplies about 63% of the return.

Unit 5

Two-asset portfolio risk

σ²p = wA²σA² + wB²σB² + 2wAwBρσAσB

In wordsPortfolio variance is each weight squared times its variance, plus twice both weights times the correlation times both standard deviations.

H31U5 · Q31CaseFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 5 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Decimal SDs (11.2, 15.8), a distracting five-year data table, and three essay parts on the efficient frontier and on whether the mix beats holding N alone.

Ritika's advisor considers Security M (expected 10%, SD 11.2%) and N (9%, SD 15.8%), correlation 0.42, allocation 70:30. (Five-year history: M 11, 6, 4, 13, 16; N 12, −5, 14, 10, 14.) Calculate the expected return and SD of the 70:30 portfolio; explain the efficient frontier and the role of diversification and correlation; analyse whether the 70:30 portfolio beats investing entirely in N.
Formula in words
Expected portfolio return
R_p = Σ wᵢRᵢ

In words: Portfolio return is each security's return times its weight, added together.

  • wᵢweight (decimal)
  • Rᵢexpected return of the security
  • wᵢRᵢthat security's contribution
Two-asset portfolio risk
σ²_p = w_A²σ_A² + w_B²σ_B² + 2 w_A w_B ρ σ_A σ_B, σ_p = √σ²_p

In words: Portfolio variance is A's weight-squared times its variance, plus B's, plus twice both weights times the correlation times both standard deviations; take the square root for risk.

  • w_A²σ_A²A's own risk contribution
  • w_B²σ_B²B's own risk contribution
  • 2w_Aw_Bρσ_Aσ_Bhow they move together; smaller when ρ is low
  • √square root gives σ_p in %
Solution
(a) R_p = 0.7 × 10 + 0.3 × 9 = 9.70%
(b) σ²_p = w²σ² + w²σ² + 2wwρσσ
    = 0.7² × 11.2² + 0.3² × 15.8² + 2 × 0.7 × 0.3 × 0.42 × 11.2 × 15.8
    = 61.47 + 22.47 + 31.22 = 115.15
    σ_p = √115.15 = 10.73%
Weighted average σ (if ρ were +1) = 0.7 × 11.2 + 0.3 × 15.8 = 12.58%
Diversification benefit = 12.58 − 10.73 = 1.85 percentage points

Use the given SDs and correlation. (The five-year data give means of 10% and 9%, matching the stated returns.)

Efficient frontier: the set of portfolios offering the highest return for each level of risk. Combining assets with correlation below +1 bends the frontier to the left; the lower the correlation, the larger the risk reduction and the more valuable the frontier's portfolios.

70:30 portfolio: Rp = 9.70%, σp = 10.73%. Versus N alone (9%, 15.8%) it gives a higher return with much lower risk, so it dominates N and is preferable for Ritika.

H32U5 · Q22NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 5 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Weights 45/55 and ρ = 0.35 give awkward squares (0.2025, 0.3025); you also have to quantify the diversification benefit.

Two securities with correlation 0.35. (a) Expected portfolio return. (b) Portfolio variance and SD. (c) Explain the effect of correlation and evaluate the diversification benefit.
Security CSecurity D
Weight45%55%
Expected return16%20%
SD12%18%
Formula in words
Expected portfolio return
R_p = Σ wᵢRᵢ

In words: Portfolio return is each security's return times its weight, added together.

  • wᵢweight (decimal)
  • Rᵢexpected return of the security
  • wᵢRᵢthat security's contribution
Two-asset portfolio risk
σ²_p = w_A²σ_A² + w_B²σ_B² + 2 w_A w_B ρ σ_A σ_B, σ_p = √σ²_p

In words: Portfolio variance is A's weight-squared times its variance, plus B's, plus twice both weights times the correlation times both standard deviations; take the square root for risk.

  • w_A²σ_A²A's own risk contribution
  • w_B²σ_B²B's own risk contribution
  • 2w_Aw_Bρσ_Aσ_Bhow they move together; smaller when ρ is low
  • √square root gives σ_p in %
Diversification benefit
Benefit = (w_Aσ_A + w_Bσ_B) − σ_p

In words: The benefit is how much lower the portfolio's risk is than the simple weighted average of the two risks.

  • w_Aσ_A + w_Bσ_Bportfolio risk if correlation were +1
  • σ_pactual portfolio risk
Solution
(a) R_p = 0.45 × 16 + 0.55 × 20 = 18.20%
(b) σ²_p = w²σ² + w²σ² + 2wwρσσ
    = 0.45² × 12.0² + 0.55² × 18.0² + 2 × 0.45 × 0.55 × 0.35 × 12.0 × 18.0
    = 29.16 + 98.01 + 37.42 = 164.59
    σ_p = √164.59 = 12.83%
Weighted average σ (if ρ were +1) = 0.45 × 12.0 + 0.55 × 18.0 = 15.30%
Diversification benefit = 15.30 − 12.83 = 2.47 percentage points

Rp = 18.20%, σp = 12.83%. Low positive correlation cuts risk by 2.47 points versus the weighted average, a clear diversification benefit.

Unit 5

Sharpe, Treynor and Jensen

S = (Rp − Rf) ÷ σ, T = (Rp − Rf) ÷ β, α = Rp − CAPM

In wordsSharpe is excess return divided by standard deviation; Treynor is excess return divided by beta; Jensen's alpha is actual return minus the CAPM return.

H33U5 · Q30NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 5 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Ten years of data: four columns to average, two SDs to compute, fund and market versions of both ratios, then a verdict. The longest calculation in the syllabus.

Ten years of data for a mutual fund, the market index and government securities. Calculate the Treynor and Sharpe ratios, rank the fund under both, analyse differences, evaluate performance relative to systematic and total risk, and say whether the manager's performance is satisfactory.
YearFund %Fund βMarket %Govt. sec %
113.851.25−10.004.76
228.001.2021.004.21
335.001.1811.055.21
411.251.20−7.506.00
524.001.224.006.50
66.851.3214.314.35
71.201.2718.953.85
821.001.2514.506.15
910.181.109.257.50
1017.650.9520.006.00
Formula in words
Multi-year averages
R̄ = ΣR ÷ n, σ = √[Σ(R − R̄)² ÷ n]

In words: Average each column over the years, then measure spread around the average for the standard deviation.

  • nnumber of years
  • σuse n (or n − 1 for a sample) consistently
Treynor ratio (Return to Volatility)
T = (R_p − R_f) ÷ β_p

In words: Treynor ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by beta (market risk).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return
  • β_psystematic risk
  • Market benchmarkR_m − R_f, because the market's β = 1
Sharpe ratio (Return to Variability)
S = (R_p − R_f) / σ_p

In words: Sharpe ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by total risk (standard deviation).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return (reward for risk)
  • σ_ptotal risk
  • Sexcess return per 1% of total risk; higher is better
Jensen's alpha
α = R_p − [R_f + β(R_m − R_f)]

In words: Jensen's alpha is the actual return minus the return CAPM says the portfolio should have earned for its beta.

  • R_pactual return
  • R_f + β(R_m − R_f)CAPM return for that beta
  • α > 0manager added value
Solution
Averages (÷ 10): R_p = 16.898%   β = 1.194   R_m = 9.556%   R_f = 5.453%
σ_p = 9.76%   σ_m = 10.40%   (population SD, ÷ n)

Treynor fund   = (16.898 − 5.453) / 1.194 = 9.59
Treynor market = 9.556 − 5.453 = 4.10   (β_m = 1)
Sharpe fund    = 11.445 / 9.76 = 1.173
Sharpe market  = 4.103 / 10.40 = 0.394
Jensen α       = 16.898 − [5.453 + 1.194 × 4.103] = 6.55%

With sample SDs (÷ n − 1): fund Sharpe 1.113, market 0.374. Same conclusion.

The fund ranks above the market on both measures (Treynor 9.59 vs 4.10; Sharpe 1.17 vs 0.39), so rankings agree. Both total and systematic risk were well rewarded, alpha is strongly positive, and the manager's performance is satisfactory.

Twelve figures (three measures × three portfolios plus CAPM), three different rankings, and an explanation of why they disagree.

Rf = 5%, Rm = 13%. Calculate (a) CAPM required return, (b) Jensen's alpha, (c) Return to Variability, (d) Return to Volatility for each; (e) rank under each measure; (f) explain why rankings differ; (g) recommend for a conservative and an aggressive investor.
PortfolioReturnBetaSD
A18%1.1020%
B20%1.4018%
C14%0.7512%
Formula in words
CAPM required return
E(R) = R_f + β(R_m − R_f)

In words: Required return equals the risk-free rate plus beta times the market's extra return over the risk-free rate.

  • R_freward for waiting (risk-free rate)
  • R_m − R_fmarket risk premium: extra return for bearing market risk
  • β ×scaled up or down for this stock's market risk
Jensen's alpha
α = R_p − [R_f + β(R_m − R_f)]

In words: Jensen's alpha is the actual return minus the return CAPM says the portfolio should have earned for its beta.

  • R_pactual return
  • R_f + β(R_m − R_f)CAPM return for that beta
  • α > 0manager added value
Sharpe ratio (Return to Variability)
S = (R_p − R_f) / σ_p

In words: Sharpe ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by total risk (standard deviation).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return (reward for risk)
  • σ_ptotal risk
  • Sexcess return per 1% of total risk; higher is better
Treynor ratio (Return to Volatility)
T = (R_p − R_f) ÷ β_p

In words: Treynor ratio is the extra return over the risk-free rate divided by beta (market risk).

  • R_p − R_fexcess return
  • β_psystematic risk
  • Market benchmarkR_m − R_f, because the market's β = 1
Solution
Required = 5 + β × 8;  Jensen α = R − Required;  Sharpe = (R − 5)/σ;  Treynor = (R − 5)/β
Portfolio(a) Required %(b) Jensen α(c) Sharpe(d) Treynor

C11.003.000.75012.00

(f) Sharpe uses total risk (σ), Treynor only systematic risk (β), and Jensen measures absolute excess return. A has a high σ (20%) for its β (1.10), i.e. it carries unsystematic risk, so it ranks last on Sharpe but well on Treynor and Jensen. (g) Conservative: C (lowest β 0.75, lowest σ, best Treynor). Aggressive: B (highest return 20%, best Sharpe).

Unit 5

Arbitrage Pricing Theory

E(R) = Rf + Σ bλ

In wordsRequired return is the risk-free rate plus, for each factor, the security's sensitivity times that factor's risk premium.

H35U5 · Q29NumericalFrom your PDFOpen in Unit 5 →
Why it's one of the hardest

Three factors to combine, a mispricing to interpret, and an arbitrage strategy to describe in words.

An institutional investor uses APT with three factors and Rf = 7%. Market-implied return 22%. (a) APT required return. (b) Compare and analyse mispricing. (c) Explain an arbitrage strategy.
FactorBetaRisk premium
GDP growth0.74%
Interest rate0.63.5%
Inflation0.42.5%
Formula in words
Arbitrage Pricing Theory
E(R) = R_f + b₁λ₁ + b₂λ₂ + b₃λ₃

In words: Required return is the risk-free rate plus, for every factor, the security's sensitivity to that factor times the factor's risk premium.

  • bⱼsensitivity (beta) to factor j
  • λⱼrisk premium of factor j
  • bⱼλⱼreward for that factor's risk
Solution
(a) E(R) = R_f + Σ bλ = 7 + 0.7(4) + 0.6(3.5) + 0.4(2.5) = 7 + 2.8 + 2.1 + 1.0 = 12.9%
(b) 22% − 12.9% = 9.1% above the APT return → underpriced

(c) Buy the security and short a portfolio with the same factor betas (0.7, 0.6, 0.4) that earns 12.9%. Factor risk cancels, locking in the 9.1% spread with no net investment; buying pressure raises the price until the expected return falls to 12.9%.

H36NewNumericalNew challenge
Why it's one of the hardest

Your PDF has only one APT question, so this harder one is added. It has a negative factor beta and the security turns out overpriced, so the arbitrage runs the opposite way (short the security).

New challenge (not in your PDF). Rf = 6%. A security has these factor sensitivities and risk premiums: GDP growth b = 1.2, λ = 3%; Inflation b = −0.5, λ = 2%; Interest rate b = 0.8, λ = 1.5%. The market prices the security to return 8.5%. (a) Find the APT required return. (b) Is it under- or over-priced? (c) Describe the arbitrage.
Formula in words
Arbitrage Pricing Theory
E(R) = R_f + b₁λ₁ + b₂λ₂ + b₃λ₃

In words: Required return is the risk-free rate plus, for every factor, the security's sensitivity to that factor times the factor's risk premium.

  • bⱼsensitivity (beta) to factor j
  • λⱼrisk premium of factor j
  • bⱼλⱼreward for that factor's risk
Solution
E(R) = 6 + 1.2(3) + (−0.5)(2) + 0.8(1.5)
     = 6 + 3.6 − 1.0 + 1.2 = 9.8%
Market-implied 8.5% − 9.8% = −1.3% → offers LESS than its factor risk requires

(a) 9.8%. (b) Overpriced (expected return below the APT requirement). (c) Short-sell the security and buy a portfolio with the same factor sensitivities (1.2, −0.5, 0.8) earning 9.8%; factor risks cancel and the 1.3% gap is locked in. Selling pressure lowers the price until its return rises to 9.8%.
